The Néron model of a higher-dimensional Lagrangian fibration
Yoon-Joo Kim

Abstract
. Let π : X ⟶ B \pi:X\longrightarrow B be a projective Lagrangian fibration of a smooth symplectic variety X X to a smooth variety B B . Denote the complement of the discriminant locus by B 0 = B ∖ Disc ⁡ ( π ) B_{0}=B\setminus\operatorname{Disc}(\pi) , its preimage by X 0 = π − 1 ​ ( B 0 ) X_{0}=\pi^{-1}(B_{0}) , and the complement of the critical locus by X ′ = X ∖ Sing ⁡ ( π ) X^{\prime}=X\setminus\operatorname{Sing}(\pi) . Under an assumption that the morphism X ′ ⟶ B X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B is surjective, we construct (1) the Néron model of the abelian fibration π 0 : X 0 ⟶ B 0 \pi_{0}:X_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0} and (2) the Néron model of its automorphism abelian scheme Aut π 0 ∘ ⟶ B 0 \operatorname{Aut}^{\circ}_{\pi_{0}}\longrightarrow B_{0} . Contrary to the case of elliptic fibrations, X ′ X^{\prime} may not be the Néron model of X 0 X_{0} ; this is precisely because of the existence of flops in higher-dimensional symplectic varieties. Using such techniques, we analyze when X ′ ⟶ B X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B is a torsor under a smooth group scheme and also revisit some known results in the literature.



1.
Introduction

Let
\pi:S\longrightarrow\Delta
be a proper minimal elliptic fibration form a complex smooth surface
S
to a smooth curve
\Delta
. Assume that
\pi
has no multiple fibers and in particular
S
has a trivial relative canonical bundle by Kodaira’s canonical bundle formula. Consider a smooth, quasi-projective, and surjective morphism

\pi^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\coloneq S\setminus\Sing(\pi)\longrightarrow\Delta.

In the 1960s, Néron
[
N“’64
]
and Raynaud
[
Ray66
]
proved the existence of a unique smooth, commutative, and quasi-projective group scheme
P\longrightarrow\Delta
making
S^{\prime}
a torsor under its action. In particular, if the elliptic fibration
\pi
has a section then
S^{\prime}=P
has a group scheme structure. Nowadays this is called the
Néron model theory
and it is interpreted in two steps: (1) the generic fiber
S_{K}
admits a Néron model that is isomorphic to
\pi^{\prime}:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow\Delta
, and (2) the (neutral) automorphism group
\Aut^{\circ}_{S_{K}}
of the generic fiber admits a Néron model
P\longrightarrow\Delta
. One can think of the torsor property as a formal consequence of these two.

A
smooth symplectic variety
is a smooth variety
X
equipped with a closed non-degenerate
2
-form
\sigma\in H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{2})
, and its
Lagrangian fibration
is a proper surjective morphism
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
with connected fibers whose general fiber is a Lagrangian subvariety. Note that
\pi
is flat if and only if
B
is smooth, and that the dimension of
B
is precisely half the dimension of
X
. The goal of this paper is to generalize the above result of Néron–Raynaud to a higher-dimensional flat Lagrangian fibration
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
from a smooth symplectic variety
X
. Examples include
\GL
-Hitchin fibrations for smooth projective curves or Lagrangian fibrations of projective hyper-Kähler manifolds.

The original definition of the Néron model makes sense only over
1
-dimensional regular schemes. Therefore, we first need its definition over higher-dimensional bases. This is achieved in
[
Hol19
, Definition 1.1]
for abelian schemes and later refined in
[
Hol+23
, Definition 6.1]
in full generality. Let us quickly recall their definition.

1.1
.

Let
B
be a variety,
B_{0}\subset B
a dense Zariski open subset, and
f_{0}:N_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
a smooth morphism. A smooth morphism
f:N\longrightarrow B
from an algebraic space
N
with
f^{-1}(B_{0})\cong N_{0}
is called the
Néron model of
f_{0}
if:

•

For every algebraic space
Z
smooth over
B
, every morphism
Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
over
B_{0}
uniquely extends to a morphism
Z\longrightarrow N
over
B
.

A technical subtlety is the introduction of algebraic spaces. We justify their necessity in
Appendix

A
. A scheme is called
nowhere reduced
if it is non-reduced at every point. We are now ready to state the main theorem of this article.

1.2
Existence of the Néron models.

Let
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
be a projective Lagrangian fibration from a smooth symplectic variety
X
to a smooth variety
B
, both defined over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
B_{0}\subset B
be the Zariski open subset over which
\pi
is smooth and
\pi_{0}:X_{0}=\pi^{-1}(B_{0})\longrightarrow B_{0}
a smooth morphism. Assume that
\pi
does not have any nowhere reduced fiber. Then

(1)

There exists a Néron model
X^{n}\longrightarrow B
of
\pi_{0}:X_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
.

(2)

There exists a Néron model
P\longrightarrow B
of the abelian scheme

P_{0}=\Aut^{\circ}_{\pi_{0}}\longrightarrow B_{0}.

As a result,
X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor.

The two Néron models
X^{n}
and
P
are isomorphic if and only if
\pi
has a rational section. However, we should consider them as different geometric objects even when they are isomorphic. To have a better feeling for the Néron model
P\longrightarrow B
, it is a smooth and commutative group algebraic space that is occasionally quite large: for example, it may be non-separated or non-finite type (
Section

1
), which is a phenomenon that never arose for elliptic fibrations. For the two examples mentioned above, recall that a
\GL
-Higgs bundle on a smooth projective curve
C
is a rank
1
torsion-free sheaf supported on a spectral curve over
C
. A natural group scheme to consider is the relative Jacobian
J\longrightarrow B
of the universal family of spectral curves. The Néron model
P
contains the relative Jacobian
J
but is typically strictly larger. A Lagrangian fibration of a projective hyper-Käher manifold a priori does not come with a group algebraic space, but
Theorem

1.2

constructs
one.

One interpretation of the Néron model
P\longrightarrow B
is a moduli space of (certain) birational automorphisms of
X
over
B
. Hence
P
acts on
X
only birationally but not regularly in general. However, it always contains the largest open subgroup
P^{a}
that acts regularly on
X
, the moduli subspace of (certain) automorphisms of
X
over
B
. The Néron model
P
satisfies the
\delta
-regularity
condition of Ngô (§
A.2.2
) as well: the closed subsets

D_{i}=\{b\in B:\dim(P_{b})_{\operatorname{aff}}\geq i\}\ \subset\ B

have codimension
\geq i
for all
i\geq 0
. Here
(P_{b})_{\operatorname{aff}}
denotes the maximal connected linear algebraic subgroup of
P_{b}
in the Chevalley structure theorem.

1.3
Structure of the Néron model
P
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of
Theorem

1.2
. Then

(1)

P\longrightarrow B
is a smooth, commutative, and
\delta
-regular group algebraic space.

(2)

P
contains a maximal open subgroup space
P^{a}
that acts regularly on
X
over
B
. Such
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
is a smooth, commutative, and quasi-projective group scheme, and the
P^{a}
-action makes
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
a
\delta
-regular abelian fibration.

(3)

For each étale morphism
U\longrightarrow B
, we have

\displaystyle P(U)=\{f:X_{U}\dashrightarrow X_{U}:

\displaystyle\,\mbox{birational automorphism over }U\mbox{ that acts on}

\displaystyle\mbox{the generic fiber as a translation automorphism}\}.

Same holds for the group
P^{a}(U)
but with automorphisms
f:X_{U}\longrightarrow X_{U}
.

(4)

The open immersion
P^{a}\subset P
is an equality over

B_{1}=\{b\in B:\dim(P_{b})_{\operatorname{aff}}\leq 1\}\quad(=B\setminus D_{2}).

The
\delta
-regularity of
P
and the first
\delta
-locus
B_{1}
in the theorem will play a crucial role in our study of Lagrangian fibrations. Note that
B_{1}\subset B
is a Zariski open subset with codimension
\geq 2
complement (
\delta
-regularity), so it captures all
codimension
1
behaviors
of the Lagrangian fibration.

1.4
.

Let
P^{\circ\circ}\subset P
be the smallest open subgroup space of
P
(§
2.2.1
). The group scheme
P^{\circ\circ}
has already been constructed several times in literature. In
[
Mar96
, §2]
and later in
[
AR21
, §1]
(their notations are
A\longrightarrow B_{nm}
and
\Aut^{0}_{\pi}\longrightarrow B
),
P^{\circ\circ}
is analytically constructed as the image of the
exponential map

\exp:\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow\Aut_{\pi}
. In
[
AF16
, §8]
, under a stronger assumption that every fiber of
\pi
is integral,
P^{\circ\circ}
is algebraically constructed, again as a locally closed subgroup scheme of
\Aut_{\pi}
. Our approach is closer to Arinkin–Fedorov’s and is purely algebraic, except for one part about the cohomology vanishing (
Section

4.1.1
) relying on the Hodge module theory in
[
Sch23
]
. Nevertheless, we emphasize that one of our main claims is that the Néron model
P
contains more information than
P^{\circ\circ}
and should be of independent interest.

To study the Néron model
X^{n}
in
Theorem

1.2
, notice first that the nowhere reduced fiber assumption in the theorem is equivalent to the surjectivity of the morphism

(1.5)

\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\coloneq X\setminus\Sing(\pi)\longrightarrow B.

Our original motivation was to check whether this smooth locus
X^{\prime}
is precisely the Néron model
X^{n}
as in the discussion for elliptic fibrations.
1
1

1

Some positive results in literature to this direction are
[
LT16
, Theorem 1.1]
for minimal, flat, and proper families of higher-genus curves over a Dedekind scheme, and
[
Bog+18
, Theorem 1.1]
for abelian surface-fibered Calabi–Yau threefolds over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Both results are over Dedekind schemes.
This may no longer be the case for higher-dimensional Lagrangian fibrations: we show instead that
X^{n}
is the union of smooth loci
Y^{\prime}
for every birational model
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
of
\pi
. In particular,
X^{n}
will fail to be separated along the flopping locus when we glue the non-isomorphic
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
together (if such a situation arises).

1.6
Structure of the Néron model
X^{n}
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of
Theorem

1.3
and (
1.5
). Then

(1)

There exists a
P^{a}
-equivariant open immersion
X^{\prime}\hookrightarrow X^{n}
.

(2)

There exists an equality

X^{n}=\bigcup_{Y_{U}}Y^{\prime}_{U},

where
U\longrightarrow B
runs through every quasi-projective étale morphism,
\tau:Y_{U}\longrightarrow U
a Lagrangian fibered smooth symplectic variety that is birational to
X_{U}\longrightarrow U
, and
Y^{\prime}_{U}=Y_{U}\setminus\Sing(\tau)
the smooth locus of
\tau
.

(3)

The open immersion
X^{\prime}\subset X^{n}
is an equality over
B_{1}
.

As a result,
X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor over
B_{1}
.

The last items of
Theorem

1.3
and
1.6
show that Lagrangian fibrations behave relatively gentle over
B_{1}
, explaining why the nicest results of
\pi
were obtained over codimension
1
points on the base (e.g., Kodaira and Néron’s results on elliptic fibrations or
[
HO09
]
). The behavior of
\pi
over the deeper
\delta
-strata can be wilder but yet its smooth locus
X^{\prime}\subset X
is dominated by the behavior of the abelian fibration
\pi_{0}:X_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
by the Néron mapping property of
X^{n}
.

Let us explain the failure of quasi-compactness and separatedness of the Néron models over
B
in
Theorem

1.2
. Because
X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor,
P
is of finite type (resp. separated) if and only if
X^{n}
is. From
Theorem

1.6
, the failure of quasi-compactness or separatedness of
X^{n}
is intimately related to how different
X^{n}
is from the smooth locus
X^{\prime}
of the Lagrangian fibration. One way of capturing this difference is to define the following subsets of
B
over which
X^{n}
is nice:

•

B_{\tor}
is the subset of
B
over which
X^{\prime}\subset X^{n}
is an equality.

•

B_{\ft}
(resp.
B_{\sep}
) is a maximal Zariski open subset of
B
over which
X^{n}
is of finite type (resp. separated).

Then
B_{\tor}=B
if and only if
X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor. The strict inclusion
B_{\ft}\subsetneq B
(resp.
B_{\sep}\subsetneq B
) will mean that
X^{n}
is not of finite type (resp. not separated) over
B
.

1.6
Failure of separatedness of
X^{n}
and the torsor property of
X^{\prime}
.

Keep the notations and assumptions as above. Then

(1)

B_{\tor}
is a Zariski open subset of
B
and
B_{1}\subset B_{\tor}\subset B_{\ft}\subset B_{\sep}\subset B
.

(2)

Assume
\dim X=4
. Then
B_{\tor}=B_{\ft}=B_{\sep}
.

(3)

The inclusions
B_{1}\subset B_{\tor}
and
B_{\sep}\subset B
may be strict.

In other words,
X^{n}
may fail to be of finite type or separated, and
X^{\prime}
may fail to be a
P
-torsor over
B
. When
\dim X=4
, these happen simultaneously.

We will later interpret the result
[
AF16
, Theorem 2]
as
B_{\elliptic}\subset B_{\tor}
where
B_{\elliptic}\subset B
is the open subset over which
\pi
has geometrically integral fibers. This complements
Section

1
(1). We believe the second item will hold in arbitrary dimension. The third item will be presented as three different examples in §
6.3
, which will be instructive to understand the behaviors of
P
and
X^{n}
. Though we have proved that
X^{\prime}
may fail to be a
P
-torsor, one may still wonder whether it is possible to find another group algebraic space
G\longrightarrow B
making
X^{\prime}
a
G
-torsor. Unfortunately, this weaker statement may not hold as well (
Section

6
).

Finally, there is a conjectural
dual Lagrangian fibration

\check{\pi}:\check{X}\longrightarrow B
predicted by a holomorphic variant of the Strominger–Yau–Zaslow conjecture. We note that this prediction is verified for
G
-Hitchin fibrations for connected reductive groups
G
in
[
DP12
, Theorem C]
and arbitrary Lagrangian fibrations of projective hyper-Kähler manifolds of known deformation types in
[
Kim25
, Theorem 1.1]
. If one believes such a conjecture and that it satisfies similar properties to
Theorem

1.2
–
1
, then the dual abelian scheme
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
is expected to admit a Néron model over
B
. This is indeed the case, providing evidence for the existence of a dual Lagrangian fibration
\check{\pi}
for arbitrary
\pi
.

1.7
Dual Néron model.

Keep the notations and assumptions as above. Let

\check{P}_{0}=\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{0}}\longrightarrow B_{0}

be the dual abelian scheme of
P_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
. Then

(1)

There exists a Néron model
\check{P}\longrightarrow B
of
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
.

(2)

\check{P}
is
\delta
-regular and shares the same
\delta
-loci with
P
.

The dual Lagrangian fibration
\check{\pi}:\check{X}\longrightarrow B
, if it exists, should be a compactification of a
\check{P}_{1}
-torsor over
B_{1}
. A recent result of Saccà
[
Sac24
, Theorem 1]
studies this problem in depth (see
[
LLX24
, Theorem 1.1]
for an alternative version). The total space
\check{X}
of such a compactification turns out to be a
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminal symplelctic variety. This agrees with the result of
[
Kim25
, Theorem 1.1]
where the expected dual fibration
\check{\pi}
is explicitly constructed when
X
is a generalized Kummer variety or an OG6-type hyper-Kähler manifold. We plan to revisit this problem in future work.

1.1.
Relation to results in literature and applications

This paper grew from an attempt to understand the holomorphic Strominger–Yau–Zaslow conjecture and to understand to what extent a Lagrangian fibration can be considered as a compactification of a smooth group scheme torsor. We arrive to a conclusion that the Néron model theory is extremely helpful to study these questions. Such a viewpoint can also be beneficial to connect several other known results in literature, which we summarize here. See §
6.4
for detailed discussions.

•

(§
6.4.1
) When
\pi
has integral fibers,
[
AF16
, Theorem 2]
constructed a smooth group scheme
G\longrightarrow B
making
X^{\prime}
a
G
-torsor. We recover their result by showing
G=P^{\circ\circ}=P^{a}=P
. An alternative interpretation of their result is that
B_{\tor}
contains every point
b\in B
such that
X_{b}
is geometrically integral.

•

(§
6.4.2
) Every rational section of
\pi
is necessarily defined over
B_{\tor}
but in general not extendable over the entire
B
. If
X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor, then
B_{\tor}=B
so every rational section is regular.

•

(§
6.4.3
)
[
HO09
]
studied the structure of general singular fibers of higher-dimensional Lagrangian fibrations, generalizing the work of Kodaira for elliptic fibrations. Many results in their §2–3 can be recovered algebraically using the
P^{a}
-action in
Theorem

1.3
.

•

(§
6.4.4
) When
X
is compact,
[
AR21
]
studied the Tate–Shafarevich group of a Lagrangian fibration, which is
H^{1}_{an}(B,P^{\circ\circ})
in our notation. The Néron mapping property of
P
helps studying this group further.

•

(§
6.4.5
)
[
CRS21
]
compared singular fibers of two explicit Lagrangian fibrations of projective hyper-Kähler manifolds
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
and
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
where
X
is of OG10-type and
Y
is of
\text{K3}^{[5]}
-type. We show both
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
are
P
-torsors (ignoring the nowhere reduced fibers), explaining some of the behaviors they discovered.

1.2.
Sketch proofs and organization of the paper

Though we have taken a bird’s-eye viewpoint in the introduction to emphasize the role of the Néron models
P
and
X^{n}
, our proof will go in the opposite direction. We will first build various coarser invariants and patch them together to construct the Néron models as a final outcome.

The first two sections §
2
–
3
are devoted to more abstract discussions about the theory of group algebraic spaces and Néron models. We decided to write these sections separately and with greater generality due to the following two reasons. First, the proof of the main results (
Theorem

2.25
and
2.5
in §
2
, and
Corollary

3.17
,
3.22
, and
3.27
in §
3
) are rather lengthy and require abstract generalities. Second, we bear in mind of future generalizations of the results in this article into more general set-ups, such as for
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial terminal symplectic algebraic spaces
X
or proper Lagrangian fibrations
\pi
.

We start the discussions about Lagrangian fibrations from §
4
. The main goal of this section is to construct the group scheme
P^{a}
. We realize
P^{a}
as a locally closed subgroup scheme of
\Aut_{\pi}
as in
[
AF16
]
and
[
AR21
]
, but use different techniques to achieve this because our group scheme may be larger than theirs. More precisely, we use
Theorem

2.25
and
2.5
to construct
P^{a}
as the equidimensional locus of the main component of
\Aut_{\pi}\longrightarrow B
(§
2.5
). Infinitesimal version of this claim should be proved beforehand: we first need to show that
\Lie(\Aut_{\pi})
contains the cotangent bundle of
B
as a Lie subalgebra scheme. This is a formal consequence of the vanishing of a certain higher direct image coherent sheaf in
Section

4.1.1
, which is proved from the Hodge module symmetry of the decomposition theorem proposed in
[
SY22
]
and proved in
[
Sch23
]
. We next prove the
\delta
-regularity of the group scheme
P^{a}
. The idea is to observe that
X
equipped with the
P^{a}
-action is a weak abelian fibration. This strategy is already contained in Ngô’s original articles
[
Ng“ˆo10
,
Ng“ˆo11
]
and also revisited several times in literature, so we make the discussion short with some proper citations.

In §
5
, we study the behavior of
\pi
over the first nontrivial
\delta
-locus
B_{1}
. The main theorem shows
P^{a}_{1}
and
X^{\prime}_{1}
are Néron under
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
. We start with an observation in
Lemma

5.7
(using
Theorem

3.22
and
3.27
) that the Néron mapping property of
X^{\prime}
is essentially equivalent to understanding the birational behavior of the Lagrangian fibration
\pi
. This turns our interest to the study of birational maps
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
between Lagrangian fibered symplectic varieties.
Theorem

5.4
is a technical input to this direction, which says that such a birational map
f
is necessarily an isomorphism over
B_{1}
. To prove this, we use Kawamata’s theorem
[
Kaw08
]
to decompose
f
into a finite sequence of flops and analyze each flop separately using the
\delta
-regularity of the action. This sometimes explains the behavior of
f
over the entire base
B
and we will need this in the following section. Along the way, we partially recover the results of
[
HO09
, §2–3]
using the
P^{a}
-action on
X
instead of the original foliation theory.

In §
6
, we construct the Néron models
P
and
X^{n}
over the entire base
B
. The Néron model
P
is obtained by gluing several copies of
P^{a}
via translations. The gluing datum is extracted from the neutral Picard algebraic space
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
, leading us to study the dual side and construct
P
and its dual
\check{P}
at the same time. More specifically, we observe the two groups
P^{a}
and
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
have complementary Néron behaviors: the former is Néron under
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
and the latter is Néron under
B_{1}\subset B
. A polarization
P^{a}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
connects their behaviors and yields the Néron models of both sides (the technical input is
Corollary

3.17
). Once this is done,
X^{n}
can be constructed as a
P
-torsor with a twisting datum inherited from that of
X^{\prime}
. The remaining claims are proved after this. Finally, we construct three examples illustrating (the failure of) the torsor property of
X^{\prime}
in §
6.3
, and present applications of our constructions to results in literature in §
6.4
.

2.
Group algebraic spaces

The first two sections of this article will be devoted to the general theory of group algebraic spaces (§
2
) and Néron models (§
3
). The results in these two sections are rather collective, so the reader is invited to jump directly to
Section

4
and come back to them whenever needed. However, we emphasize that some of the technical hearts of the article are contained in these first two sections. The reference
[
Stacks
]
will be cited often, so we simply write their Tag numbers to cite them. For example, (Tag ABCD) means
[
Stacks
, Tag ABCD]
.

Let
S
be a locally separated and locally Noetherian algebraic space over a field of characteristic
0
.

2.1
.

A
group algebraic space
, or simply a
group space
, over
S
is an algebraic space
G\longrightarrow S
equipped with an identity
e:S\longrightarrow G
, multiplication
m:G\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G
, and inverse
i:G\longrightarrow G
satisfying the axioms of groups.

Every group space in this article will be assumed to be locally separated and locally of finite type over a locally separated and locally Noetherian base algebraic space
S
of characteristic
0
.

Schemes are always locally separated. Every locally separated and locally of finite type algebraic space is quasi-separated (Tag 088J and 0BB6), so every group space
G\longrightarrow S
in this article will be quasi-separated as well. A
(locally) algebraic group
over a field
k
is a group space (locally) of finite type over
S=\Spec k
. A locally algebraic group is necessarily a scheme
[
Art69
, Lemma 4.2]
, separated (Tag 047L), and smooth (Tag 047N: Cartier’s theorem); everything is as nice as possible over a field. We highlight Cartier’s theorem as it is the main reason why group spaces behave nicer in characteristic
0
.

2.2
Cartier’s theorem.

Every locally algebraic group over a field of characteristic
0
is smooth.

One should keep in mind that a group space over a general base
S
may easily fail to be smooth or separated, even when
S
is regular of characteristic
0
. See §
2.6.3
for many examples. A group space is separated if and only if its identity morphism
e:S\longrightarrow G
is a closed immerion (Tag 06P6). Over a reduced base
S
, basic smoothness criterion is provided by the following proposition.

2.2
.

Let
f:G\longrightarrow S
be a group space over a reduced space
S
. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

f
is smooth.

(2)

f
is flat.

(3)

f
is universally equidimensional (in the sense of
[
SV00
, §2.1]
, see §
A.1.5
).

Proof.

The implications (1)
\Longrightarrow
(2)
\Longrightarrow
(3) are clear. (2)
\Longrightarrow
(1) follows from Cartier’s theorem that every fiber is smooth. For (3)
\Longrightarrow
(2), notice that
f
is universally open by
Section

A.1.5
, the fibers of
f
are (geometrically) reduced by Cartier’s theorem, and
S
is reduced by assumption. These are enough to prove the flatness of
f
by
Theorem

A.13
. We note that
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
]
Corollary 4.4, Proposition 1.6, and Theorem 3.10 proves this statement under an assumption that every fiber of
f
is connected.
∎

We also recall the following result, stating that a group space in certain cases is a scheme.

2.3
[
FC90
, Theorem I.1.9]
,
[
Ray70a
, Theorem 3.3.1]
.

(1)

Every smooth proper group space
A\longrightarrow S
with connected fibers over a scheme
S
is a scheme, called an
abelian scheme
.

(2)

Every separated group space
G\longrightarrow\Delta
over a Dedekind scheme
\Delta
is a scheme.

2.1.
Lie algebra spaces

Since we are assuming
G\longrightarrow S
is locally separated, the identity section
e:S\longrightarrow G
is an immersion. We denote its conormal sheaf by
C_{e}
, or
C_{G}
for simplicity; it is a coherent sheaf on
S
. The conormal sheaf of a group space plays an equally important role to the relative cotangent sheaf by the following:

2.3
Tag 047I.

Let
f:G\longrightarrow S
be a group space with an identity section
e:S\longrightarrow G
. Then the cotangent sheaf of
f
and the conormal sheaf of
e
are related via canonical isomorphisms

\Omega_{f}=f^{*}C_{e},\qquad C_{e}=e^{*}\Omega_{f}.

Every group space
G\longrightarrow S
has an associated Lie algebra sheaf
\underline{\Lie}\,G
, which by definition is an fppf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module (see
[
SGA $3˙I$
, II Definition 3.9.0]
or equivalently the paragraph above
[
AF16
, Proposition 8.2]
)

\underline{\Lie}\,G=\Hom^{\fppf}(C_{e},\mathcal{O}_{S})\ :\ \big[h:T\longrightarrow S\big]\longmapsto\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}(h^{*}C_{e},\mathcal{O}_{T}),

equipped with a Lie algebra structure. It is the fppf dual of the conormal sheaf
C_{e}
, so by §
A.1.6
it is represented by a linear space

\Lie G=\Spec_{S}(\Sym^{*}C_{e})\longrightarrow S,

which we call the
associated Lie algebra space
of
G
. The Lie bracket operation
[\,,\,]:\Lie G\times_{S}\Lie G\longrightarrow\Lie G
translates to a Lie cobracket operation

d:C_{e}\longrightarrow\bigwedge^{2}C_{e},

a homomorphism of coherent sheaves with
d^{2}=0
(cf.,
[
Mic80
]
). Both the conormal sheaf and Lie algebra space perspectives are beneficial.

Recall a series of implications: a scheme is normal
\Longrightarrow
it is reduced and geometrically unibranch (Tag 0BQ3)
\Longrightarrow
its connected components are integral. Given a group space
G\longrightarrow S
, taking a base change by the normalization
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
produces a new group space
G^{\prime}=G\times_{S}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
whose base is normal.

2.3
.

Let
f:G\longrightarrow S
be a group space over a reduced space
S
. Then the following are equivalent.

(4)

f
is smooth along the identity section
e
.

(5)

f
has constant fiber dimension.

(6)

The conormal sheaf
C_{e}
of the identity section is locally free.

(7)

\Lie f:\Lie G\longrightarrow S
is smooth.

Assume furthermore that
S
is geometrically unibranch and every irreducible component of
G
dominates an irreducible component of
S
. Then these conditions are further equivalent to the conditions in
Section

2
.

Proof.

The implications (1)
\Longrightarrow
(4)
\Longleftrightarrow
(5)
\Longleftrightarrow
(6)
\Longleftrightarrow
(7) are combinations of
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
Proposition 1.6]
and
[
AF16
, Proposition 8.2]
. Let us now assume
S
and
G
satisfy the given assumptions, and assume (5). Then
f
is equidimensional and furthermore, universally equidimensional by
Section

A.1.5
. This proves (3).
∎

2.1.1.
Deformation to the Lie algebra

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space. The identity section
e:S\longrightarrow G
is a closed immersion, so we may consider its deformation to the normal cone construction, which we recall for reader’s convenience (see
[
Ful98
, §5]
for details). Start with the trivial family
G\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
, blow it up along its closed subscheme
e(S)\times\{\infty\}
to obtain
\operatorname{Bl}_{e(S)\times\infty}(G\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and take a complement in the central fiber

\mathfrak{G}=\Big(\operatorname{Bl}_{e(S)\times\infty}(G\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\Big)\setminus\operatorname{Bl}_{e(S)}G\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}.

It is a flat algebraic space over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and admits a morphism

(2.4)

\mathfrak{G}\longrightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\quad\mbox{with a section }\ e:S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{G},

which can be considered as a deformation of the group space
G\longrightarrow S
with its section. It satisfies the following properties:

•

Over
S\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
for
\mathbb{A}^{1}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{\infty\}
, (
2.4
) is isomorphic to the trivial
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-family of
G\longrightarrow S
together with the identity section
e:S\longrightarrow G
; and

•

Over
S\times\{\infty\}
, it is isomorphic to the normal cone

N=\Spec_{S}\Big(\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}I^{k}/I^{k+1}\Big)\longrightarrow S,

of
e(S)\subset G
, where
I
is the ideal sheaf of the closed subscheme
e(S)\subset G
.

2.5
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space and (
2.4
) the deformation to the normal cone of its identity section. Then there exists a closed immersion
\mathfrak{G}_{\infty}\subset\Lie G
that is set-theoretically equal.

Proof.

Let
I
be the ideal sheaf of
e(S)\subset G
. The conormal sheaf
C_{e}
is by definition
I/I^{2}
, so there exists a surjective homomorphism of quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-algebras

\bigoplus_{k}\Sym^{k}C_{e}\twoheadrightarrow\bigoplus_{k}I^{k}/I^{k+1}

which in turn induces a closed immersion
\mathfrak{G}_{\infty}\subset\Lie G
over
S
. Over a point
s\in S
, the fiber of the group space is a locally algebraic group
G_{s}
over
k(s)
, which is always smooth by Cartier’s theorem. Hence the identity section
\Spec k(s)\longrightarrow G_{s}
is a regular point, so its ideal sheaf
I_{s}=I\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}k(s)
satisfies
\Sym^{k}(I_{s}/I_{s}^{2})\cong I_{s}^{k}/I_{s}^{k+1}
. This proves the closed immersion
\mathfrak{G}_{\infty}\subset\Lie G
is an equality over
s\in S
. Hence
\mathfrak{G}_{\infty}\subset\Lie G
is a set-theoretic equality, i.e.,
\mathfrak{G}_{\infty}
is defined by a nilpotent ideal.
∎

Because of this lemma, we will (somewhat incorrectly) call the deformation to the normal cone construction above a
deformation to the Lie algebra
.

2.6
.

For a separated group space
G\longrightarrow S
, we call the morphism (
2.4
) its
deformation to its Lie algebra
.

2.7
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space.

(1)

The deformation to the Lie algebra morphism respects arbitrary base change
T\longrightarrow S
.

(2)

If
H\subset G
is a locally closed subgroup space, then there exists an immersion between deformation to the Lie algebras

\hbox to79.83pt{\vbox to39.65pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 39.91658pt\lower-19.82272pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Proof.

Immediate from construction.
∎

2.8
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space and
\mathfrak{G}\longrightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
its deformation to the Lie algebra in (
2.4
).

(1)

If
\Lie G\longrightarrow S
is smooth, then
\mathfrak{G}_{\infty}=\Lie G
scheme-theoretically.

(2)

If
G\longrightarrow S
is smooth, then
\mathfrak{G}\longrightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is smooth.

Proof.

(1) If
\Lie G\longrightarrow S
is smooth then the identity section
e:S\longrightarrow G
is a regular immersion by
Section

2.1
. Hence we have an isomorphism
\Sym^{k}(I/I^{2})=I^{k}/I^{k+1}
for the ideal sheaf
I
of
e
, proving that the Lie algebra space is the normal cone. (2) The algebraic space
\mathfrak{G}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is flat by construction. By (Tag 05X1: flatness criterion by fibers),
g:\mathfrak{G}\longrightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is smooth if and only if
g_{t}:\mathfrak{G}_{t}\longrightarrow S_{t}
is smooth for all
t\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. If
G\longrightarrow S
is smooth, then
\mathfrak{G}_{\infty}=\Lie G\longrightarrow S
is smooth as well by the first item.
∎

2.2.
Neutral components

Given a locally algebraic space
G
over
k
, the unique connected component
G^{\circ}
containing the identity defines an open and closed subgroup scheme of
G
, called the neutral component. A group space
G\longrightarrow S
over a general base may have two different notion of “neutral components”, which we describe in this section.

2.2.1.
Group spaces with connected fibers and strict neutral component

In general, the smoothness of
\Lie G\longrightarrow S
does not imply the smoothness of
G\longrightarrow S
(see
Section

2.1
and §
2.6.3
). However, this implies the fiberwise neutral component of
G
, the
strict neutral component
, is smooth.

2.9
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
Theorem 3.10]
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space with smooth Lie algebra space
\Lie G\longrightarrow S
. Then there exists a unique smooth, open, and normal subgroup space
G^{\circ\circ}\subset G
such that for every
s\in S
, the fiber
G^{\circ\circ}_{s}
is the neutral component of the locally algebraic group
G_{s}
.

2.10
Strict neutral component.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space with smooth Lie algebra. We call the smooth open subgroup space
G^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow S
in
Theorem

2.9
the
strict neutral component
of
G
.

Recall that an arbitrary group space may very well be non-separated. However, for smooth group spaces, only the disconnectedness of fibers is responsible for its non-separatedness.

2.11
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
Corollary 5.5]
.

Every smooth group space
G\longrightarrow S
with connected fibers is separated and of finite type.

2.12
.

Let
f:G\longrightarrow H
be a homomorphism between smooth group spaces over
S
. Then the restriction of
f
induces
G^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow H^{\circ\circ}
.

Proof.

For each point
s\in S
,
f_{s}:G^{\circ\circ}_{s}\longrightarrow H_{s}
factors through
H^{\circ\circ}_{s}
. Since
H^{\circ\circ}\subset H
is an open subspace, this will suffice to prove the claim.
∎

The following theorem will be very useful later on.

2.13
[
Ray70
, Corollary IX.1.4]
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
and
H\longrightarrow S
be group spaces. Assume that
S
is
\mathrm{(S_{2})}
,
S_{1}\subset S
is a dense open subspace with codimension
\geq 2
complement, and
G\longrightarrow S
is smooth with connected fibers. Then every homomorphism
f_{1}:G_{1}\longrightarrow H_{1}
over
S_{1}
uniquely extends to a homomorphism
f:G\longrightarrow H
over
S
.

2.2.2.
Connected group spaces and (weak) neutral component

We assume
S
is connected for simplicity. When
S
is disconnected, repeat the definition for all connected components of
S
.

2.14
Neutral component.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space over a connected space
S
. We call the unique connected component
G^{\circ}\subset G
containing the identity section the
(weak) neutral component
of
G
.

When
G
is a locally algebraic group over
S=\Spec k
, the notion of neutral and strict neutral components make no difference. In general the two are different, and we may have a strict open immersion
G^{\circ\circ}\subsetneq G^{\circ}
. See §
2.6.3
.

2.14
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space. Assume either

(i)

G\longrightarrow S
is smooth; or

(ii)

S
is reduced and geometrically unibranch, and
\Lie G\longrightarrow S
is smooth.

Then the neutral component
G^{\circ}\longrightarrow S
is a smooth, open and closed, and normal subgroup space of
G
.

Proof.

(i) Assume
S
is connected. The inverse morphism
i:G\longrightarrow G
certainly restricts to a morphism
i:G^{\circ}\longrightarrow G^{\circ}
between connected components. To show
m:G\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G
restricts to
G^{\circ}\times_{S}G^{\circ}\longrightarrow G^{\circ}
, it is enough to show
G^{\circ}\times_{S}G^{\circ}
is a connected algebraic space. The strict neutral component
G^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow S
has geometrically connected fibers (every connected algebraic group is geometrically connected), so
G^{\circ\circ}\times_{S}G^{\circ}
is connected (Tag 0385). Since
G^{\circ}
is smooth, it is the union of translations of
G^{\circ\circ}
by étale local sections (
Section

A.1.1
), so
G^{\circ}\times_{S}G^{\circ}
is connected as well. This shows
G^{\circ}
is an open subgroup space of
G
.

To prove it is a normal subgroup space, we prove it is a characteristic subgroup. Given an étale morphism
U\longrightarrow S
, let
f:G_{U}\longrightarrow G_{U}
be an automorphism of a group space
G_{U}\longrightarrow U
. Then
f
sends
G^{\circ}_{U}
to itself. That is,
G^{\circ}\subset G
is a characteristic subgroup and hence a normal subgroup.

(ii) The same argument above will work once we prove
G^{\circ}\longrightarrow S
is smooth. Let us first reduce the problem to the case when
G\longrightarrow S
is locally quasi-finite. By
Theorem

2.9
,
G^{\circ\circ}
is a smooth subgroup space of
G
. The above argument still constructs a morphism
G^{\circ\circ}\times_{S}G^{\circ}\longrightarrow G^{\circ}
, meaning
G^{\circ}
is closed under the
G^{\circ\circ}
-action by translation. Take the smooth quotients
[
BLR90
, Proposition 8.3.9]

K^{\circ}=G^{\circ}/G^{\circ\circ}\ \ \subset\ \ K=G/G^{\circ\circ}.

Then
G^{\circ}
will be smooth once we prove
K^{\circ}
is smooth, reducing the problem to the case when
G\longrightarrow S
has a constant fiber dimension
0
.

Assume
G\longrightarrow S
is locally quasi-finite. To prove
G^{\circ}\longrightarrow S
is étale, it is enough to prove it is étale at all points on
one
irreducible component
Y
of
G^{\circ}
. This is because such
Y
becomes geometrically unibranch, so it cannot have any intersection with other irreducible components of
G^{\circ}
(while
G^{\circ}
is connected). Consider the irreducible component
Y=\overline{e(S)}\subset G^{\circ}
, the closure of the identity section. Let
x\in Y\subset G^{\circ}
be a point and
X=\Spec A\longrightarrow G^{\circ}
a connected affine étale chart around
x
. Note that
X
dominates
S
. We prove in the following
Lemma

2.15
that
X
is étale over
S
in this situation. This completes the proof.
∎

2.15
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a quasi-finite, separated, and dominant morphism over a reduced and geometrically unibranch space
S
. Assume that
X
is connected and
f
has smooth fibers. Then
f
is étale.

Proof.

Shrinking, we may assume
S
is Noetherian. Since
f
is dominant, there exists an integral component
Y\subset X
that dominates
S
.

We claim that
f
is étale along points in
Y
, or equivalently
f
is flat along points in
Y
. Let
x\in Y\subset X
be a point and
s=f(x)\in S
its image. To show
f
is flat at
x
, it is enough to show that for each trait
\Delta\longrightarrow S
passing through
s
, the base change
X_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is flat over every point
x^{\prime}\in X_{\Delta}
over
x
(
Section

A.1.4
). Let us first claim that
Y_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is dominant (“going-down”). By Zariski’s main theorem (Tag 082K),
Y\longrightarrow S
factorizes through an open immersion followed by a finite morphism
Y\subset\bar{Y}\longrightarrow S
. Since
Y\longrightarrow S
was dominant,
\bar{Y}\longrightarrow S
is finite surjective. Therefore, we can use the valuative criterion for properness to lift the trait
\Delta\longrightarrow S
to a trait
\Delta\longrightarrow\bar{Y}
through
x
. Such a trait factorizes through
Y
, so
Y_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is dominant. Now that we know
Y_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is dominant and
f_{\Delta}:X_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is quasi-finite,
f_{\Delta}
is universally equidimensional (and hence universally open) along
Y_{\Delta}
. The closed fiber of
f_{\Delta}
is smooth by assumption, so this proves
f_{\Delta}
is flat along
Y_{\Delta}
by
Theorem

A.13
.

Now
f
is étale along
Y
, so
X
is geometrically unibranch along
Y
. This means
Y
cannot intersect any other irreducible components of
X
. Since
X
is connected, this means
Y=X
and
f
is étale.
∎

2.16
Generic smoothness of the neutral component.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space over a reduced space
S
. Then there exists a dense open subspace
S_{0}\subset S
such that
G^{\circ}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a smooth group space.

Proof.

We claim that there exists a dense open subspace
S_{0}\subset S
such that
S_{0}
is geometrically unibranch and
G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
has constant fiber dimension. Such
S_{0}
will satisfy the desired property by
Section

2.2.2
and
2.1
.

The claim is étale local on
S
, so we may assume
S=\Spec A
is a reduced, Noetherian, and affine scheme. The normalization
S^{\nu}\longrightarrow S
is then birational, so we may further assume
S
is normal (which is geometrically unibranch). Now by the semicontinuity of fiber dimension of
G\longrightarrow S
on the base
S

[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
Proposition 4.1]
, there exists a dense open subscheme
S_{0}\subset S
over which the fiber dimension of
G\longrightarrow S
is locally constant.
∎

2.17
.

The full group space
G\longrightarrow S
may not be generically smooth in the above sense (when it is not finite type). See §
2.6.3
.

2.3.
Alignment

A smooth group space
G\longrightarrow S
may very well be non-separated, but its strict neutral component
G^{\circ\circ}
is always separated by
Theorem

2.11
. This means the non-neutral fiber components of
G
are responsible for the non-separatedness of
G
. In this section, we will see there are in fact two levels of non-separatedness depending on an
alignment
of the fiber components.

Recall that a group space is separated if and only if its identity section is a closed immersion. Denoting by
E
the scheme-theoretic image of the identity section
e:S\longrightarrow G
, the group
G
is separated if and only if
E\longrightarrow S
is an isomorphism.

2.18
Aligned group.

A smooth group space
G\longrightarrow S
is called
aligned
if the scheme-theoretic image
E
of its identity section
e:S\longrightarrow G
is étale over
S
.

The idea and terminology of this definition is taken from
[
Hol19
, Definition 2.11]
, where the notion of an alignment is defined for a proper flat family of semistable curves
f:\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow S
. Our definition models their Theorem 5.17, where they showed that
f:\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow S
is aligned iff its neutral Picard space
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow S
is aligned in the sense of
Definition

2.18
. The following models Theorem 6.2 in loc. cit.

2.18
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a smooth group space over a reduced space
S
. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

G\longrightarrow S
is aligned.

(2)

There exists a surjective homomorphism
h:G\longrightarrow\bar{G}
to a smooth and separated group space
\bar{G}\longrightarrow S
, whose restriction to the strict neutral components
h_{|G^{\circ\circ}}:G^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow\bar{G}^{\circ\circ}
is an isomorphism.

Such a homomorphism
h
is called a
separated quotient
of
G
.

Proof.

(1)
\Longrightarrow
(2) Assume
E\longrightarrow S
is étale. Let us first claim that
E
is a closed étale normal subgroup space of
G
. The inverse morphism
i:G\longrightarrow G
clearly restricts to
i:E\longrightarrow E
. To show
m:G\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G
restricts to
E\times_{S}E\longrightarrow E
, it is enough to show that for two étale local sections
a,b:S\longrightarrow E
(after shrinking
S
), its product
a\cdot b:S\longrightarrow G
factors through
E
. This is because
a
and
b
are the identity over a dense open subspace
S_{0}\subset S
, so
a\cdot b
is the identity over
S_{0}
as well. This shows
E
is a closed subgroup space of
G
. It is characteristic because every (étale local) automorphism
f:G\longrightarrow G
of the group space
G
sends
E\subset G
to
E\subset G
. In particular, it is a normal subgroup.

We may now consider a smooth quotient group space
\bar{G}=G/E

[
BLR90
, Proposition 8.3.9]
. Since
G^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow S
is separated by
Theorem

2.11
,
E\cap G^{\circ\circ}
is trivial hence
G\longrightarrow\bar{G}
induces an open immersion of
G^{\circ\circ}
to
\bar{G}
, whose image is necessarily the strict neutral component of
\bar{G}
.

(2)
\Longrightarrow
(1) Conversely, assume we have a separated quotient
h:G\longrightarrow\bar{G}
. Notice that
h_{s}:G_{s}\longrightarrow\bar{G}_{s}
is flat for all points
s\in S
because
h_{s}(G_{s}^{\circ\circ})=\bar{G}_{s}^{\circ\circ}

[
BLR90
, Lemma 7.3.1]
. The fiberwise flatness criterion (Tag 05X1) concludes
h
is flat. By definition,
h
induces an isomorphism between
G^{\circ\circ}
and
\bar{G}^{\circ\circ}
, meaning its kernel is locally quasi-finite. This proves
h
is étale, or equivalently
\ker h\longrightarrow S
is étale. Now
h
sends
E
to the identity section of
\bar{G}
, so we have
E\subset\ker h
. Since
\ker h
is étale and contains the identity section, this is an equality. Hence
E=\ker h
is étale over
S
.
∎

2.19
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a smooth aligned group space over a reduced space with a separated quotient
G\longrightarrow\bar{G}
as above. Then every homomorphism
G\longrightarrow H
to a separated group space
H\longrightarrow S
uniquely factors through
\bar{G}
.

Proof.

Let
f:G\longrightarrow H
be such a homomorphism. Since the identity section of
H
is a closed immersion,
\ker f
contains the closure
E
of the identity section of
G
. The claim follows from
\bar{G}=G/E
.
∎

2.19
.

Every smooth group space
G\longrightarrow\Delta
over a Dedekind scheme is aligned.

Proof.

We may assume
\Delta=\Spec R
where
R
is a dvr with function field
K
. Let
E
be the closure of the identity section. Then
E\longrightarrow\Delta
is flat since
E
is integral and dominating
\Delta
(
Section

A.1.4
). To show it is étale, note that the locally algebraic group
G_{K}
is separated so
E\longrightarrow\Delta
is an isomorphism over
K
. Hence the result follows from
[
Hol19
, Lemma 5.18]
.
∎

2.19
.

Let
f:G\longrightarrow S
be smooth group space of finite type over a normal Noetherian space
S
. Then

(1)

There exists a dense open subspace
S_{0}\subset S
such that
G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is separated.

(2)

There exists a dense open subspace
S_{1}\subset S
with codimension
\geq 2
complement such that
G_{1}\longrightarrow S_{1}
is aligned.

Proof.

The first item is
[
EGA $IV˙3$
, Theorem 8.10.5]
, so let us focus on the second item. We may assume
S
is a normal connected scheme. Let
E\subset G
be the closure of the identity section, which is trivial over
S_{0}
by the first item. Consider the étale locus
E^{\prime}\subset E
of the morphism
E\longrightarrow S
. The image of its complement
f(E\setminus E^{\prime})\subset S
is a constructible set by Chevalley’s theorem. Let
\eta\in S
be a codimension
1
point and
\Delta=\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,\eta}\longrightarrow G
a flat trait. Then
E_{\Delta}\subset G_{\Delta}
is the closure of the identity section
[
BLR90
, Proposition 2.5.2]
, so it is étale over
\Delta
by
Section

2.3
. This means the constructible set
f(E\setminus E^{\prime})
contains no codimension
1
points. Hence there exists a codimension
\geq 2
complement open subset
S_{1}\subset S
such that
E
is étale over
S_{1}
.
∎

2.19
.

Let
G
and
H
be smooth group spaces over
S
, and
f:G\longrightarrow H
an étale homomorphism between them. If
H
is aligned, then
G
is aligned.

Proof.

Let
E_{G}\subset G
and
E_{H}\subset H
be the scheme-theoretic images of the identity sections. Since
f
is étale and
E_{H}\subset H
is closed, the preimage
f^{-1}(E_{H})\subset G
is closed and étale over
S
. Its closed subscheme
E_{G}
is étale over
S
as well.
∎

2.4.
Étale group algebraic spaces

Étale group spaces are smooth group spaces of relative dimension
0
, not necessarily of finite type or separated. A finite type étale group space is often called an (étale) constructible sheaf of finite groups. Here we collect their basic properties.

2.19
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a locally quasi-finite group space over a reduced and geometrically unibranch scheme
S
. Then its neutral component
G^{\circ}\longrightarrow S
is an étale subgroup space.

Proof.

This is
Section

2.2.2
with constant fiber dimension
0
.
∎

2.20
.

Let
f:G\longrightarrow S
be a separated étale group space. If there exists a dense open subscheme
S_{0}\subset S
over which
f
is trivial, then
f
is trivial.

Proof.

Let
s\in S
be a point and
x\in G_{s}
a closed point. Shrinking
S
étale locally, we can choose a section
s:S\longrightarrow G
passing through
x
. Since
G\longrightarrow S
is trivial over
S_{0}
, the section
s
agrees with the identity section
e
over
S_{0}
. By
Lemma

A.7
,
s=e
and thus
x
lies on the identity section.
∎

Every locally algebraic group
G
over a field induces an étale algebraic group
\pi_{0}(G)=G/G^{\circ}
, the group of its connected components. This phenomenon generalizes to group spaces with smooth Lie algebra. Recall from
Theorem

2.9
that their strict neutral component is a smooth normal subgroup.

2.21
\pi_{0}
-group.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space with a smooth Lie algebra. The
component group
or
\pi_{0}
-group
of
G
is a locally quasi-finite group space
\pi_{0}(G)\longrightarrow S
obtained by the quotient

(2.22)

If
G
is a smooth group space, then
\pi_{0}(G)
is an étale group sapce.

2.22
.

A group space
G\longrightarrow S
with smooth Lie algebra is of finite type if and only if
\pi_{0}(G)
is quasi-finite.

Proof.

The strict neutral component
G^{\circ\circ}
is of finite type by
Theorem

2.11
, so the morphism
\pi_{0}
is always of finite type.
∎

2.22
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space with smooth Lie algebra. Then there exists an inclusion-preserving bijective correspondence

\quad\{\mbox{open (normal) subgroup spaces of }G\}\\
\longleftrightarrow\{\mbox{open (normal) subgroup spaces of }\pi_{0}(G)\}\quad

Proof.

The correspondence is defined by
H\longmapsto\pi_{0}(H)
with inverse
K\longmapsto\pi_{0}^{-1}(K)
. To show they are inverse to each other, it is enough to show that every open subgroup space
H\subset G
contains the strict neutral component
G^{\circ\circ}
as a (normal) subgroup. Since both
H
and
G^{\circ\circ}
are open, this can be checked pointwise on the base: for each point
t\in S
, we have an open algebraic subgroup
H_{t}\subset G_{t}
, so we have
G^{\circ\circ}_{t}\subset H_{t}
.
∎

If
G\longrightarrow S
is a smooth group space of finite type, then
\pi_{0}(G)
is an étale constructible sheaf of finite groups. Hence open subgroup spaces of
G
are classified by subsheaves of the constructible sheaf
\pi_{0}(G)
.

Every étale
commutative
group space
G\longrightarrow S
has an open subgroup space

G_{\tor}=\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}}\ker\big([m]:G\longrightarrow G\big),

called the
torsion subgroup
of
G
. We say
G
is
torsion
if
G_{\tor}\subset G
is an equality.

2.23
Numerically trivial commutative group.

(1)

A smooth commutative group space
G\longrightarrow S
is called
numerically trivial
if its
\pi_{0}
-group
\pi_{0}(G)\longrightarrow S
is torsion.

(2)

Given a smooth commutative group space
G\longrightarrow S
, its
numerically trivial subgroup
is an open subgroup space

G^{\tau}\subset G

corresponding to the torsion subgroup space
\pi_{0}(G)_{\tor}\subset\pi_{0}(G)
.

2.23
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a smooth commutative group space over a normal scheme
S
. If
G
is numerically trivial, then it is aligned. In particular, if
G
is of finite type, then it is aligned.

Proof.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be an arbitrary smooth commutative group space. Set
E
to be the closure of the identity section,
E^{\prime}\subset E
the étale locus of
E\longrightarrow S
, and
Z=E\setminus E^{\prime}
its complement, a closed subscheme of
G
. We claim that
\pi_{0}(Z)\subset\pi_{0}(G)
is closed under taking power, i.e., if
\alpha\in\pi_{0}(Z)
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
then
m\cdot\alpha\in\pi_{0}(Z)
. This in particular implies that
\pi_{0}(Z)\cap\pi_{0}(G)_{\tor}=\varnothing
and hence the desired claim.

Let
\alpha\in\pi_{0}(E)
be a point and
s\in S
its image. Denote by

G_{s}^{\alpha}=\pi_{0}^{-1}(\alpha)\subset G_{s},\qquad E_{s}^{\alpha}=E\cap G_{s}^{\alpha}

the connected component of a locally algebraic group
G_{s}
and the restriction of
E
to it, respectively. We claim that

E_{s}^{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\mbox{singleton}\quad&\mbox{when }\ \alpha\in\pi_{0}(E^{\prime}),\\
\mbox{positive dimensional}\quad&\mbox{when }\ \alpha\in\pi_{0}(Z).\end{cases}

Choose an arbitrary section
a:S\longrightarrow G
through the component
G_{s}^{\alpha}
(possibly after shrinking
S
) and consider its
G^{\circ\circ}
-orbit
X=G^{\circ\circ}.a\subset G
, an open subspace because the
G^{\circ\circ}
-action on
G
is free.
Theorem

2.11
says
X
is separated and of finite type. If
\alpha\in\pi_{0}(E^{\prime})
then the intersection
E\cap X\longrightarrow S
is separated and étale over
s\in S
, so
E^{\alpha}_{s}=(E\cap X)_{s}
is a point. If
\alpha\in\pi_{0}(Z)
then
E\cap X\longrightarrow S
is separated and birational but not étale over
s\in S
, so by Zariski’s main (connectedness) theorem its fiber
E^{\alpha}_{s}
is positive dimensional and connected.

Now the multiplication endomorphism
[m]:G\longrightarrow G
is étale so we have
m\cdot E^{\alpha}_{s}=E^{m\cdot\alpha}_{s}
. If
\alpha\in\pi_{0}(Z)
then
E^{\alpha}_{s}
had dimension
\geq 1
, so its image under
[m]
has dimension
\geq 1
and hence
m\cdot\alpha\in\pi_{0}(Z)
. This completes the proof.
∎

2.5.
Main component

Let
S
be an integral scheme. By generic smoothness in
Corollary

2.16
, there exists a dense open subscheme
S_{0}\subset S
such that the neutral component
G^{\circ}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
of
G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a smooth subgroup space.

2.24
Main component.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space over an integral scheme
S
. Let
S_{0}\subset S
be a dense open subscheme over which the neutral component
G^{\circ}
is smooth (
Corollary

2.16
). The
main component

M
of
G
is the Zariski closure of
G^{\circ}_{0}
in
G
with the reduced induced structure.

Consider the generic fiber
G_{K}
, a locally algebraic group over the function field
K
. Then the main component
M
can be alternatively described by the Zariski closure of the generic point
\xi\in G^{\circ}_{K}
, proving its well-definedness independent to the choice of
S_{0}\subset S
. Since
M_{0}=G^{\circ}_{0}
is a subgroup space of
G_{0}
, it is natural to ask whether
M
is still a subgroup space of
G
over the full base
S
. Unfortunately, this statement is too optimistic to hold in general (§
2.6.3
).

The goal of this section is to answer this question positively in certain special cases. Before stating the theorem, we note that a Lie algebra scheme over
S
is a commutative group scheme, so it makes sense to consider its main component as in
Definition

2.24
.

2.25
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space over a reduced and geometrically unibranch scheme
S
. Assume that the main component
\mathfrak{h}
of
\Lie G
is smooth over
S
. Then the main component
M
of
G
has a unique open subspace
H\subset M
with the following properties:

(1)

H\longrightarrow S
is universally equidimensional (see §
A.1.5
).

(2)

For every universally open morphism
Z\longrightarrow S
from a reduced algebraic space
Z
, every morphism
f:Z\longrightarrow M
uniquely factors through

\hbox to97.03pt{\vbox to40.24pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 48.51234pt\lower-18.7527pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-48.51234pt}{-15.09297pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -67.13 -20.88)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The subspace
H\subset G
behaves like a subgroup space in the following sense. For the notion of a non-degenerate trait, see §
A.1.4
.

2.25
.

In
Theorem

2.25
, we have the following properties of
H
.

(1)

The following are equivalent.

(a)

H\longrightarrow S
is a locally closed subgroup space of
G
.

(b)

H\longrightarrow S
has reduced fibers.

In this case,
H\longrightarrow S
is smooth and
\Lie H=\mathfrak{h}
.

(2)

Fix a dense open subscheme
S_{0}\subset S
over which
G^{\circ}
is smooth. Then for every non-degenerate trait
\Delta\longrightarrow S
with respect to
S_{0}\subset S
, reduction of the base change

(H_{\Delta})_{\red}\longrightarrow\Delta

is a smooth locally closed subgroup of
G_{\Delta}
. As a result,

(a)

(H_{\Delta})_{\red}
is an open subgroup space of the main component of
G_{\Delta}
(see
Section

2.5
).

(b)

For every point
s\in S
, the reduction
(H_{s})_{\red}
is a closed algebraic subgroup of
G_{s}
.

2.26
.

There are examples where
H\longrightarrow S
has non-reduced fibers: see §
2.6.3
. We do not know whether the inclusion
H\subset M
in
Theorem

2.25
can be strict.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of
Theorem

2.25
and
2.5
.

2.27
.

Let
S
be an integral scheme.

(1)

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space and
M
its main component. If
M\times_{S}M
is integral, then
M\longrightarrow S
is a closed subgroup space of
G
.

(2)

Let
V\longrightarrow S
be a linear (resp. Lie algebra) scheme and
W
its main component. If
W\times_{S}W
is integral, then
W\longrightarrow S
is a closed sublinear (resp. Lie subalgebra) scheme of
V
.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in S
be the generic point. The main component
M
is characterized by the maximal integral subspace of
G
containing
e(\eta)
. Since the inverse automorphism
i:G\longrightarrow G
sends
e(\eta)
to itself, it restricts to
i:M\longrightarrow M
. Similarly, let
m:G\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G
be the multiplication morphism. Since we have assumed
M\times_{S}M
is integral, the scheme-theoretic image of
M\times_{S}M
by
m
is an integral subspace of
G
containing
e(\eta)\cdot e(\eta)=e(\eta)\in G
. We thus have a restriction
m:M\times_{S}M\longrightarrow M
. The group axioms are automatically satisfied.

Same argument applies to the second item and proves that
W
respects the Lie bracket operation. The scalar multiplication
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}V\longrightarrow V
restricts to
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}W\longrightarrow W
because
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}W
is integral.
∎

2.28
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}\longrightarrow S
be a Lie algebra scheme over an integral scheme. If its main component
\mathfrak{h}\longrightarrow S
is smooth (as in the assumption of
Theorem

2.25
), then it is automatically a closed Lie subalgebra scheme from
Lemma

2.27
.

The following is a version of
Theorem

2.25
when
S=\Delta
is a Dedekind scheme.

2.28
.

Let
G\longrightarrow\Delta
be a separated group space over a Dedekind scheme. Then its main component
H=M\longrightarrow\Delta
is always a smooth and closed subgroup space of
G
. Moreover,
\Lie H
is the main component of
\Lie G
.

Proof.

The morphism
H\longrightarrow\Delta
is flat because
H
is integral (
Section

A.1.4
). Hence

H^{\times 2}\coloneq H\times_{\Delta}H\longrightarrow\Delta

is flat as well. Fix an open subset
\Delta_{0}\subset\Delta
over which
G^{\circ}
is smooth (
Corollary

2.16
). Then
H^{\times 2}_{0}=(G_{0}^{\circ})^{\times 2}
is a smooth connected group space over
\Delta_{0}
. Together with the flatness of
H^{\times 2}\longrightarrow\Delta
, this shows (1)
H^{\times 2}
is irreducible because
H^{\times 2}_{0}
is, (2) it is
\mathrm{(R_{0})}
because
H^{\times 2}_{0}
is regular, and (3) it is
\mathrm{(S_{1})}
because it has no embedded points by
Section

A.1.4
. This proves
H^{\times 2}
is integral, and therefore
H
is a closed subgroup space by
Lemma

2.27
. It is smooth because it is flat (
Section

2
).

To show
\Lie H\subset\Lie G
is the main component, simply notice that
\Lie H\longrightarrow\Delta
is an
\mathbb{A}^{d}
-bundle so
\Lie H
is integral. Since
\Lie H=\Lie G
generically, it is the main component.
∎

2.29
.

Same argument shows that the main component of a Lie algebra scheme over
\Delta
is always a smooth subalgebra scheme.

2.30
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a separated group space over an integral scheme
S
and
M
its main component. Assume that the main component
\mathfrak{h}
of
\Lie G
is smooth over
S
. Then for every section
u:S\longrightarrow M
, the morphism
M\longrightarrow S
is equidimensional along
u(S)\subset M
.

Proof.

Translating everything in
G
by
u^{-1}
, we may assume
u=e
is the identity section. Consider the deformation of
G\longrightarrow S
to its Lie algebra (§
2.1
)

g:\mathfrak{G}\longrightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}.

To minimize potential confusion between the special fiber of
\mathfrak{G}
and restriction of
\mathfrak{G}
to the open subset
S_{0}
, we write
t_{0}=\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
for the special point: the special fiber is set-theoretically
\mathfrak{G}_{t_{0}}=\Lie G
. Let
S_{0}\subset S
be a dense open subscheme such that
M_{0}\coloneq G^{\circ}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a smooth group space. Its deformation to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\mathfrak{G}^{\circ}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
satisfies the following:

•

\mathfrak{M}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is smooth (
Lemma

2.8
) and is a restriction of
g
(
Lemma

2.7
).

•

(\mathfrak{M}_{0})_{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{h}_{0}
over
S_{0}
scheme-theoretically (
Lemma

2.8
and
2.28
).

Consider the closure
\mathfrak{M}=\overline{\mathfrak{M}_{0}}\subset\mathfrak{G}
with a reduced induced structure:

Over the open subset
S\times(\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{t_{0}\})
, this diagram is a trivial family of the main component
M\subset G
over
S
. Over the open subset
S_{0}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we had
\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\mathfrak{G}^{\circ}_{0}
by construction. Therefore, over
S\times\{t_{0}\}
, we have a sequence of closed immersions

\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{M}_{t_{0}}\subset\mathfrak{G}_{t_{0}}\subset\Lie G\qquad\mbox{over }\ S,

where the last inclusion is a set-theoretic equality.

We claim that the first closed immersion
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{M}_{t_{0}}
is a set-theoretical equality (i.e.,
\mathfrak{h}
is the reduction of
\mathfrak{M}_{t_{0}}
). Let
\Delta\longrightarrow S
be a non-degenerate trait with respect to
S_{0}
and
G_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
the base change. By
Section

2.5
, the main component of
G_{\Delta}
is a smooth subgroup space
H^{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
with
\Lie H^{\Delta}=\mathfrak{h}_{\Delta}
. Consider its deformation to the Lie algebra
h^{\Delta}:\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which is smooth by
Lemma

2.8
. From
Lemma

2.7
we can form a commutative diagram

\mathfrak{M}

, for each point

with respect to

[

. Composing with

, we obtain a non-degenerate trait
\Delta\longrightarrow S
with respect to
S_{0}

Recalling the equality
\mathfrak{M}_{0}=\mathfrak{G}^{\circ}_{0}
, we can see the section
a
sends
\Delta_{0}
into
(\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta})_{|\Delta_{0}}=\mathfrak{M}_{\Delta_{0}}=\mathfrak{G}^{\circ}_{\Delta_{0}}
. In particular, the image of
a
is in
\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta}
. We have proved a set-theoretic equality

\mathfrak{M}=\bigcup_{\Delta\longrightarrow S}\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta},

where
\Delta\longrightarrow S
runs through every non-degenerate trait of
S
. In particular, over a point
(s,t_{0})\in S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
we have a set-theoretic equality

\mathfrak{M}_{(s,t_{0})}=\bigcup_{\Delta\longrightarrow S}\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta}_{(s,t_{0})},\qquad\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta}_{(s,t_{0})}=(h^{\Delta})^{-1}(s,t_{0})

where
\Delta\longrightarrow S
runs through every non-degenerate trait through
s
. Since we already know an isomorphism
\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta}_{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{h}_{\Delta}
, we have
\mathfrak{H}^{\Delta}_{(s,t_{0})}=\mathfrak{h}_{s}
for every
\Delta\longrightarrow S
through
s
. This shows
\mathfrak{M}_{(s,t_{0})}=\mathfrak{h}_{s}
and hence
\mathfrak{M}_{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{h}
set-theoretically.

We now know the set-theoretic equality
\mathfrak{M}_{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{h}
. Say the generic fiber of
G\longrightarrow S
has dimension
d
. Our goal was to prove
M\longrightarrow S
has constant fiber dimension
d
along the identity section
e(S)
. To do so, we study the fiber dimensions of the entire morphism
m:\mathfrak{M}\longrightarrow S\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Its fiber dimension function

\dim_{m}:\mathfrak{M}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z},\qquad x\longmapsto\dim_{x}\mathfrak{M}_{m(x)}

is an upper semicontinuous function with minimum value
d
. From
\mathfrak{M}_{t_{0}}=\mathfrak{h}
we obtain
\dim_{m}(x)=d
for every
x\in e(S\times\{t_{0}\})
. By semicontinuity, there exists a Zariski open subset
V\subset\mathfrak{M}
containing
e(S\times\{t_{0}\})
such that
\dim_{m}(x)=d
for every
x\in V
. The open set
V
contains
e(S\times\{\eta\})
where
\eta\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the generic point. This shows
M\longrightarrow S
has the fiber dimension
d
along
e(S)
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

2.25
.

Since
H
is reduced and
H\longrightarrow S
is universally open (
Section

A.1.5
), the universal property proves the uniqueness of such
H
. To prove its existence, denote by
g:G\longrightarrow S
the given group space and
d
its generic relative dimension. Set

H=\{x\in M:\dim_{x}M_{g(x)}=d\},

which is an open subspace of
M
by Chevalley’s theorem on upper semicontinuity of fiber dimension. Since
H
is irreducible and
S
is geometrically unibranch,
H\longrightarrow S
is universally equidimensional by
Section

A.1.5
.

It remains to prove that such
H
satisfies the universal property. Let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a universally open morphism from a reduced algebraic space
Z
and
u:Z\longrightarrow M
an arbitrary morphism. Since
Z
is reduced, it is enough to show a set-theoretic inclusion
u(Z)\subset H
or equivalently, show
u(Z_{s})\subset H_{s}
for every
s\in S
. By
Section

A.1.1
and
A.1
, there exists a section
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z
where
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is a quasi-finite and universally equidimensional morphism (
Section

A.1.5
) from an integral scheme
S^{\prime}
containing
s\in S
in its image. The composition
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow G
defines an
S^{\prime}
-section of
G
:

\hbox to107.88pt{\vbox to39.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 53.93878pt\lower-19.33725pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{
