{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 20.88 -18.23 L 37.97 -18.23}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{27.64235pt}{-13.17752pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 38.25 -18.23)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Since
G^{\prime}\longrightarrow G
is universally equidimensional whence open, the main component of
G^{\prime}
dominates the main component of
G
; in other words, the main component of
G^{\prime}
is the reduction
M^{\prime}_{\red}
of the base change
M^{\prime}=M\times_{S}S^{\prime}
. By
Lemma

2.30
, this proves
M^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
has a constant fiber dimension
d
along
h(S^{\prime})
, or equivalently
M\longrightarrow S
has a constant fiber dimension
d
along
h(S^{\prime})
. By varying the section
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow Z
passing through closed points of
Z_{s}
, this shows the image of
u:Z\longrightarrow M
is equidimensional over
S
. That is,
u(Z_{s})\subset H_{s}
.
∎

Proof of
Section

2.5
.

The identity morphism
e:S\longrightarrow G
factors through
H
by the universal property in
Theorem

2.25
. The inverse morphism
i:G\longrightarrow G
similarly restricts to
i:H\longrightarrow H
. The multiplication morphism
m:G\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G
, however, only restricts to

(H\times_{S}H)_{\red}\longrightarrow H,

since
H\times_{S}H\longrightarrow S
is universally open but the reducedness of
H\times_{S}H
is uncertain.

(1) The implication (a)
\Longrightarrow
(b) is clear from Cartier’s theorem. To show (b)
\Longrightarrow
(a), use
Theorem

A.13
to deduce that
H\longrightarrow S
is flat. Hence
H\times_{S}H\longrightarrow S
is flat and has reduced fibers by (Tag 035Z). By
[
Mat89
, Theorem 23.9]
, this implies the total space
H\times_{S}H
is reduced and hence the multiplication morphism on
H
above is well-defined. This makes
H\longrightarrow S
a subgroup space of
G
. It is smooth because it is universally equidimensional (
Section

2
).

(2) Let
\Delta\longrightarrow S
be a non-degenerate trait. Since
H\longrightarrow S
is universally equidimensional, its base change
H_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is again universally equidimensional. Hence the reduction
H_{\Delta,\red}=(H_{\Delta})_{\red}\longrightarrow\Delta
is flat by
Section

A.1.4
. The above discussion shows that
H_{\Delta,\red}
has the identity, inverse morphism, and the multiplication up to the non-reducedness ambiguity

m_{\Delta}:(H_{\Delta}\times_{\Delta}H_{\Delta})_{\red}\longrightarrow H_{\Delta,\red}.

But
H_{\Delta,\red}\longrightarrow\Delta
was flat, so
H_{\Delta,\red}\times_{\Delta}H_{\Delta,\red}\longrightarrow\Delta
is again flat and hence
H_{\Delta,\red}\times_{\Delta}H_{\Delta,\red}
is reduced by
Section

A.1.4
. This shows
m_{\Delta}
is indeed the multiplication morphism on
H_{\Delta,\red}
.
∎

2.6.
Automorphism space, Picard space, and examples

2.6.1.
The automorphism group space

Given an algebraic space
f:X\longrightarrow S
, its automorphism sheaf is an fppf sheaf of groups

\underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{f}:(\mathsf{Sch}/S)^{op}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Gp},\qquad\big[T\longrightarrow S\big]\longmapsto\{g:X_{T}\longrightarrow X_{T}:\mbox{automorphism over }T\}.

2.31
Automorphism space.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a proper flat algebraic space over a locally Noetherian scheme. Then

(1)

\underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{f}
is representable by a separated and locally finite type group space
\Aut_{f}\longrightarrow S
.

(2)

There exists a canonical isomorphism of fppf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules

\Lie\Aut_{f}=f_{*}T^{\fppf}_{f}.

Here
T^{\fppf}_{f}=\Hom^{\fppf}_{X}(\Omega_{f},\mathcal{O}_{X})
is the fppf dual (
A.17
) of the relative cotangent sheaf (that is represented by the tangent space
\mathbb{T}_{f}
(
A.19
)).

Proof.

[
Ols06
, Lemma 5.1]
or (Tag 0D19) shows that the morphism of fppf sheaves assigning a morphism to its graph

\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X)\hookrightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hilb}}_{X\times_{S}X/S},\qquad\big[g:X_{T}\longrightarrow X_{T}\big]\longmapsto\big[\Gamma_{g}\subset(X\times_{S}X)_{T}\big]

is (representable by) an open immersion. Since
\underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{f}=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X)\cap t(\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{S}(X,X))
where
t
is the transposition involution on
\operatorname{Hilb}_{X\times_{S}X/S}
, we get an open immersion
\underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{f}\hookrightarrow\underline{\operatorname{Hilb}}_{X\times_{S}X/S}
. The Hilbert sheaf is representable by a separated and locally finite type algebraic space (Tag 0DM7), so this proves the first item. The second item is
[
AF16
, Proposition 8.5]
.
∎

We call such a group space
\Aut_{f}\longrightarrow S
the
automorphism (group) space
of
f
. If
f:X\longrightarrow S
is a flat projective scheme then the Hilbert functor is representable by a scheme, so
\Aut_{f}\longrightarrow S
is a group scheme with quasi-projective connected components.

2.6.2.
The Picard group space

Given an algebraic space
f:X\longrightarrow S
, its Picard sheaf is
\underline{\Pic}_{f}=R^{1}f_{*}\mathbb{G}_{m}
, the higher direct image of an fppf abelian sheaf
\mathbb{G}_{m}
. It is equivalently an fppf sheafification of the presheaf

(\mathsf{Sch}/S)^{op}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Gp},\qquad\big[T\longrightarrow S\big]\longmapsto\Pic(X_{T})/\Pic(T).

2.32
Picard space.

Let
X
be an algebraic space and
f:X\longrightarrow S
a proper flat morphism over a locally Noetherian scheme with
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\fppf}=\mathcal{O}_{S}^{\fppf}
.
2
2

2

A more standard way of saying this is:
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{S}
holds universally
or
f
is cohomologycally flat in dimension
0
.
Then

(1)

\underline{\Pic}_{f}
is representable by a locally separated, locally finite type, and commutative group space
\Pic_{f}\longrightarrow S
.

(2)

There exists a canonical isomorphism of fppf
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-modules

\Lie\Pic_{f}=\big(R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\big)^{\fppf}.

Here
\big(R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\big)^{\fppf}
is the associated fppf sheaf (
A.16
) of the coherent sheaf
R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Proof.

The first item is
[
Art69
, Theorem 7.3]
,
[
BLR90
, §8.3]
, or (Tag 0D24). The second item is
[
BLR90
, Theorem 8.4.1]
or
[
LLR04
, Proposition 1.3]
.
∎

Such a commutative group space
\Pic_{f}
is called the
Picard (group) space
of
f
.

2.6.3.
Examples and counterexamples of group spaces

2.33
Basic examples.

Let
S=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{x}
be an affine line with coordinate
x
over a field
k
of characteristic
0
.

•

Let
G
be an affine line with double origin. Then the morphism collapsing the two origins
G\longrightarrow S
has a smooth and non-separated group scheme structure. The fibers over any nonzero
s\in S
is trivial, and the fiber over
0
is
\mathbb{Z}/2
.

•

Let
G=\mathbb{A}^{1}\sqcup\{0\}
. Then
G\longrightarrow S
has an obvious group structure, which is separated and non-smooth. Its Lie algebra scheme is smooth. The morphism
G\longrightarrow S
is not equidimensional in the sense of §
A.1.5
.

•

Let
G=(xy=0)\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(x,y)}
. Then the projection
G\longrightarrow S
admits a clear group structure, which is separated and non-smooth. Indeed, its fiber dimension jumps at
0\in S
so it is not equidimensional.

•

Let
G=((x+y)y=0)\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(x,y)}
. The projection
G\longrightarrow S
is flat but can never admit any group scheme structure. This is because its central fiber
\Spec k[y]/y^{2}
is non-reduced, so it cannot admit any algebraic group structure by Cartier’s theorem.

•

Fix a set of infinite closed points
Z=\{i\in\mathbb{A}^{1}(k):i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
and
G=\mathbb{A}^{1}\sqcup Z
. The projection
G\longrightarrow S
has a group scheme structure whose fibers over
Z
are
\mathbb{Z}/2
and trivial elsewhere. Then
G\longrightarrow S
cannot be smooth over every Zariski open subset
S_{0}\subset S
(generic smoothness in
Corollary

2.16
fails for
G
when it is not finite type).

•

This time, set
G
to be an affine line with infinitely many double points over
Z
. Again the projection
G\longrightarrow S
has a group scheme structure. It is non-separated over every Zariski open subset
S_{0}\subset S
(generic separatedness in
Section

2.3
fails).

2.34
Equidimensional but non-smooth group scheme.

Set
S=(xy=0)\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(x,y)}
and
G=S\sqcup\mathbb{A}^{1}_{x}\sqcup\big(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{y}\setminus(y=0)\big)
. The morphism
f:G\longrightarrow S
admits a group scheme structure with
\mathbb{Z}/2
fibers. It is equidimensional but not universally equidimensional, so it is not smooth by
Section

2
. Similar example works for a nodal cubic curve
S
, in which case the base is integral.

2.35
Group scheme with non-reduced total space.

Let
C=(x^{3}=y^{2})\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(x,y)}
be a cuspidal cubic. Its tangent scheme
\mathbb{T}_{C}=\Spec_{C}(\Sym^{*}\Omega_{C})\longrightarrow C
is a separated commutative group scheme (it is a linear scheme). Writing
u=3dx
and
v=2dy
, the total space
\mathbb{T}_{C}
is a closed subscheme of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(x,y)}\times\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(u,v)}
cut out by an ideal

I=(x^{3}-y^{2},\ x^{2}u-yv)\ \subset\ k[x,y,u,v],

which is not radical; while
xv-yu\notin I
, we have
(xv-yu)^{3}\in I
.

2.36
Weak and strict neutral components.

Let
\Delta=\Spec R
be a local Dedekind scheme with residue field
k
and function field
K
.

•

Let
f:S\longrightarrow\Delta
be a minimal elliptic surface with a central fiber
S_{t_{0}}
of Kodaira type
\mathrm{I}_{2}
. Its relative Picard scheme
\Pic_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
is smooth and non-separated
[
BLR90
, Proposition 8.4.2]
. The scheme
\Pic_{f}
has
\NS(S_{K})\cong\mathbb{Z}
connected components. Its neutral component
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
is smooth (and non-separated) by
Section

2.2.2
and parametrizes line bundles of multi-degree
(d,-d)
for all
d\in\mathbb{Z}
on the
\mathrm{I}_{2}
central fiber
S_{t_{0}}
. Its strict neutral component
\Pic^{\circ\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
is smooth by
Theorem

2.9
and parametrizes line bundles of multi-degree
(0,0)
on the central fiber
S_{t_{0}}
.

•

In
[
BLR90
, Example 10.1.5]
, a smooth, separated and connected group scheme
G\longrightarrow\Delta
with generic fiber
G_{K}=\mathbb{G}_{m,K}
and special fiber
G_{t_{0}}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
of infinitely many connected components is constructed (it is the Néron model of
\mathbb{G}_{m,K}
over
\Delta
). We have
G^{\circ}=G
because
G
is connected. However, its strict neutral component is
G^{\circ\circ}=\mathbb{G}_{m,\Delta}
.

2.37
\pi_{0}
-group.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a flat projective morphism with
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\fppf}=\mathcal{O}_{S}^{\fppf}
, so that the Picard space
\Pic_{f}\longrightarrow S
is well-defined. Assume that
R^{1}f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
is locally free, or
\Lie\Pic_{f}\longrightarrow S
is smooth by
Theorem

2.32
. The
\pi_{0}
-group
\NS_{f}=\pi_{0}(\Pic_{f})\longrightarrow S
is well-defined, parametrizing the Néron–Severi groups
\NS(X_{s})
for
s\in S
. It is a locally quasi-finite group space. A well-known fact is that the Picard rank function

S\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z},\qquad s\longmapsto\rk\NS(X_{s})

is wild and in general not a constructible function. This is explained by the last example in
Example

2.33
(and that
\NS_{f}
may have infinitely many disconnected components).

2.38
Non-aligned group.

In
[
Hol19
]
, non-aligned smooth group spaces
G\longrightarrow S
over a regular Noetherian base
S
are constructed. Holmes’ construction is the neutral Picard scheme
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow S
for a flat projective family of non-aligned stable curves
f:\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow S
. See the original reference for more details.

2.39
Automorphism scheme and main component.

Let
f:S\longrightarrow\Delta
be a minimal elliptic surface over a local Dedekind scheme
\Delta
with a central fiber of Kodaira type
\textnormal{I}_{2}
:

S_{t_{0}}=C_{0}\cup C_{1}\quad\mbox{for smooth rational curves }C_{0}\mbox{ and }C_{1}.

Assume
f
has sections
T_{0}
and
T_{1}
passing through
C_{0}
and
C_{1}
, respectively.

The automorphism scheme
\Aut_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
is a separated group scheme. Its generic fiber is an algebraic group
\Aut_{S_{K}/K}
whose neutral component is isomorphic to
S_{K}
(translation automorphisms of the elliptic curve
S_{K}
). Its central fiber
\Aut_{f,t_{0}}
is a (non-commutative) linear algebraic group

\Aut_{f,t_{0}}=\big(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times 2}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\big)\rtimes\mathfrak{S}_{2},

where
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
acts on
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times 2}
by swapping factors. In particular,
\Aut_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
is not equidimensional and hence non-smooth. Nevertheless, thanks to
Section

2.5
, its main component
H\subset\Aut_{f}
is a smooth and closed subgroup scheme over
\Delta
. It is commutative because it is generically commutative and separated.

2.40
Picard scheme and separated quotient.

Let us keep working on the previous example
f:S\longrightarrow\Delta
. The Picard scheme
\Pic_{f}
and its neutral component
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
are smooth and non-separated group schemes over
\Delta
(e.g.,
[
BLR90
, §8–9]
). We have two short exact sequences of group schemes

0\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\Pic_{f}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\longrightarrow 0,\qquad 0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m,k}\longrightarrow\Pic_{f,t_{0}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2}\longrightarrow 0,

each over
\Delta
and over the closed point
t_{0}\in\Delta
, respectively. The discrepancy between
\NS(S_{K})=\mathbb{Z}
and
\NS(S_{t_{0}})=\mathbb{Z}^{2}
explains the strict inclusion
\Pic^{\circ\circ}_{f}\subsetneq\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
, and that
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
is not of finite type. More specifically, consider a line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{S}(C_{1})
on
S
. It is generically trivial on
S_{K}
but has a multi-degree
(2,-2)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
on the central fiber
S_{t_{0}}
(compute the intersection numbers). That is, it is a section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
that cannot be separated to the identity. Consider a line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{S}(T_{0}-T_{1})
. It is a section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
with multi-degree
(1,-1)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
on the central fiber
S_{t_{0}}
.

The group scheme
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
is aligned by
Section

2.3
, so it admits a separated quotient
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\twoheadrightarrow\check{P}
by
Section

2.3
. The closure of the identity section
E\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{f}
is an étale group space over
\Delta
whose central fiber passes through the component

(2,-2)\ \in\ \pi_{0}(\Pic^{\circ}_{f})_{t_{0}}=\{(d,-d)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

We will later see in
Example

3.8
that
\check{P}_{t_{0}}
has two connected components, meaning
E
does not pass through the component
(1,-1)
.

We conclude with examples where
Theorem

2.25
fails without appropriate hypotheses.

2.41
Main component I.

Let
C
be a curve with single node at
p
. Its tangent scheme
\mathbb{T}_{C}\longrightarrow C
is a commutative group scheme with fiber
(\mathbb{T}_{C})_{p}=\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times 2}
over the node but
(\mathbb{T}_{C})_{x}=\mathbb{G}_{a}
elsewhere. The main component
M
of
\mathbb{T}_{C}
cannot be a subgroup scheme, since there are two branches of tangent directions at the node
p
;
Theorem

2.25
and
2.5
do not apply because
C
is not unibranch.

2.42
Main component II.

Let
C
be a cuspidal cubic with a cusp at
p=(0,0)
, and consider its tangent scheme
\mathbb{T}_{C}\longrightarrow C
as in
Example

2.35
. Let
M\subset\mathbb{T}_{C}
be its main component. At first glance, the family
M\longrightarrow C
seems to have
\mathbb{A}^{1}
fibers over every point and hence is a subgroup scheme. This is not the case, as we show its fiber over
p
is a double line.

The primary decomposition of the ideal
I
in
Example

2.35
is
[
Mac2
]

(x^{3}-y^{2},\ x^{2}u-yv,\ yu-xv,\ xu^{2}-v^{2})\ \cap\ (x^{3},\ xy,\ y^{2},\ x^{2}u-yv).

The main component
M\subset\mathbb{A}^{4}_{(x,y,u,v)}
is cut out by the first ideal (which is a radical ideal). Setting
x=y=0
, the fiber
M_{p}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(u,v)}
is defined by
(v^{2})
, i.e., it is a double line. Its equidimensional subscheme
H\subset M
in
Theorem

2.25
is
H=M
. This shows that the assumption on reduced fibers in
Section

2.5
is necessary.

3.
Néron models

Let
\Delta
be a Dedekind (=
1
-dimensional regular Noetherian) scheme with function field
K
. Given a smooth variety
N_{K}\longrightarrow\Spec K
, the Néron model theory in
[
BLR90
]
seeks for its nicest smooth and separated model
N\longrightarrow\Delta
of finite type satisfying a certain universal property, the Néron mapping property.
[
Hol19
, Definition 1.1]
introduced a generalization of this notion to higher dimensional bases
S
. The separatedness assumption is subsequently dropped in
[
Hol+23
]
.

Let
S
be an algebraic space and
S_{0}\subset S
a scheme-theoretically dense open subspace. Given an algebraic space
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
, its
extension
over
S
is an algebraic space
N\longrightarrow S
equipped with a fixed identification
N_{|S_{0}}\cong N_{0}
.

3.1
Néron model;
[
Hol+23
, Definition 6.1]
.

Let
S
be an algebraic space,
S_{0}\subset S
its scheme-theoretically dense open subspace, and
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
a smooth algebraic space over it. An extension
N\longrightarrow S
of
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is called a
Néron extension
or
Néron model
if:

(1)

N\longrightarrow S
is a smooth algebraic space.

(2)

(Néron mapping property) For every smooth algebraic space
Z\longrightarrow S
, the restriction map

\operatorname{Mor}_{S}(Z,N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{S_{0}}(Z_{0},N_{0}),\qquad u\longmapsto u_{0}=u_{|S_{0}}

is bijective.

For simplicity, we will often say “
N
is Néron under the extension of the base
S_{0}\subset S
” or even “
N
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
”.

3.2
.

(1)

Néron model is unique up to unique isomorphism once it exists. It is a universal extension retaining only the smoothness of a given algebraic space, so it may fail to be separated, surjective, or of finite type (but it is always locally of finite type because it is smooth).

(2)

It is enough to check the Néron mapping property axiom for quasi-affine smooth morphisms
Z\longrightarrow S
of finite type. To prove this, cover an arbitrary smooth algebraic space
Z\longrightarrow S
by finite type affine schemes
\Spec A\longrightarrow S
for which the Néron mapping property hold by assumption. Two étale local charts intersect on a finite type quasi-affine scheme
\Spec A\times_{Z}\Spec B\longrightarrow S
, so two morphisms
\Spec A\longrightarrow N
and
\Spec B\longrightarrow N
agreeing over
S_{0}
uniquely patch over
S
by the Néron mapping property.

(3)

If
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a smooth algebraic space and
j:S_{0}\longrightarrow S
is an open immersion, then the Néron model represents the lisse-étale sheaf
j_{*}N_{0}
on
S
.

(4)

Table

1
compares the terminology with others in literature.

Original terminology

In terms of
Definition

3.1

Néron model in
[
BLR90
]

Finite type Néron model over
\Delta

Néron lft-model in
[
BLR90
, §10]

Néron model over
\Delta

Néron model in
[
Hol19
]

Separated Néron model of an abelian scheme

Table 1.
Comparison of
Definition

3.1
with others in the literature. Here
\Delta
is a Dedekind scheme.

The base
S
in
Definition

3.1
is allowed to be an algebraic space for technical flexibility. However, one can reduce to the case when
S
is a scheme using
Section

3.1
. Throughout this section, we assume
S_{0}\subset S
is a (scheme-theoretically) dense open subspace and
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a smooth algebraic space. The subscript
-_{0}
always indicates the fiber product
-\times_{S}S_{0}
.

3.1.
Basic properties

Basic properties of Néron models will be discussed in this subsection. The following two statements are immediate from the universal property of fiber product.

3.2
Product.

Let
N
and
M
be smooth algebraic spaces over
S
that are Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. Then
N\times_{S}M
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. ∎

3.2
Product over two bases.

Let
S
and
S^{\prime}
be smooth algebraic spaces over
T
, and
S_{0}\subset S
and
S^{\prime}_{0}\subset S^{\prime}
their dense open subspaces. Let
N\longrightarrow S
be Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
and similarly
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
as well. Then
N\times_{T}N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S\times_{T}S^{\prime}
is Néron under
S_{0}\times_{T}S^{\prime}_{0}\subset S\times_{T}S^{\prime}
. ∎

3.2
Sequence of base extensions.

Let
S_{0}\subset S_{1}\subset S
be a sequence of dense open subspaces.

(1)

Let
N
be Néron under
S_{1}\subset S
and
N_{1}=N_{|S_{1}}
Néron under
S_{0}\subset S_{1}
. Then
N
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
.

(2)

Let
N
be Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
and
N_{1}
Néron under
S_{0}\subset S_{1}
. Then
N
is Néron under
S_{1}\subset S
.

(3)

Let
N
be a separated Néron model under
S_{0}\subset S
. Then
N
is Néron under
S_{1}\subset S
and
N_{1}=N_{|S_{1}}
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S_{1}
.

Proof.

The first item is clear. To prove the second item, let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space and consider a sequence of restriction maps
N(Z)\longrightarrow N(Z_{1})\longrightarrow N(Z_{0})
. The second map and the composition map are bijective, so the first map is bijective. In the case of third item, both maps are injective because
Z_{0}\subset Z_{1}\subset Z
are dense and
N
is separated (
Lemma

A.6
and
A.7
). The composition is bijective, so both are bijective.
∎

The following proposition claims that the construction of the Néron model is a local property on the base in smooth topology.

3.2
.

Let
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
be a smooth algebraic space and
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
a smooth morphism of algebraic spaces. Consider
S_{0}^{\prime}=S^{\prime}\times_{S}S_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
and a cartesian diagram

(1)

(Smooth base change)
If
N_{0}
has a Néron model
N\longrightarrow S
, then
N_{0}^{\prime}
has a Néron model
N^{\prime}=N\times_{S}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
.

(2)

(Smooth descent)
Assume further
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is surjective. If
N_{0}^{\prime}
has a Néron model
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
, then
N_{0}
has a Néron model
N\longrightarrow S
. Moreover,
N\times_{S}S^{\prime}\cong N^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The first item is a routine diagram chasing using the universal property of fiber product. The second item is proved in
[
Hol19
, Lemma 6.1]
, but since their definition is slightly different (Néron models are assumed to be separated) we provide a proof that does not use separatedness.

Let us first construct an algebraic space
N\longrightarrow S
by descending the Néron model
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
along
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
. Consider
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
as a covering in smooth topology, and define its powers
S^{\prime\prime}=S^{\prime}\times_{S}S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime\prime}=S^{\prime}\times_{S}S^{\prime}\times_{S}S^{\prime}
with projections
p_{i}:S^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
and
p_{jk}:S^{\prime\prime\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime\prime}
. Write

N^{\prime}S^{\prime}=N^{\prime}\times_{S}S^{\prime}=p_{1}^{*}N^{\prime},\qquad S^{\prime}N^{\prime}=S^{\prime}\times_{S}N^{\prime}=p_{2}^{*}N^{\prime},\qquad\cdots.

To descend, we need to construct an isomorphism (descent datum) of algebraic spaces
\varphi:N^{\prime}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}N^{\prime}
over
S^{\prime\prime}
making the cocyle diagram commute over
S^{\prime\prime\prime}
:

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-49.75975pt}{-7.69695pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -68.85 -10.65)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 28.63 -5.95 L 55.87 5.73}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.91905}{0.39413}{-0.39413}{0.91905}{40.56152pt}{4.22115pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.91905 0.39413 -0.39413 0.91905 56.12 5.84)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
}}{
}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{33.0715pt}{-7.69695pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 45.76 -10.65)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The given algebraic space
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
induces a trivial descent datum
\varphi_{0}:N^{\prime}S^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}N^{\prime}_{0}
over
S_{0}^{\prime\prime}
. The morphism
S^{\prime}N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
N^{\prime}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime\prime}
) is Néron under
S_{0}^{\prime\prime}\subset S^{\prime\prime}
since it is a smooth base change of the Néron model
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
. Hence
\varphi_{0}
uniquely extends to an isomorphism
\varphi:N^{\prime}S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}N^{\prime}
over
S^{\prime\prime}
. The commutativity of (
3.3
) is from its commutativity over
S_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}
together with the Néron mapping property of
S^{\prime}S^{\prime}N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime\prime\prime}
under
S_{0}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subset S^{\prime\prime\prime}
. This constructs a descent datum
\varphi
and hence an algebraic space
N\longrightarrow S
.

To prove the Néron mapping property of such
N\longrightarrow S
, say
Z\longrightarrow S
is a smooth algebriace space and
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
is a morphism over
S_{0}
. Pulling back to
S^{\prime}
, the morphism
u^{\prime}_{0}:Z^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}_{0}
uniquely extends to
u^{\prime}:Z^{\prime}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
by the Néron mapping property of
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
. To (uniquely) descend it to
u:Z\longrightarrow N
, it is enough to show the two pullbacks

p_{1}^{*}u^{\prime},p_{2}^{*}u^{\prime}:Z^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow N^{\prime\prime}

agree (now that we know
N\longrightarrow S
exists, we may identify
N^{\prime\prime}\coloneq N^{\prime}S^{\prime}=S^{\prime}N^{\prime}
via
\varphi
). Since
u^{\prime}_{0}
already descends to
u_{0}
over
S_{0}
by assumption, we have
p_{1}^{*}u^{\prime}_{0}=p_{2}^{*}u^{\prime}_{0}
as morphisms of algebraic spaces over
S_{0}^{\prime\prime}
. Such an equality extends to
p_{1}^{*}u^{\prime}=p_{2}^{*}u^{\prime}
by the uniqueness part of the Néron mapping property of
N^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

3.4
.

Here are some remarks on
Section

3.1
.

(1)

Smooth descent property is likely to fail if we insist everything to be a scheme in
Definition

3.1
, as not every descent datum is effective for schemes (e.g.,
[
BLR90
, §6.7]
).

(2)

The proposition fails for flat and locally finite type morphisms
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
. In other words, the construction of the Néron model is not an fppf local property on the base (
[
BLR90
, §7.2]
or
Example

3.9
). Nevertheless, there are the following variants:

•

[
LT16
, Proposition 2.4]
Let
N\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space and
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
an fppf morphism. If the base change
N^{\prime}
is
separated
Néron under
S^{\prime}_{0}\subset S^{\prime}
, then
N
is separated Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
.

•

[
BLR90
, Proposition 7.6.6]
Let
f:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
be a finite flat morphism and
N^{\prime}
a separated Néron model under
S_{0}^{\prime}\subset S^{\prime}
. Then
f_{*}N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is a quasi-separated Néron model under
S_{0}\subset S
.

For the first item, simply notice that the last paragraph of the proof of
Section

3.1
holds for fppf morphisms
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
, but with the uniqueness of the Néron mapping property replaced by the separatedness of the morphism. For the second item, start with
[
Ols06
, Theorem 1.5]
that shows the fppf sheaf
f_{*}N^{\prime}
is representable by a quasi-separated and locally finite type algebraic space (need
f
to be proper flat). The adjunction

\operatorname{Mor}_{S}(Z,f_{*}N^{\prime})=\operatorname{Mor}_{S^{\prime}}(Z^{\prime},N^{\prime})

for affine schemes
Z
shows
f_{*}N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is formally smooth (need
f
to be affine). The adjunction for smooth
Z\longrightarrow S
shows the Néron mapping property.

3.4
Composition.

Let
f:S\longrightarrow T
be étale and Néron under
T_{0}\subset T
and
g:N\longrightarrow S
be Néron under
S_{0}=S_{|T_{0}}\subset S
. Then the composition
f\circ g:N\longrightarrow T
is Néron under
T_{0}\subset T
.

Proof.

Let
Z\longrightarrow T
be a smooth algebraic space and
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
be a morphism. The composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{0}:Z_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}

uniquely extends to
v:Z\longrightarrow S
by the Néron mapping property of
S\longrightarrow T
. Since
Z\longrightarrow T
is smooth and
S\longrightarrow T
is étale,
v:Z\longrightarrow S
is smooth (e.g.,
[
BLR90
, Lemma 2.2.9]
). Apply the Néron mapping property of
N\longrightarrow S
to conclude.
∎

3.2.
Review: abelian schemes over Dedekind schemes

Existence of a Néron model is a difficult problem. By the pioneering work of Néron
[
N“’64
]
and Raynaud
[
Ray66
]
, we know every abelian torsor over a Dedekind scheme has a Néron model. Let us quickly review this fact following
[
BLR90
, §1.3]
.

3.5
Néron, Raynaud.

Let
\Delta
be a Dedekind scheme and
\Delta^{*}\subset\Delta
its dense open subscheme. Then

(1)

Every abelian scheme
P^{*}\longrightarrow\Delta^{*}
admits a quasi-projective Néron model
P\longrightarrow\Delta
.

(2)

Every
P^{*}
-torsor
N^{*}\longrightarrow\Delta^{*}
admits a quasi-projective Néron model
N\longrightarrow\Delta
.

The reader should keep in mind that the Néron model
N\longrightarrow\Delta
in the second item is not necessarily surjective: it is surjective if and only if it is a
P
-torsor. See
[
BLR90
, Corollary 6.5.3]
and its following paragraph, or
Section

3.4
. Néron’s existence proof is constructive:

3.6
Néron.

Let
\Delta=\Spec R
be a local Dedekind scheme with function field
K
and
\Delta^{*}=\Spec K\subset\Delta
a dense open subscheme. Then the Néron model of an abelian variety
\nu_{K}:P^{*}\longrightarrow\Delta^{*}
can be constructed as follows.

Step 1

Fix an arbitrary flat projective model
\nu^{(1)}:P^{(1)}\longrightarrow\Delta
of
\nu_{K}
from a regular scheme
P^{(1)}
.

Step 2

Consider its smooth locus

\nu^{(2)}:P^{(2)}=P^{(1)}\setminus\Sing(\nu^{(1)})\longrightarrow\Delta.

This is a weak Néron model of
\nu_{K}
.

Step 3

Fix a relative canonical divisor of
\nu^{(2)}
of the form
K_{\nu^{(2)}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}F_{i}
where
F_{1},\cdots,F_{m}
are irreducible components of the central fiber
P^{(2)}_{0}
. Define
a=\min\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\}
and set

\nu^{(3)}:P^{(3)}=P^{(2)}\setminus\bigcup_{a_{i}>a}F_{i}\longrightarrow\Delta.

Step 4

\nu^{(3)}
admits a birational group law extending the group law of
\nu_{K}
. Its group scheme solution
\nu:P\longrightarrow\Delta
contains
P^{(3)}
as an open subscheme and is the Néron model of
\nu_{K}
.

3.7
.

(1)

For the notion of a weak Néron model, see
[
BLR90
, Definition 1.1.1 and 3.5.1]
or its generalization
Definition

3.20
. The notion of a birational group law and its group scheme solution can be found in §5 in loc. cit. but we will not use them in this article.

(2)

If
\nu^{(2)}
in Step 2 has a group structure, then it is already Néron. See Theorem 7.1.1 in loc. cit. or its generalization
Theorem

3.22
.

3.8
Minimal elliptic surface.

Let
f:S\longrightarrow\Delta
be a relatively minimal elliptic surface over a local Dedekind scheme
\Delta=\Spec R

with a fixed section
. Let
f_{K}:S_{K}\longrightarrow\Delta^{*}=\Spec K
be the generic fiber, an elliptic curve over
K
. Since
f
is projective and
S
is regular, we can run the construction
3.6
with
\nu^{(1)}=f
. Fixing a trivial relative canonical divisor
K_{f}=0
, Step 2 and 3 yield

\nu^{(3)}:S^{\prime}=S\setminus\Sing f\longrightarrow\Delta.

Step 4 gives a Néron model
N\supset S^{\prime}
that is potentially larger than
S^{\prime}
. The valuative criterion of properness for
f
together with
Section

A.1.1
shows that every étale local section of
S\longrightarrow\Delta
factors through
S^{\prime}
, whence the equality
S^{\prime}=N
. This is essentially the method in
[
BLR90
, Proposition 1.5.1]
.

Here are two more ways of constructing the Néron model that will be discussed in this article. The first way is to start with the Picard space
\Pic_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
. It is a smooth group space by Proposition 8.4.2 in loc. cit. Its neutral component
\Pic^{\circ}_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
admits a separated quotient
\check{P}\longrightarrow\Delta
by
Section

2.3
, which turns out to be the Néron model of
S_{K}
. See Theorem 9.5.4 in loc. cit or
Lemma

6.9
.

The second way is to start with the automorphism scheme
\Aut_{f}\longrightarrow\Delta
. Its main component
P\longrightarrow\Delta
is a smooth, commutative, and quasi-projective group space by
Section

2.5
. We will show it is also a Néron model of
S_{K}
in
Theorem

5.2
. Therefore, all three constructions
S^{\prime}=\check{P}=P
give us the Néron model for elliptic fibrations. For higher-dimensional Lagrangian fibrations, none of the equalities will hold in general.

3.9
.

Let
\Delta=\Spec R
be the local Dedekind scheme with function field
K
and
P^{*}\longrightarrow\Delta^{*}=\Spec K
be an abelian scheme over
K
. It has a quasi-projective Néron model
P\longrightarrow\Delta
by
Theorem

3.5
. Assume that
P
is not semiabelian. By the semistable reduction theorem (e.g.,
[
BLR90
, §7.4]
), there exists a finite ramified morphism
\tilde{\Delta}=\Spec\tilde{R}\longrightarrow\Delta
with function field
\tilde{K}
such that the Néron model of

\tilde{P}^{*}=P_{\tilde{K}}\longrightarrow\tilde{\Delta}^{*}=\Spec\tilde{K}

is a semiabelian scheme
\tilde{P}^{n}\longrightarrow\tilde{\Delta}
. The base change
\tilde{P}=P\times_{\Delta}\tilde{\Delta}\longrightarrow\tilde{\Delta}
is still a smooth group scheme (with a non-semiabelian central fiber), so by the Néron mapping property we have a homomorphism

\tilde{P}\longrightarrow\tilde{P}^{n}.

This map is an isomorphism over
\tilde{\Delta}^{*}
but contracts the central fiber of
\tilde{P}
.

3.3.
Quasi-projective étale schemes

In this section, we study the Néron model of the simplest smooth morphisms
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
: separated, finite type, and étale morphisms. Grothendieck’s formulation of Zariski’s main theorem (Tag 082K) plays a central role in this case. The following is a direct consequence of Zariski’s main theorem.

3.10
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a morphism between algebraic spaces and assume
S
is Noetherian. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

f
is separated, finite type, and étale.

(2)

f
is quasi-affine, finite type, and étale.

(3)

f
is quasi-projective étale. ∎

Assuming
S
is a scheme, we call such an algebraic space
X
a quasi-projective étale scheme (over
S
). The main result of this subsection is the following.

3.11
.

Let
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
be a quasi-projective étale scheme over a normal Noetherian scheme
S
. Then

(1)

N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
admits a quasi-projective Néron model
N\longrightarrow S
.

(2)

Every quasi-projective étale extension
M\longrightarrow S
of
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is an open subscheme of
N
.

3.12
.

Let
N^{a}\longrightarrow S
be a quasi-projective étale scheme over a normal Noetherian scheme
S
. Then

(1)

There exists a maximal quasi-projective étale scheme
N
containing
N^{a}
as an open subscheme.

(2)

For every dense open subscheme
S_{0}\subset S
,
N
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
.

Proof.

(1) Zariski’s main theorem factorizes the given morphism into an open immersion and finite morphism
N^{a}\hookrightarrow T\longrightarrow S
. Since
N^{a}
is normal, we may assume
T
is normal and
N^{a}\hookrightarrow T
is dense. Define
N\subset T
by the étale locus of the morphism
T\longrightarrow S
. We claim such
N\longrightarrow S
is the desired maximal quasi-projective étale scheme containing
N^{a}
.

Consider an arbitrary quasi-projective étale scheme
M\longrightarrow S
containing
N^{a}
as an open subscheme. We have a commutative diagram over
S
:

\hbox to99.82pt{\vbox to37.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 49.91011pt\lower-18.65274pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

We first show the rational map
j:M\dashrightarrow T
is a morphism. Since
M
is normal and
T\longrightarrow S
is proper,
j
is defined on codimension
1
points in
M
by the valuative criterion. But since
T\longrightarrow S
is affine, the undefined locus of
j
must be of pure codimension
1
by
[
EGA $IV˙4$
, Corollaire 20.4.12]
, forcing
j
to be defined everywhere.

Now
j:M\longrightarrow T
is a quasi-finite (because
M\longrightarrow S
is quasi-finite) and birational morphism to a normal scheme
T
. Zariski’s main theorem shows that it is an open immersion. Hence it factors through
M\xhookrightarrow{j}N\subset T
, implying the desired maximality of
N
.

(2) Let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space and
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
a morphism over
S_{0}
. The rational map
u:Z\dashrightarrow N\subset T
extends to a morphism
u:Z\longrightarrow T
by the same argument as above, so it remains to show
u(Z)\subset N
. Let
s\in S
be a point and
z\in Z_{s}
a closed point. Since the claim is local on
S
, shrinking
S
étale locally we may assume there exists a section
e:S\longrightarrow Z
passing through
z
. The composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f:S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

is a section of
T
passing through
u(z)\in T_{s}
. Over
S_{0}
,
u_{0}
lands in
N_{0}
by assumption and hence
f_{0}:S_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}\subset T_{0}
is a section of an étale morphism
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
. This means
f_{0}
is an open immersion. Since
T
is normal, by Zariski’s main theorem the section
f:S\longrightarrow T
is an open immersion over
S
. But it is also closed because it is a section of a separated morphism
T\longrightarrow S
, proving
S
is a connected component of
T
. In particular,
T\longrightarrow S
is étale at
u(z)
, or equivalently
u(z)\in N
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.11
.

(1) The composition
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}\subset S
is a quasi-projective étale morphism, so
Lemma

3.12
claims there exists a maximal quasi-projective étale scheme
\tilde{N}\longrightarrow S
containing
N_{0}
. Though
\tilde{N}\longrightarrow S
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
, it may be larger than the desired Néron model of
N_{0}
. To make
\tilde{N}
smaller, consider a closed subset
C_{0}=\tilde{N}_{0}\setminus N_{0}
over
S_{0}
. Take its Zariski closure
C=\overline{C_{0}}
in
\tilde{N}
and define

N=\tilde{N}\setminus C.

By construction,
N\longrightarrow S
is an open subscheme of
\tilde{N}
and an extension of
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
.

To show the Néron mapping property of
N
, let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space and
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
a morphism. Then
u_{0}
extends to
u:Z\longrightarrow\tilde{N}
by the Néron mapping property of
\tilde{N}
. It remains to prove
u(Z)\subset N
. Note that
u
is smooth because
Z\longrightarrow S
is smooth and
\tilde{N}\longrightarrow S
is étale
[
BLR90
, Lemma 2.2.9]
. Therefore
u(Z)\subset\tilde{N}
is open. Taking the Zariski closure of
C_{0}\subset\tilde{N}_{0}\setminus u_{0}(Z_{0})
in
\tilde{N}
, we obtain a sequence of inclusions (the superscript
(-)^{-}
means Zariski closure)

C\subset\big(\tilde{N}_{0}\setminus u_{0}(Z_{0})\big)^{-}=\big(\tilde{N}_{0}\setminus u(Z)\big)^{-}\subset\big(\tilde{N}\setminus u(Z)\big)^{-}=\tilde{N}\setminus u(Z).

In other words,
u(Z)\subset\tilde{N}\setminus C=N
. The Néron mapping property of
N
follows.

(2) Extend the identity map
M_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
to a morphism
M\longrightarrow N
by the Néron mapping property. Such a morphism is quasi-finite and birational to a normal Noetherian scheme
N
, so again Zariski’s main theorem concludes.
∎

3.4.
Group algebraic spaces and torsors

Néron models are mostly used for group spaces and torsors. Let us collect some results about them.

3.12
.

Let
G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
be a smooth group space and
G\longrightarrow S
its Néron model. Then

(1)

G
has a unique group structure extending that on
G_{0}
. If
G_{0}
is commutative, then so is
G
.

(2)

Let
H\longrightarrow S
be a smooth group space. Then the Néron extension of a homomorphism
f_{0}:H_{0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
to
f:H\longrightarrow G
is a homomorphism. ∎

We may therefore say “
G
is a group space Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
” for simplicity.

3.12
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a smooth group space and
H
a group space Néron under
S_{1}\subset S
. Assume that
S
is
\mathrm{(S_{2})}
and
S_{1}\subset S
has codimension
\geq 2
complement. Then

(1)

The extension of an étale homomorphism
f_{1}:G_{1}\longrightarrow H_{1}
is an étale homomorphism
f:G\longrightarrow H
.

(2)

If
G
is separated, then the extension of an open immersion homomorphism
f_{1}:G_{1}\longrightarrow H_{1}
is an open immersion homomorphism
f:G\longrightarrow H
.

Proof.

(1) The extension
f
is a homomorphism by
Section

3.4
. To show it is étale, it is enough to show

f_{*}:\Lie G\longrightarrow\Lie H

is an isomorphism, or equivalently a homomorphism
f^{*}:C_{H}\longrightarrow C_{G}
between their conormal sheaves is an isomorphism. Both
C_{G}
and
C_{H}
are locally free sheaves on an
\mathrm{(S_{2})}
scheme
S
, so Hartogs’ theorem says
f^{*}
is an isomorphism iff its restriction to
S_{1}
is. The codimension
\geq 2
assumption is necessary; see
Example

3.9
.

(2) If
G
is separated and
f_{1}
is an open immersion, then
\ker f
is an étale and separated group scheme that is trivial over
S_{1}
. Hence it is trivial by
Lemma

2.20
. Every étale monomorphism is an open immersion (Tag 025G).
∎

3.12
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a smooth group space and
N\longrightarrow S
a
G
-torsor. Then
G
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
if and only if
N
is.

Proof.

The question is étale local on the base
S
by
Section

3.1
, so we may assume
N\longrightarrow S
has a section after shrinking
S
étale locally. Then
N
is a trivial
G
-torsor, or
N\cong G
.
∎

3.12
.

Let
G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
be a smooth group space and
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
a
G_{0}
-torsor.

(1)

Assume
G\longrightarrow S
and
N\longrightarrow S
are Néron models of
G_{0}
and
N_{0}
. Then
N
is a
G
-torsor if and only if
N\longrightarrow S
is surjective.

(2)

Assume
N_{0}
has a surjective Néron model
N\longrightarrow S
. Then
G_{0}
has a Néron model
G\longrightarrow S
(and thus
N
is a
G
-torsor).

Proof.

(1) The implication
(\Longrightarrow)
is clear, so let us prove
(\Longleftarrow)
. The
G_{0}
-action
G_{0}\times_{S_{0}}N_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
uniquely extends to a
G
-action
G\times_{S}N\longrightarrow N
by the Néron mapping property. To show this defines a torsor, assume
N\longrightarrow S
admits a section
e:S\longrightarrow N
after shrinking
S
étale locally. The orbit map
\phi_{e}:G\longrightarrow N
(e.g., see §
A.2.1
) is an isomorphism over
S_{0}
since it trivializes the
G_{0}
-torsor
N_{0}
. The isomorphism extends over
S
, as both
G
and
N
are Néron models. (In fact, this argument proves
N
is a
G
-torsor over the image of
N\longrightarrow S
.)

(2) Since
N\longrightarrow S
is surjective, we can take a covering
S^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{\alpha}U_{\alpha}\longrightarrow S
in étale topology so that
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
admits a section
e:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}
. Use its restriction
e_{0}:S^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}_{0}
to trivialize the
G^{\prime}_{0}
-torsor
N^{\prime}_{0}
and yield an isomorphism of algebraic spaces
\phi_{e_{0}}:G^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow N^{\prime}_{0}
. Since
N^{\prime}_{0}
has a Néron model
N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
by
Section

3.1
,
G^{\prime}_{0}
admits the same Néron model
G^{\prime}=N^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
. Descend it to a Néron model
G\longrightarrow S
of
G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
using
Section

3.1
. As the Néron model of a group space,
G
is a group space as well.
∎

3.13
.

We do not know whether the following statement is true: let
N_{0}
be a
G_{0}
-torsor. If
G_{0}
has a Néron model
G
, then so does
N_{0}
.

3.13
.

Let
G
be a group space Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. Then the restriction map of pointed sets

H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(S,G)\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(S_{0},G)

is injective. Moreover,
[N_{0}]\in H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(S_{0},G)
is contained in its image if and only if
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
admits a surjective smooth extension
N^{a}\longrightarrow S
.

Proof.

To prove the injectiveness, let
N\longrightarrow S
and
M\longrightarrow S
be
G
-torsors whose restrictions over
S_{0}
are isomorphic. By
Section

3.4
, they are both Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
and hence isomorphic by the uniqueness of the Néron model.

For the second claim, consider the given extension
S^{\prime}\coloneq N^{a}\longrightarrow S
as a covering in smooth topology. Its restriction
S^{\prime}_{0}=N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
trivializes the torsor (by definition of torsor):

Note that
G^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}_{0}
admits a Néron model
G^{\prime}\longrightarrow S^{\prime}
. Such a Néron model descends to a Néron model
N\longrightarrow S
of
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
by
Section

3.1
. The morphism
N\longrightarrow S
is surjective by descent, so it is a
G
-torsor by
Section

3.4
.
∎

3.14
.

Let
K\longrightarrow T
be a group space Néron under
T_{0}\subset T
,
S\longrightarrow T
a smooth algebraic space, and
N\longrightarrow S
a
K
-torsor. Then
N\longrightarrow S
satisfies a “strong Néron mapping property” in the following sense:

•

For every algebraic space
Z\longrightarrow S
that is smooth over
T
, the restriction map

\operatorname{Mor}_{S}(Z,N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{S_{0}}(Z_{0},N_{0}),\qquad u\longmapsto u_{0}=u_{|S_{0}}

is bijective.

Proof.

Universal property of fiber product constructs a diagram

The base change
N_{Z}\longrightarrow Z
is a torsor under a group space
K_{Z}\longrightarrow Z
, which is Néron under
Z_{0}\subset Z
since
Z\longrightarrow T
is smooth (
Section

3.1
). Hence
N_{Z}\longrightarrow Z
is Néron under
Z_{0}\subset Z
by
Section

3.4
, making
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{Z_{0}}
uniquely extends to
u:Z\longrightarrow N_{Z}\longrightarrow N
.
∎

.

where
G\longrightarrow S
is a Néron model of
G^{a}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
that contains
G^{a}
as an open subgroup space.

Let us state two immediate corollaries of this theorem.

3.16
.

Let
H
be a group space Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. Then every open subgroup space
G_{0}\subset H_{0}
over
S_{0}
admits a Néron model
G\subset H
over
S
, an open subgroup space of
H
.

Proof.

The strict neutral component
H^{\circ\circ}\subset H
is an open subgroup space by
Theorem

2.9
. The union
G^{a}=G_{0}\cup H^{\circ\circ}\subset H
is an open subgroup space of
H
. Apply
Theorem

3.15
to
G^{a}\subset H
.
∎

3.17
.

Let
S
be a normal Noetherian scheme and
H
a group space Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. Assume that a smooth commutative group space
G^{a}\longrightarrow S
admits a quasi-projective étale homomorphism

f^{a}:G^{a}\longrightarrow H.

Then there exists a Néron model
G\longrightarrow S
of
G^{a}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
containing
G^{a}
as an open subgroup space. Moreover,
f^{a}
extends to a quasi-projective étale homomorphism
f:G\longrightarrow H
.

Proof.

Set
K^{a}=\ker f^{a}
, a quasi-projective, étale, and commutative group space over
S
. By
Theorem

3.11
,
K^{a}_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
admits a quasi-projective commutative Néron model
K\longrightarrow S
that contains
K^{a}
as an open subgroup space. Define a pushout

G^{b}=\big(G^{a}\times_{S}K\big)/K^{a}\qquad\mbox{where}\quad K^{a}\hookrightarrow G^{a}\times_{S}K\quad\mbox{by}\quad x\longmapsto(x,-x),

a smooth group space over
S
. Such a new group space sits in a commutative diagram

\hbox to151.32pt{\vbox to42.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 75.65764pt\lower-19.4811pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

Apply
Theorem

3.15
to the second row.
∎

3.18
.

If
G^{a}\longrightarrow S
is separated and of finite type (e.g., when it has connected fibers by
Theorem

2.11
), then every étale homomorphism
f^{a}:G^{a}\longrightarrow H
is automatically quasi-projective.

3.18
.

Let
G
,
H
, and
K
be smooth group spaces over
S
.

(1)

Let
1\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow H
be a left exact sequence. If
G
and
H
are Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
, then so is
K
.

(2)

Let
1\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow H\longrightarrow 1
be a short exact sequence. If
K
and
H
are Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
, then so is
G
.

Proof.

(1) is clear. (2) Let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space. Consider the restriction maps between cohomology long exact sequences (e.g.,
[
Mil80
, Proposition III.4.5]
):

The first and third vertical arrows are isomorphisms by the Néron mapping property. The last vertical arrow is an injective map of pointed sets by
Section

3.4
. The five lemma-type diagram chasing shows the second vertical arrow is an isomorphism (when group spaces are commutative, this is the actual five lemma).
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.15
.

Let us set up notations. Let
f^{a}:G^{a}\longrightarrow H
be the given homomorphism with kernel
K
and write
I^{a}=\im f^{a}
for its image, an open subgroup space of
H
. The lower index
-_{0}
will indicate the base change
-\times_{S}S_{0}
as usual. For simplicity, denote the restriction of
f^{a}:G^{a}\longrightarrow I^{a}
over
S_{0}
by
f_{0}:G_{0}\longrightarrow I_{0}
without the upper index
-^{a}
. We divide the proof into four steps.

(Step 1: constructing an algebraic space
I
) Let us first enlarge
I^{a}
to an open subspace
I\subset H
: this will be the image of the extended homomorphism
f:G\longrightarrow H
. Consider a collection of étale local sections of
H\longrightarrow S

\{s_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\longrightarrow H:s_{\alpha,0}=(s_{\alpha})_{|U_{\alpha,0}}\mbox{ lifts to }t_{\alpha,0}:U_{\alpha,0}\longrightarrow G_{0}\}.

The open subgroup space
I^{a}
acts on
H
freely by translation, so the orbit map
\phi_{s_{\alpha}}:I^{a}_{\alpha}\hookrightarrow H_{\alpha}
of
s_{\alpha}
is an open immersion of algebraic spaces over
U_{\alpha}
(§
A.2.1
). Their collection defines an étale (and hence open) morphism

(3.19)

I^{\prime}\coloneq\bigsqcup_{\alpha}I^{a}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow H\qquad\mbox{by combining}\quad\hbox to126.66pt{\vbox to18.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.33063pt\lower-7.82639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

Define an open subspace
I\subset H
by its image. That is,
I\subset H
is the “union of
I^{a}
-orbits for all
G_{0}
-liftable local sections”. It contains
I^{a}
as an open subspace because the identity section
e:S\longrightarrow H
is
G_{0}
-liftable. Since the image of
f_{0}:G_{0}\longrightarrow H_{0}
was
I_{0}
, we have
I_{|S_{0}}=I_{0}
and the notation is consistent.

(Step 2: constructing the morphism
f:G\longrightarrow I
) Recall the definition of
I^{\prime}
and the surjective étale morphism
I^{\prime}\longrightarrow I
in (
3.19
). Collect the pullbacks of
f^{a}:G^{a}\longrightarrow I^{a}
to define a morphism

f^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\coloneq\bigsqcup_{\alpha}G^{a}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow I^{\prime}=\bigsqcup_{\alpha}I^{a}_{\alpha}.

Since
f^{a}
is a
K
-torsor, so is
f^{\prime}
. In particular,
f^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\longrightarrow I^{\prime}
is Néron under
I^{\prime}_{0}\subset I^{\prime}
by
Section

3.4
. On the other hand, recall that
s_{\alpha,0}:U_{\alpha,0}\longrightarrow H_{0}
lifts to
t_{\alpha,0}:U_{\alpha,0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
. Collecting the orbit maps
\phi_{t_{\alpha}}:G^{a}_{\alpha,0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
of
t_{\alpha,0}
for each
\alpha
, we obtain a cartesian diagram

Now
Section

3.1
descends the Néron model
f^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\longrightarrow I^{\prime}
of
f^{\prime}_{0}:G^{\prime}_{0}\longrightarrow I^{\prime}_{0}
to the Néron model
f:G\longrightarrow I
of
f_{0}:G_{0}\longrightarrow I_{0}
. Note that
f
is a
K
-torsor.

(Step 3: Néron mapping property of
G\longrightarrow S
) At this point, we have an algebraic space
f:G\longrightarrow I
, which is both a
K
-torsor and an extension of the given homomorphism
f_{0}:G_{0}\longrightarrow I_{0}
. Let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space and
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
an arbitrary morphism. The following diagram will be useful:

The composition
Z_{0}\longrightarrow H_{0}
uniquely extends to a morphism
Z\longrightarrow H
by the Néron mapping property of
H
. Let us show that this morphism factors though
I\subset H
. Given an arbitrary closed point
z\in Z_{s}
of a fiber over
s\in S
, fix an étale local section
U_{\alpha}\longrightarrow Z
passing through
z
. Then the composition
s_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\longrightarrow H
is a section of
H
that lifts to a section of
G_{0}
, so by construction in Step 1, it factors through
I
. We have thus constructed a morphism
Z\longrightarrow I
and a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{21.42914pt}{-1.75pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 29.65 -2.42)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Lemma

3.14
concludes that
u_{0}
uniquely extends to a morphism
u:Z\longrightarrow G
(Note: though
f:G\longrightarrow I
is a Néron model, the morphism
Z\longrightarrow I
may not be smooth so this does not follow directly from the Néron mapping property of
f
). This proves the Néron mapping property of
G\longrightarrow S
.

(Step 4: completion of the proof) Now that we know the Néron mapping property of
G
, its group structure is uniquely extended from that of
G_{0}=G^{a}_{0}
. The given homomorphism
f_{0}:G_{0}\longrightarrow H_{0}
extends to a homomorphism
G\longrightarrow H
and coincides with the previous morphism
f:G\longrightarrow I\subset H
by the uniqueness of the Néron mapping property of
H
. This a posteriori shows that
I
is a subgroup space of
H
. Finally, since
f
was a
K
-torsor, it has the kernel
K
as a homomorphism of group spaces.
∎

3.6.
Weak Néron models

A weaker version of the Néron mapping property can be still interesting to some extent. In this subsection, we generalize the notion of a weak Néron model in
[
BLR90
, §3.5]
to higher-dimensional bases.

3.6.1.
Weak Néron models

The notion of a weak Néron model over a Dedekind scheme is introduced in Definition 1.1.1, Definition 3.5.1 and Proposition 3.5.6 in
[
BLR90
]
. Moreover, Theorem 7.1.1 in loc. cit. shows the equivalence between weak Néron models and Néron models for nice group schemes.

3.20
Weak Néron model.

Under the same assumption in
Definition

3.1
, an extension
N\longrightarrow S
of
N_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is called a
weak Néron extension
or
weak Néron model
if it satisfies the axiom (1) together with:

(2w)

(Weak Néron mapping property) For every étale algebraic space
U\longrightarrow S
, the restriction map

\operatorname{Mor}_{S}(U,N)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Mor}_{S_{0}}(U_{0},N_{0}),\qquad u\longmapsto u_{0}=u_{|S_{0}}

is bijective.

3.21
.

(1)

It is enough to check the weak Néron mapping property axiom for separated, finite type, and étale morphisms
U\longrightarrow S
(similar to
Remark

3.2
). When
S
is Noetherian, we may assume
U\longrightarrow S
is quasi-projective étale (
Lemma

3.10
).

(2)

Weak Néron model in the sense of
[
BLR90
, Definition 3.5.1, Proposition 3.5.6]
is a finite type weak Néron model over a Dedekind scheme in the sense of
Definition

3.20
. Separatedness automatically follows from
Corollary

3.25
and hence it is a scheme by
Theorem

2.3
. The weak Néron mapping property axiom in
Definition

3.20
is called the
extension property for étale points (morphisms)
in Definition 1.1.1 of the book. The weak Néron mapping property in the book has a different meaning (in Proposition 3.5.3) but we will not use this notion in this article.

The following is the main result in this subsection, generalizing
[
BLR90
, Theorem 7.1.1]
to higher-dimensional bases. The rest of this subsection will be devoted to its proof.

3.22
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a smooth group space of finite type over a normal scheme
S
. Then
G
is weakly Néron if and only if it is Néron (under
S_{0}\subset S
).

The finite type assumption seems to be necessary; see the paragraph after Proposition 10.1.2 in
[
BLR90
, p.290]
. Let us start with basic properties of weak Néron models. Since Néron models are weak Néron models, these results apply to Néron models as well.

To start,
Section

3.1
and its proof holds verbatim but in étale topology instead of smooth topology; we will not repeat the statement. Hence the construction of the weak Néron model is an étale local property on the base.

3.23
.

Let
N
be weakly Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. Then
N
satisfies the uniqueness part of the Néron mapping property.

Proof.

Let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space and
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
a morphism over
S_{0}
. Assume
u_{0}
admits two extensions
u,v:Z\longrightarrow N
. Let us first prove
u=v
pointwise on
Z
. Assume on the contrary that there exists a closed point
z\in Z
with
u(z)\neq v(z)
. Take an étale local section
s:U\longrightarrow Z
passing through
z
. Then
u\circ s,v\circ s:U\longrightarrow N
defines two different extensions of
u_{0}\circ s_{0}:U_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
, violating the weak Néron mapping property.

Pulling back the diagonal map
N\longrightarrow N\times_{S}N
by
(u,v):Z\longrightarrow N\times_{S}N
defines a locally closed subspace of
Z
(recall that
N\longrightarrow S
is locally separated). Since it is set-theoretically equal to
Z
by the first paragraph, it is a closed subscheme of
Z
. But it contains a scheme-theoretically dense open subspace
Z_{0}\subset Z
, proving it is an equality. This proves
u=v
as morphisms.
∎

Lemma

3.23
shows that only the existence part of the Néron mapping property is problematic for weak Néron models. Moreover, the lemma provides an analogue statement of
Lemma

A.7
. That is, if
u:Z\dashrightarrow N
is a rational map from a smooth algebraic space to a weak Néron model that is defined over
S_{0}
, then there exists a maximal open subspace
\operatorname{Dom}(u)\subset Z
, the
domain of definition
of
u
, such that
u
is represented by a morphism
u:\Dom(u)\longrightarrow N
.

3.24
.

Let
N\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

N
is Néron (resp. weakly Néron) under
S_{0}\subset S
.

(2)

For each point
s\in S
, the base change to a local scheme

N_{(s)}\longrightarrow S_{(s)}=\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,s}

is Néron (resp. weakly Néron) under
S_{(s)}\times_{S}S_{0}\subset S_{(s)}
.

Proof.

Same proof of
[
BLR90
, Proposition 1.2.4]
applies. Use
Remark

3.2
(resp.
3.21
) to assume the test space
Z\longrightarrow S
is of finite type. Only the local finite type assumption of
N\longrightarrow S
is needed to apply
[
EGA $IV˙3$
, Theorem 8.8.2]
.
∎

3.24
.

Let
G
be a group space weakly Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. If
H\subset G
is an aligned subgroup such that
H_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is separated, then
H\longrightarrow S
is separated.

Proof.

Let
E_{H}\subset H
be the scheme-theoretic image of the identity section of
H
, which is étale over
S
by the alignment of
H
. Let
x\in E_{H}
be a closed point. Shrinking
S
étale locally, we may choose a section
s:S\longrightarrow E_{H}
passing through
x
. Since
H
is separated over
S_{0}
whence
s
is trivial over
S_{0}
, the weak Néron mapping property of
G
forces
s=e
.
∎

The following explains the separatedness assumption in the (weak) Néron lft-model in the sense of
[
BLR90
, §10]
is redundant, as observed in
[
Hol+23
, §1.1]
.

3.25
.

Let
\Delta
be a Dedekind scheme and
G_{0}\longrightarrow\Delta_{0}
a smooth separated group space. Then its weak Néron model
G\longrightarrow\Delta
is separated.

Proof.

Combine
Section

2.3
and
Section

3.6.1
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.22
.

Assume
G\longrightarrow S
is weakly Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. Let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space and
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
a morphism over
S_{0}
. The extension
u:Z\longrightarrow G
is unique by
Lemma

3.23
, so we need to show its existence. Taking a covering
\bigsqcup_{i}Z_{i}\longrightarrow Z
in étale topology where each
Z_{i}\longrightarrow S
is a smooth affine scheme of finite type and with irreducible (or empty) fibers, we may reduce to the case where
Z\longrightarrow S
is smooth, of finite type, and has irreducible fibers. Moreover, shrinking
S
étale locally, we may assume
S
is Noetherian and
Z\longrightarrow S
has a section
e:S\longrightarrow Z
.

Let
\eta\in S\setminus S_{0}
be a codimension
1
point and
\Delta=\Spec\mathcal{O}_{S,\eta}\longrightarrow S
a flat trait. The base change
G_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is a weak Néron model of finite type over a Dedekind scheme by
Lemma

3.24
. Its generic fiber
G_{K}\longrightarrow\Delta_{0}=\Spec K
is an algebraic group so it is separated. Therefore,
G_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is separated by
Corollary

3.25
and
G_{\Delta}
is a scheme by
Theorem

2.3
. We may thus apply
[
BLR90
, Theorem 7.1.1]
to conclude that
G_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is a Néron model. This means the rational map
u_{\Delta}:Z_{\Delta}\dashrightarrow G_{\Delta}
extends to a morphism. By limit argument (Lemma 1.2.5 in loc. cit. or more generally
[
EGA $IV˙3$
, Theorem 8.8.2]
), the morphism
u_{\Delta}
is represented by
u_{U}:Z_{U}\longrightarrow G_{U}
for an open subscheme
U\subset S
, i.e., the rational map
u:Z\dashrightarrow G
is defined over a Zariski neighborhood
U\subset S
containing
\eta
. Taking union for every codimension
1
point
\eta\in S
, this shows
u_{0}
extends to a morphism
u_{1}:Z_{1}\longrightarrow G_{1}
over a dense open subscheme
S_{1}\subset S
with codimension
\geq 2
complement.

The section
e:S\longrightarrow Z
composed with
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
defines an
S_{0}
-section
\varepsilon_{0}:S_{0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
, which extends to an
S
-section
\varepsilon:S\longrightarrow G
by the weak Néron mapping property. By
Lemma

3.26
below,
u:Z\dashrightarrow G
is defined along
e:S\longrightarrow Z
. Since
Z\longrightarrow S
has connected fibers,
u
is an
S
-rational map
in the sense of §2.5 in loc. cit. By the theorem of Weil (Theorem 4.4.1 in loc. cit.),
u
is defined everywhere and extends to a morphism
u:Z\longrightarrow G
.
∎

3.26
[
Ray70
, Proposition IX.1.1]
.

Let
Z\longrightarrow S
and
N\longrightarrow S
be algebraic spaces over an
\mathrm{(S_{2})}
scheme
S
together with sections
e:S\longrightarrow Z
and
\varepsilon:S\longrightarrow N
. Assume
Z\longrightarrow S
is smooth and has connected fibers, and
N\longrightarrow S
is either

(i)

separated; or

(ii)

weakly Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
.

Let
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow N_{0}
be a morphism over
S_{0}
and consider it as a rational map
u:Z\dashrightarrow N
over
S
. Assume

(a)

There exists a dense open subscheme
S_{0}\subset S_{1}\subset S
with codimension
\geq 2
complement such that
e(S_{1})\subset\Dom(u)
.

(b)

u(e(S_{1}))\subset\varepsilon(S)
.

Then
e(S)\subset\Dom(u)
.

Proof.

We simply observe that our lemma is a special case of Raynaud’s original statement. Since
S
is
\mathrm{(S_{2})}
, the codimension
\geq 2
complement
S_{1}
contains all depth
\leq 1
points, which is Raynaud’s original assumption. Raynaud also required the following more technical assumption
\mathrm{(b^{\prime})}
: for all
z\in e(S)\cap\Dom(u)
, there exists a representative
u^{V}:V\longrightarrow N
of
u
defined on a Zariski open neighborhood
z\in V\subset\Dom(u)
such that
u^{V}(e(S)\cap V)\subset\varepsilon(S)
. Our assumption (b) implies
\mathrm{(b^{\prime})}
by
Lemma

A.7
in case (i) and
Lemma

3.23
in case (ii).
∎

3.6.2.
Weak Néron models with free actions

It is possible to prove a variant of
Theorem

3.22
where the assumption on a group space is weakened to that on an algebraic space with free group action. The following is the main result of this subsection.

3.27
.

Let
N\longrightarrow S
be a smooth, surjective, and finite type algebraic space over a normal scheme
S
. Assume the following:

(i)

There exists a smooth group space
G^{a}\longrightarrow S
acting on
N
over
S
.

(ii)

The action is free over
S
and transitive over
S_{0}
.

Then
N
is weakly Néron if and only if it is Néron (under
S_{0}\subset S
).

Proof.

Replacing
S
by its covering étale topology (weak Néron model version of
Section

3.1
), we may assume
N\longrightarrow S
has a section. Such a section trivializes the
G^{a}_{0}
-torsor
N_{0}
, so we may assume
N_{0}=G^{a}_{0}
and write
G=N
for notational simplicity. Our goal is to show
G
is Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
. Thanks to
Theorem

3.22
, it is enough to show
G\longrightarrow S
is a group space.

The identity section
e_{0}:S_{0}\longrightarrow G^{a}_{0}
uniquely extends to
e:S\longrightarrow G
by the weak Néron mapping property of
G
. Let us define the group operation of
G
. Since the
G^{a}
-action on
G
is free, every étale local section
s:U\longrightarrow G
has an open immersion orbit map
\phi_{s}:G^{a}_{U}\hookrightarrow G_{U}
. Collect sufficiently many étale local sections to obtain a covering in étale topology

G^{\prime}\coloneq\bigsqcup_{\alpha}G^{a}_{\alpha}\longrightarrow G,\qquad g^{a}\longmapsto g^{a}.s_{\alpha}.

Set
G^{\prime\prime}=G^{\prime}\times_{G}G^{\prime}
and write
p_{1},p_{2}:G^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow G^{\prime}
the two projections. Define a morphism

m^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G,\qquad(g^{a}_{\alpha},h)\longmapsto g^{a}_{\alpha}.(s_{\alpha}\cdot h).

Since
G^{a}_{0}=G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
is a group space, the morphism
m^{\prime}_{0}
over
S_{0}
is nothing but the base change of
m_{0}:G_{0}\times_{S}G_{0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
under
G^{\prime}\longrightarrow G
. That is,
m^{\prime}
satisfies the trivial descent condition

m^{\prime}\circ(p_{1}\times\id)=m^{\prime}\circ(p_{2}\times\id):G^{\prime\prime}\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G

over
S_{0}
. Now
G\longrightarrow S
is weakly Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
, so it satisfies the
uniqueness part
of the Néron mapping property by
Lemma

3.23
. This forces
m^{\prime}\circ(p_{1}\times\id)=m^{\prime}\circ(p_{2}\times\id)
over
S
. Descending
m^{\prime}
, we obtain a desired multiplication morphism
m:G\times_{S}G\longrightarrow G
.

The inverse morphism
G\longrightarrow G
is constructed similarly. First, construct
s^{-1}_{\alpha}
by taking the inverse of
s_{\alpha,0}
over
S_{0}
and then extending it using the weak Néron mapping property. Define a morphism
i^{\prime}:G^{\prime}\longrightarrow G
by
g^{a}_{\alpha}\longmapsto s_{\alpha}^{-1}\cdot(g^{a}_{\alpha})^{-1}
using the multiplication morphism defined above and finally descend. The group axioms are verified by the uniqueness of the Néron mapping property.
∎

Let us end this section with the following lemma, which will be used in the future argument. It is a variant of
Section

3.4
.

3.28
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
be a group space Néron under
S_{0}\subset S
and
G^{a}\subset G
an open subgroup space with
G^{a}_{0}=G_{0}
. Let
Z\longrightarrow S
be a smooth algebraic space extension of
G_{0}\longrightarrow S_{0}
that admits a free
G^{a}
-action. Then

(1)

The isomorphism
u_{0}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow G_{0}
extends to a
G^{a}
-equivariant étale morphism
u:Z\longrightarrow G
.

(2)

If we further assume
S
is normal and
Z\longrightarrow S
is separated and of finite type, then the extension
u:Z\longrightarrow G
is an open immersion.

Proof.

(1) The equivariance of the extended morphism
u:Z\longrightarrow G
is from the Néron mapping property. For each closed point
z\in Z
, fix a section
e:S\longrightarrow Z
passing through
z
after shrinking
S
étale locally. The
G^{a}
-equivariance of
u
implies the orbit maps of the sections
e
and
u\circ e
commute:

Since the
G^{a}
-action on
Z
and
G
are free, both
\phi_{e}
and
\phi_{u\circ e}
are open immersions. This proves
u
is étale at
z
.

(2) The previous argument shows
u:Z\longrightarrow G
is étale and birational. The image
u(Z)\subset G
is an open subspace since
u
is étale. We claim
u:Z\longrightarrow u(Z)
is an isomorphism. Shrinking, we may assume
S
is normal Noetherian. Now
u:Z\longrightarrow u(Z)
is quasi-finite separated and
u(Z)
is normal Noetherian, so Zariski’s main theorem concludes that the birational map
u:Z\longrightarrow u(Z)
is an isomorphism.
∎

4.
The
\delta
-regular action on a Lagrangian fibration

Let us from now on exclusively focus on the study of Lagrangian fibrations of symplectic varieties. We will work over
\mathbb{C}
. In this first section, we construct a
\delta
-regular action on the Lagrangian fibration. Results in §
2
will be used frequently but results in §
3
will not be used in this section.

Given a morphism
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
, recall that a (relative) automorphism
f
of
X
over
B
is an automorphism
f:X\longrightarrow X
respecting
\pi
, or
\pi=\pi\circ f
.

4.1
.

Let
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
be a flat morphism with a dense open subscheme
B_{0}\subset B
over which
\pi
has abelian variety fibers. A
translation automorphism
of
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
is an automorphism
f
of
X
over
B
such that for every point
b\in B_{0}
, the automorphism of the fiber
f_{b}:X_{b}\longrightarrow X_{b}
is a translation of an abelian variety.

Here is how we interpret translation automorphisms: if
f
is a translation automorphism and
X_{b}
is a singular fiber, then the automorphism
f_{b}:X_{b}\longrightarrow X_{b}
is a limit of translation automorphisms on nearby abelian variety fibers. We can now state the first main theorem of this section.

4.2
.

Let
X
be a smooth symplectic variety and
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
a projective Lagrangian fibration to a smooth variety
B
. Assume

(4.3)

\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\coloneq X\setminus\Sing(\pi)\longrightarrow B

is surjective. Then there exists a smooth, commutative, and quasi-projective group scheme
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
such that for every étale morphism
U\longrightarrow B
, we have

(4.4)

P^{a}(U)=\{f:X_{U}\longrightarrow X_{U}:f\mbox{ is a translation automorphism over }U\}.

The group scheme
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
is an extension of an abelian scheme
P_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
and acts faithfully on
X
over
B
.

4.5
.

\pi^{\prime}
in (
4.3
) is surjective if and only if every fiber of
\pi
has at least one reduced irreducible component. Since
\pi
is flat, shrinking
B
to a Zariski open subset
\pi(X^{\prime})
will always make
\pi^{\prime}
surjective.

4.6
.

We call the group scheme
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
in
Theorem

4.2
the
translation automorphism scheme
of
\pi
.

It is easy to generalize (
4.4
) to every flat and locally finite type morphism
U\longrightarrow B
. However, we caution the reader that this is not the case for an arbitrary morphism
U\longrightarrow B
. For example, if
\{b\}\hookrightarrow B
is a closed point over which the fiber
X_{b}
singular, then
X_{b}\longrightarrow\{b\}
has no nearby abelian variety fibers so the notion of translation automorphism does not make sense.

Under a more restrictive assumption that every fiber of
\pi
is integral, the theorem was proved in
[
AF16
, Theorem 2]
. Their idea was to construct
P^{a}
as a locally closed subgroup scheme of the relative automorphism scheme
\Aut_{\pi}
. We will follow their idea of constructing
P^{a}
in
\Aut_{\pi}
but use different techniques to realize this:
P^{a}
will be the equidimensional locus of the main component of
\Aut_{\pi}
(§
2.5
).

The
P^{a}
-action on
X
was faithful, so
(X,P^{a})
automatically satisfies the axioms of weak abelian fibration (§
A.2.2
). It is also
\delta
-regular.

4.7
.

Notations as in
Theorem

4.2
. Then the translation automorphism scheme
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
is
\delta
-regular and the Lagrangian fibration
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
is a
\delta
-regular abelian fibration in the sense of Ngô.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of these two theorems.

4.1.
Smooth subalgebra of the automorphism Lie algebra

Let
X
be a smooth symplectic variety and
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
its Lagrangian fibration to a smooth variety
B
. The Lagrangian fibration
\pi
is flat because it is equidimensional (use
[
Mat00
, Theorem 1]
an miracle flatness). Consider the cotangent bundle

\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}=\Spec_{B}\big(\Sym^{*}T_{B}\big)\longrightarrow B.

Over the complement of the discriminant locus
B_{0}\subset B
, there is a well-known isomorphism

\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B_{0}}\longrightarrow\Lie(\Aut_{\pi_{0}})

induced from the symplectic form
\sigma
. The goal of this subsection is to extend this map over the entire base
B
. It is an infinitesimal version of
Theorem

4.2
and is a generalization of
[
AF16
, Lemma 8.4(i)]
.

To state the theorem, consider the relative automorphism scheme
\Aut_{\pi}\longrightarrow B
of the flat proper morphism
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
(§
2.6.1
), which is a separated group scheme over
B
. Its Lie algebra scheme is a scheme
\Lie(\Aut_{\pi})\longrightarrow B
representing its Lie algebra sheaf (§
2.1
). The
main component
of
\Lie(\Aut_{\pi})
is a Zariski closure of the irreducible component containing its zero section (§
2.5
).

4.8
.

The main component of the Lie algebra scheme of
\Aut_{\pi}
is the cotangent bundle of
B
. More precisely, the symplectic form
\sigma
induces an injective Lie algebra scheme homomorphism

(4.9)

\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}\hookrightarrow\Lie\big(\Aut_{\pi}\big),

which is an isomorphism over
B_{0}
.

4.10
.

Theorem

4.8
does not need the surjectivity assumption of
\pi^{\prime}
in (
4.3
).

To tackle the theorem, we first consider the following commutative diagram of coherent sheaves on
X
with exact rows and columns. This diagram has appeared several times in literature when
X
is a compact hyper-Kähler manifold (e.g.,
[
Mat05
]
,
[
Saw08
]
, and
[
Leh11
,
Leh16
]
) but it also holds in our generality.

(4.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}\Omega_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\pi}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi^{*}T_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{1}_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{1}_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

The first row of the diagram is the cotangent sequence of
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
, which is injective since it is generically injective and
\pi^{*}\Omega_{B}
is locally free. The second row is the coherent sheaf dual of the first using the notation

\Omega_{\pi}^{\vee}=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X}),\qquad T^{1}_{\pi}=\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X}).

The second column is an isomorphism induced from the symplectic form
\sigma
. Since
\pi
is Lagrangian,
\sigma:\Omega_{X}^{\otimes 2}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
vanishes on the subsheaf
\pi^{*}\Omega_{B}^{\otimes 2}
and induces a homomorphism
\Omega_{\pi}\longrightarrow\pi^{*}T_{B}
in the third column. Its cokernel is isomorphic to
T^{1}_{\pi}
by the snake lemma. Its kernel
\tau
is the torsion part of
\Omega_{\pi}
and is interesting in its own right, but we will not need this in our discussion. Finally, the first column is induced by diagram chasing; it is injective and has cokernel
\tau
.

Before using the diagram, let us prove the following lemma.

4.12
.

The adjunction map
\mathcal{O}_{B}^{\fppf}\longrightarrow\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\fppf}
is an isomorphism of fppf
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-modules.
3
3

3

In other words,
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\mathcal{O}_{B}
universally, or
\pi
is cohomologically flat in dimension
0
.

Proof.

Consider an affine test scheme
f:S=\Spec A\longrightarrow B
and cartesian diagram
