We need to show a bijection
H^{0}(S,\mathcal{O}_{S})=H^{0}(X_{S},\mathcal{O}_{X_{S}})
or a coherent sheaf isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{S}=\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{S}}
. Since
\pi
is flat, cohomology base change theorem for quasi-coherent sheaves (Tag 08IB) claims the adjunction map

Lf^{*}(R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})\longrightarrow R\pi_{*}(Lf^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X})

is a quasi-isomorphism. In the case of Lagrangian fibrations, we further have
R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{n}\Omega_{B}^{k}[-k]
by Matsushita’s theorem in
[
Mat05
, Theorem 1.3]
and
[
Sch23
, §4]
. The
0
-th cohomology of the quasi-isomorphism is the desired isomorphism.
∎

Consider the third column
\Omega_{\pi}\longrightarrow\pi^{*}T_{B}\longrightarrow T^{1}_{\pi}\longrightarrow 0
of (
4.11
). Apply the fppf dual functor
\Hom^{\fppf}(-,\mathcal{O}_{X})
and subsequently
\pi_{*}
to obtain a left exact sequence of fppf
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-modules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{

Notice that we need
Lemma

4.12
to apply the projection formula
\Omega_{B}^{\fppf}=\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\Omega_{B}^{\fppf}
. Since
\Omega_{B}^{\fppf}
and
\pi_{*}T_{\pi}^{\fppf}
are represented by linear schemes
\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}
and
\Lie\Aut_{\pi}
((
A.19
) and
Theorem

2.31
), the latter homomorphism
\sigma
is the desired homomorphism in
Theorem

4.8
: the content of the theorem is the vanishing of the fppf
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-module
\pi_{*}\Hom^{\fppf}(T_{\pi}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{X})
. This question is equivalent to the vanishing of a higher direct image coherent sheaf by the following.

4.13
.

The fppf
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-module
\pi_{*}\Hom^{\fppf}(T_{\pi}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{X})
vanishes if and only if the coherent sheaf
R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}
on
B
vanishes.

Proof.

The sheaf
\pi_{*}\Hom^{\fppf}(T_{\pi}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{X})
, as the kernel of a linear scheme homomorphism (
4.9
), is representable by a linear scheme over
B
(
Section

A.1.6
) and thus its vanishing can be checked pointwise over closed points of
B
by Nakayama’s lemma. Over a closed point
b\in B
, we have

\big(\pi_{*}\Hom^{\fppf}(T^{1}_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X})\big)(b)=\Hom^{\fppf}(T^{1}_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X})(X_{b})=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{X_{b}}}(T^{1}_{\pi|b},\mathcal{O}_{X_{b}}).

Since
\pi_{b}:X_{b}\longrightarrow\{b\}
is an lci scheme with trivial canonical bundle (by adjunction formula), its dualizing complex is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{X_{b}}[n]
. Combining the Serre duality, cohomology base change (Tag 08IB), and convergence of Grothendieck spectral sequences, we have

\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{X_{b}}}(T^{1}_{\pi|b},\mathcal{O}_{X_{b}})=H^{n}(X_{b},T^{1}_{\pi|b})^{\vee}=\big(R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{B}}k(b)\big)^{\vee}.

Again by Nakayama’s lemma,
R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{B}}k(b)=0
for all closed points
b\in B
if and only if
R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}=0
. This completes the proof.
∎

Therefore,
Theorem

4.8
boils down to showing the vanishing of the higher direct image coherent sheaf
R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}
. We prove this using the symmetry property of the decomposition theorem in
[
Sch23
]
.

4.1.1.
Vanishing of the higher direct image

Our goal is reduced to showing the vanishing of the coherent sheaf
R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}
. We use Hodge modules to achieve this.

4.13
.

Let
T^{1}_{\pi}=\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X})
be a coherent sheaf on
X
. Then

R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}=0.

Denote the (coherent sheaf) Verdier dual functors of the smooth varieties
X
and
B
by

\mathbb{D}_{X}=R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(-,\omega_{X}[2n]),\qquad\mathbb{D}_{B}=R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{O}_{B}}(-,\omega_{B}[n]).

The symplectic structure on
X
gives an isomorphism
\omega_{X}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

4.14
.

The desired higher direct image sheaf lies in a four-term exact sequence of coherent sheaves on
B

(4.15)

\begin{multlined}\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\Big[\mathbb{D}_{B}\big(R\pi_{*}\Omega_{X}[2n-1]\big)\Big]\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{n}\Big[\mathbb{D}_{B}(R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}[2n]\otimes\Omega_{B})\Big]\qquad\\
\longrightarrow R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{n}\Big[\mathbb{D}_{B}\big(R\pi_{*}\Omega_{X}[2n-1]\big)\Big].\end{multlined}

Proof.

We first claim there exists an isomorphism

R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}\ \cong\ \mathcal{H}^{-n+1}\big[\mathbb{D}_{B}R\pi_{*}\Omega_{\pi}\big].

To prove this, recall from (
4.11
) that
\Omega_{\pi}
had a length
1
locally free resolution
\pi^{*}\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X}
. Hence from the convergence of the spectral sequence we have

\displaystyle R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}

\displaystyle=R^{n}\pi_{*}\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{n+1}\big[R\pi_{*}R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X})\big]\quad

(spectral sequence)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{-n+1}\big[R\pi_{*}\mathbb{D}_{X}\Omega_{\pi}\big]\quad

\displaystyle(\omega_{X}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{O}_{X}[2n])

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{-n+1}\big[\mathbb{D}_{B}R\pi_{*}\Omega_{\pi}\big]\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{(Grothendieck duality)}.

Again, start from the short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\pi^{*}\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\pi}\longrightarrow 0
. Applying
\mathbb{D}_{B}\circ R\pi_{*}
, we obtain an exact triangle

\hbox to283.76pt{\vbox to19.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 141.8798pt\lower-9.65973pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{

\lxSVG@closescope {}{

{{{{{}}{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{112.20625pt}{2.68611pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 155.26 3.72)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The desired sequence (
4.15
) is its cohomology long exact sequence.
∎

To understand the exact sequence (
4.15
), let us use some facts on the decomposition theorem for
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
in
[
SY22
]
and
[
Sch23
]
. We do not need the full strength of their results, so let us summarize only the necessary parts following
[
Sch23
, §1.2]
. The decomposition theorem for
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
is an isomorphism in the bounded derived category of Hodge modules on
B

(4.16)

R\pi_{*}\Big(\mathbb{Q}_{X}(n)[2n]\Big)\cong\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}P_{i}[-i],

where
P_{i}
is a Hodge module of pure weight
i
for
i=-n,\cdots,n
. The bound on the index
i
is determined by the defect of semismallness
\dim X\times_{B}X-\dim X=n
. The weight
i
Hodge module
P_{i}
contains a filtered (regular holonomic)
D
-module
(\mathcal{P}_{i},F_{\bullet}\mathcal{P}_{i})
in its datum, so we may consider its associated filtered de Rham complex

F_{k}\operatorname{DR}(\mathcal{P}_{i})=\Big[F_{k-n}\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes\bigwedge^{n}T_{B}\longrightarrow\ \cdots\ \longrightarrow F_{k-1}\mathcal{P}_{i}\otimes T_{B}\longrightarrow F_{k}\mathcal{P}_{i}\Big],

that lives in
D^{b}_{coh}(B)
and is concentrated in degree
[-n,0]
.

4.17
.

Following
[
Sch23
, §1.2]
, we denote the
(-k)
-th graded associated piece of the filtered de Rham complex
(\operatorname{DR}(\mathcal{P}_{i}),\,F_{\bullet}\operatorname{DR}(\mathcal{P}_{i}))
, up to degree shift, by

G_{i,k}=\operatorname{gr}_{-k}^{F}\operatorname{DR}(\mathcal{P}_{i})[-i].

The isomorphism (
4.16
) induces an isomorphism of filtered
D
-modules. Applying the functor
\operatorname{gr}^{F}_{-k}\operatorname{DR}
to this filtered
D
-module isomorphism yields an isomorphism in
D^{b}_{coh}(B)
:

(4.18)

R\pi_{*}\Omega_{X}^{n+k}[n-k]\cong\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}G_{i,k}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad k=-n,\cdots,n.

The following is originally proved in
[
Mat05
, Theorem 1.3]
and later revisited in
[
Sch23
, §4]
.

4.19
Matsushita, Schnell.

We have isomorphisms

G_{i,n}\cong\begin{cases}\Omega_{B}^{i}[-i]\quad&\mbox{for}\quad i\geq 0\\
0&\mbox{for}\quad i<0\end{cases}.

In other words, the isomorphism (
4.18
) for
k=n
is

R\pi_{*}\omega_{X}\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\Omega_{B}^{i}[-i].

We can now start calculating the sequence (
4.15
) of interest.

4.20
.

The sequence (
4.15
) up to isomorphism is computed as follows.

(1)

The first term of the sequence is

\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\Big(G_{n-1,n-1}\oplus G_{n,n-1}\Big)\cong\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\Big(G_{n-1,n-1}\Big)\oplus\Omega_{B}^{n-1}.

(2)

The second term of the sequence is

\mathcal{H}^{n}\Big(G_{n,n}\otimes T_{B}\Big)\cong\Omega_{B}^{n-1}.

(3)

The homomorphism between the first two terms, with respect to the above isomorphisms, contains the identity map
\Omega_{B}^{n-1}\longrightarrow\Omega_{B}^{n-1}
. In particular, it is surjective.

(4)

The last term of the sequence vanishes.

Proof of
Section

4.1.1
.

From
Lemma

4.20
, the first homomorphism in (
4.15
) is surjective and the fourth term vanishes. This proves the third term
R^{n}\pi_{*}T^{1}_{\pi}
vanishes as well (and completes the proof of
Theorem

4.8
).
∎

Proof of
Lemma

4.20
.

(1) and (4) Consider a sequence of isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{p}\Big[\mathbb{D}_{B}\big(R\pi_{*}\Omega_{X}[2n-1]\big)\Big]

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{H}^{p}\Big[\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}\mathbb{D}_{B}G_{i,-n+1}\Big]

(by (
4.18
))

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{H}^{p}\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}G_{i,n-1}

\displaystyle\mbox{(by \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{sch23}{}{}, (43.1)]})}.

By
[
Sch23
, Lemma 44]
, the complex
G_{i,k}
is concentrated in degrees
\leq\min(i,k,i+k)
, so none of the
G_{i,n-1}
’s can have a nontrivial
n
-th cohomology. This proves (4). For (1), set
p=n-1
so that the only terms with potentially nonvanishing
(n-1)
-th cohomology are
G_{n-1,n-1}
and
G_{n,n-1}
. The latter cohomology is

\mathcal{H}^{n-1}G_{n,n-1}\cong\Omega^{n-1}_{B}

from either an explicit computation as in (3) below, or more conveniently from
Theorem

4.19
together with the main result of Schnell in
[
Sch23
, Theorem 24]
.

(2) Recall again from (
4.18
) that we have

R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}[2n]\cong\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}G_{i,-n},\qquad G_{i,-n}\cong\Omega_{B}^{n+i}[n+i].

We therefore get

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}[2n]\otimes\Omega_{B},\omega_{B}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle\cong\

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}G_{i,-n}\otimes\Omega_{B},\omega_{B}^{\bullet})=\mathcal{H}^{n}\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(G_{i,-n}\otimes\Omega_{B},\omega_{B}^{\bullet})

\displaystyle\cong\

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\Omega_{B}^{n+i},\Omega_{B}^{n-1})[-i]=R\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}(\mathcal{O}_{B},\Omega_{B}^{n-1})=\Omega_{B}^{n-1}.

(3) To analyze the homomorphism, we need to be more careful about applying the isomosphisms above. Recall how we have defined the homomorphism. We considered a map
\pi^{*}\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X}
and applied the functor
R\pi_{*}
to get

(4.21)

R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow R\pi_{*}\Omega_{X}.

This homomorphism is the
\Omega_{B}
-contraction homomorphism from the filtered
D
-module structure on
\pi_{+}(\omega_{X},F_{\bullet}\omega_{X})
(e.g.,
[
Sch23
, (14)]
). As a result, the
\Omega_{B}
-contraction respects the decomposition (
4.18
) and acts componentwise:

G_{i,-n}\otimes\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow G_{i,-n+1}[1].

The map (
4.21
) can be decomposed as a direct sum of these maps for
i=-n,\cdots,n
together with an isomorphism (
4.18
).

If we set
i=-n
, then we can explicitly calculate this map
G_{-n,-n}\otimes\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow G_{-n,-n+1}[1]
. Since the fibers of
\pi
are all connected, standard argument in decomposition theorem implies that the lowest weight part of the decomposition theorem
R\pi_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{X}(n)[2n]\cong\bigoplus_{i=-n}^{n}P_{i}[-i]
is

P_{-n}=\mathbb{Q}_{B}(n)[n],

the weight
-n
Hodge module associated to the trivial VHS
\mathbb{Q}_{B}(n)
on
B
. Its filtered
D
-module part
(\mathcal{P}_{-n},F_{\bullet}\mathcal{P}_{-n})
is described by

\mathcal{P}_{-n}=\omega_{B},\qquad\cdots=F_{-2}=F_{-1}=0,\qquad F_{0}=F_{1}=\cdots=\omega_{B}.

Hence one can explicitly compute the gradings on the de Rham complex
\operatorname{DR}(\mathcal{P}_{-n})
as

G_{-n,-n}=\mathcal{O}_{B}[2n],\qquad G_{-n,-n+1}=\Omega_{B}[2n-1].

The homomorpihsm
G_{-n,-n}\otimes\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow G_{-n,-n+1}[1]
is the contraction homomorphism coming from the
D
-module structure, which in this case is an identity
\mathcal{O}_{B}[2n]\otimes\Omega_{B}\longrightarrow\Omega_{B}[2n]
. This completes the proof of
Lemma

4.20
.
∎

4.2.
Smooth subgroup scheme of the automorphism scheme

We can now upgrade the infinitesimal statement
Theorem

4.8
to our desired
Theorem

4.2
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.2
.

Let us write
\Aut
for
\Aut_{\pi}
to ease notation.
Theorem

4.8
showed the main component of the Lie algebra scheme
\Lie\Aut
is smooth. We can thus use
Theorem

2.25
to yield the largest universally equidimensional and locally closed subscheme
P^{a}\subset\Aut
. To show
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
is a smooth subgroup scheme, it is enough to show that it has reduced fibers by
Section

2.5
.

Since the question is étale local on
B
and
\pi^{\prime}
is surjective, we may assume
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
has a section
e:B\longrightarrow X
(
Section

A.1.1
). Such
e
is a section of
X^{\prime}
because
X
and
B
are smooth (
Section

A.1.1
). Note that
\Aut
acts on
X^{\prime}
, so we can consider an orbit map (e.g., §
A.2.1
)

\phi=\phi_{e}:\Aut\longrightarrow X^{\prime},\qquad f\longmapsto f.e.

We claim its restriction

(4.22)

\phi_{|P^{a}}:P^{a}\subset\Aut\longrightarrow X^{\prime}

is an open immersion. This will prove
P^{a}_{b}
is reduced for every point
b\in B
and hence completes the proof of the theorem.

Let us first prove the following lemma.

4.23
.

Let
\Delta\longrightarrow B
be a non-degenerate trait and
\pi^{\prime}_{\Delta}:X^{\prime}_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
the base change of
\pi^{\prime}
. Let
e:\Delta\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{\Delta}
be a section and
\phi:\Aut_{\Delta}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{\Delta}
its orbit map. Then
\phi
restricted to the main component

(4.24)

\phi_{|H^{\Delta}}:H^{\Delta}\subset\Aut_{\Delta}\longrightarrow X_{\Delta}^{\prime}

is an open immersion.

Proof.

By
Section

2.5
, the main component
H^{\Delta}
of
\Aut_{\Delta}
is a smooth and closed subgroup scheme over
\Delta
whose Lie algebra is

\Lie H^{\Delta}=(\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B})_{|\Delta}

by
Theorem

4.8
. Consider the induced
H^{\Delta}
-action on
X^{\prime}_{\Delta}
. To prove its orbit map
H^{\Delta}\longrightarrow X_{\Delta}^{\prime}
is an open immersion, it is enough to show the
H^{\Delta}
-action on
X^{\prime}_{\Delta}
is free, or equivalently the stabilizer scheme
\St^{\prime}_{\Delta}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{\Delta}
is trivial by
Section

A.2.1
.

Recall that (
4.9
) was an fppf
\mathcal{O}_{B}
-module homomorphim
\Omega^{\fppf}_{B}\hookrightarrow\pi_{*}T_{\pi}^{\fppf}
, which was obtained by first taking the right exact sequence
\Omega_{\pi}\longrightarrow\pi^{*}T_{B}\longrightarrow T^{1}_{\pi}\longrightarrow 0
in (
4.11
), taking its fppf dual
0\longrightarrow\Hom^{\fppf}(T^{1}_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{B})\longrightarrow\pi^{*}\Omega^{\fppf}_{B}\longrightarrow T_{\pi}^{\fppf}
, and then subsequently taking
\pi_{*}
. Note that
T^{1}_{\pi}
is supported outside of
X^{\prime}
(it is supported on
\Sing(\pi)=X\setminus X^{\prime}
, thanks to
Section

A.1.2
). Now pullback the original right exact sequence first to
X^{\prime}_{\Delta}
and obtain

\Omega_{\pi^{\prime}_{\Delta}}\longrightarrow(\pi^{\prime}_{\Delta})^{*}T_{B}\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow 0.

Taking its fppf dual, we get a left exact sequence

0\longrightarrow 0\longrightarrow(\pi^{\prime}_{\Delta})^{*}\big((\Omega_{B}^{\fppf})_{|\Delta}\big)\longrightarrow T^{\fppf}_{\pi^{\prime}_{\Delta}},

which models the infinitesimal
H^{\Delta}
-action on
X^{\prime}_{\Delta}
because
\Lie H^{\Delta}=(\Omega^{\fppf}_{B})_{|\Delta}
. In other words, this sequence is
Section

A.2.1
and hence
\Lie\St^{\prime}_{\Delta}=0
.
∎

We can now complete the proof of the theorem. To show (
4.22
) is an open immersion, it is enough to show it is injective on closed points by
Section

A.1.7
(Zariski’s main theorem). Let
b\in B
be a closed point and
\Delta\longrightarrow B
a non-degenerate
quasi-finite
(or
unramified
) trait through
b
. The quasi-finiteness says the closed point of
\Delta
has residue field
\mathbb{C}
. Since (
4.24
) is an open immersion,
\phi
induces an injective map on closed points
(H^{\Delta})_{b}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}_{b}(\mathbb{C})
. Since
(P^{a}_{\Delta})_{\red}\subset H^{\Delta}
by
Section

2.5
(2a),
\phi
induces an injective map on closed points

\phi:P^{a}_{b}(\mathbb{C})\subset(H^{\Delta})_{b}(\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}_{b}(\mathbb{C}).

Vary
b\in B
to conclude that
\phi:P^{a}(\mathbb{C})\longrightarrow X^{\prime}(\mathbb{C})
is injective.
∎

4.3.
\delta
-regularity of the group scheme

We show the
\delta
-regularity of
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
in this subsection. For the notion of
\delta
-regularity and weak abelian fibrations, see §
A.2.2
. We have already seen that the coherent sheaf

T^{1}_{\pi}=\underline{\mathrm{Ext}}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\Omega_{\pi},\mathcal{O}_{X})

plays a particular role in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
and
4.8
. A posteriori, we can endow it with the following geometric meaning.

4.24
.

Let
\St\longrightarrow X
be the stabilizer group scheme of the
P^{a}
-action on
X
over
B
. Then
T^{1}_{\pi}
is the conormal sheaf of the identity section
X\longrightarrow\St
, i.e.,
\Lie\St
is a linear scheme associated to the coherent sheaf
T^{1}_{\pi}
.

Proof.

Take the third column
\Omega_{\pi}\longrightarrow\pi^{*}T_{B}\longrightarrow T^{1}_{\pi}\longrightarrow 0
of (
4.11
). Its fppf dual is a left exact sequence of linear schemes

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{V}_{T^{1}_{\pi}}\longrightarrow\pi^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\pi}.

The translation automorphism scheme
P^{a}\subset\Aut_{\pi}
satisfies

\Lie P^{a}=\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}\ \subset\ \Lie\Aut_{\pi}=\pi_{*}\mathbb{T}_{\pi}

via the inclusion in
Theorem

4.8
, which was again induced from the adjoint map of
\sigma:\pi^{*}\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\pi}
by the construction in §
4.1
. This means the last homomorphism above is precisely
\pi^{*}(\Lie P^{a})\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{\pi}
induced from the
P^{a}
-action.
Section

A.2.1
concludes
\Lie\St=\mathbb{V}_{T^{1}_{\pi}}
.
∎

We call the coherent sheaf
T^{1}_{\pi}
the
stabilizer sheaf
of the
P^{a}
-action on
X
. It contains infinitesimal information of the stabilizers of the
P^{a}
-action, e.g., dimension of the stabilizers.

4.24
.

The
P^{a}
-action on
X^{\prime}
is free.

Proof.

Since the (Fitting) support of
T^{1}_{\pi}
is
\Sing(\pi)
, the Lie algebra
\Lie(\St)\longrightarrow X
vanishes on
X^{\prime}
. Use
Section

A.2.1
to conclude.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

4.7
.

The following is essentially
[
Ng“ˆo11
, §2]
and
[
AF16
, Proposition 8.9]
. The group scheme
P^{a}
is a subgroup scheme of
\Aut_{\pi}
by construction. Hence
P^{a}
acts on
X
faithfully and
(X,P^{a})
is automatically a weak abelian fibration (e.g.,
Example

A.28
). We may thus use
Section

A.2.2
to check the
\delta
-regularity of
P^{a}
. By
Section

4.3
, it is enough to show

\codim\pi(\Fitt_{i-1}T^{1}_{\pi})\geq i.

By the diagram (
4.11
) and
Section

A.1.2
, we have an identity

\Fitt_{i-1}T^{1}_{\pi}=\Fitt_{n+i-1}\Omega_{\pi},

so the claim follows from
Lemma

A.4
.
∎

5.
Codimension
1
behavior of a Lagrangian fibration

Let again
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
be a Lagrangian fibration of a smooth symplectic variety over a smooth variety
B
. To use the results in §
4
, we always assume
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
is surjective. Consider the
\delta
-function
\delta:B\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
of the
\delta
-regular group scheme
P^{a}
as in (
A.24
) and define a decreasing sequence of Zariski closed subsets

D_{i}=\{b\in B:\delta(b)\geq i\}\ \subset B\qquad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,\cdots,n.

Note that
D_{1}
is precisely the discriminant locus of
\pi
, so every irreducible component of
D_{1}
has codimension
1
by
[
HO09
, Proposition 3.1]
. The
\delta
-regularity result in
Theorem

4.7
is the inequality
\codim D_{i}\geq i
. We will more often use their complements

(5.1)

B_{i}=\{b\in B:\delta(b)\leq i\}=B\setminus D_{i+1}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad i=0,\cdots,n,

which is an increasing sequence of Zariski open subsets.

In this section, we focus on the behavior of
\pi
over the first nontrivial
\delta
-locus
B_{1}
. We somewhat vaguely call it a study of the
codimension
1
behavior
of
\pi
. Results in §
2
and §
3
will be freely used. Recall that the Lagrangian fibration outside of the discriminant locus
\pi_{0}:X_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
is a
P_{0}
-torsor for a uniquely determined abelian scheme
P_{0}=\Aut^{\circ}_{\pi_{0}}\longrightarrow B_{0}
(see
[
AF16
, §8]
or
[
Kim25
, §3]
). The goal of this section is to show the morphism
\pi_{1}:X_{1}=\pi^{-1}(B_{1})\longrightarrow B_{1}
is still manageable. Our results are highly motivated by the work
[
HO09
]
, which is a generalization of Kodaira’s work on singular fibers of minimal elliptic fibrations.

5.2
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of
Theorem

4.2
. Let
B_{0}
and
B_{1}
be its first two
\delta
-loci in
B
as in (
5.1
). Then

(1)

X^{\prime}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
is a Néron model of
X_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
.

(2)

P^{a}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
is a Néron model of
P_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
.

As a consequence,
X^{\prime}_{1}
is a
P^{a}_{1}
-torsor over
B_{1}
.

5.3
.

Let
b\in B
be a closed point and
\Delta\longrightarrow B
a non-degenerate
unramified
trait through
b
.
[
HO09
,
HO11
]
studied the behavior of the fiber
X_{b}
under an assumption that
X_{\Delta}=X\times_{B}\Delta
is regular. One can show in this situation that (we omit the details)

(1)

b\in B_{1}
(construct a surjection
\pi^{*}\Omega_{\Delta}\longrightarrow T^{1}_{\pi_{\Delta}}=(T^{1}_{\pi})_{|\Delta}
similarly to (
4.11
) and show that the stabilizer sheaf
T^{1}_{\pi_{\Delta}}
has fiber dimensions
\leq 1
); and

(2)

X^{\prime}_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is a Néron model (use
Theorem

3.6
and imitate the first paragraph of
Example

3.8
).

Theorem

5.2
is a higher-dimensional base formulation of these results.

We will see that the Néron mapping property of
X^{\prime}
is intimately related to the behavior of
birational translation automorphisms
of
X
(
Definition

5.6
). This means we should study birational translation automorphisms, or more generally arbitrary birational maps between two Lagrangian fibered symplectic varieties. To state the main theorem to this direction, let us first note that the translation
automorphism
scheme
P^{a}
is not an invariant under birational maps between Lagrangian fibrations. To reconcile this, we consider its strict neutral component (§
2.2.1
)

P^{\circ\circ}\subset P^{a}.

5.4
.

Let
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
and
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
be Lagrangain fibrations of smooth symplectic varieties with surjective
\pi^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
. Let
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
be a birational map over
B
. Then

(1)

f
induces an isomorphism
P_{X}^{\circ\circ}=P_{Y}^{\circ\circ}(=P^{\circ\circ})
and it is
P^{\circ\circ}
-equivariant. In particular, the
\delta
-loci (
5.1
) of
\pi
and
\tau
coincide.

(2)

f
is an isomorphism over the first
\delta
-locus
B_{1}
. In particular,
P^{a}_{X}
and
P^{a}_{Y}
are isomorphic over
B_{1}
.

5.5
.

Two remarks about
Theorem

5.4
.

(1)

The birational map
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
is allowed to be non-symplectic.

(2)

B_{1}\subset B
always has a codimension
\geq 2
complement by
\delta
-regularity but the inequality may be strict. For example, we may have
B_{1}=B
in certain cases, and the theorem for these cases claims that every birational map
f
is an isomorphism.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of the two main theorems.

5.1.
Flops between Lagrangian fibrations

We start with an observation that the Néron mapping property of
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}=X\setminus\Sing(\pi)\longrightarrow B
in (
4.3
) and
birational translation automorphisms
of
\pi
are closely related.

5.6
.

In the setting of
Definition

4.1
, a
birational translation automorphism
of
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
is a birational automorphism
f:X\dashrightarrow X
over
B
such that for every point
b\in B_{0}
, the automorphism
f_{b}:X_{b}\longrightarrow X_{b}
is a translation of an abelian variety.

Notice that every birational automorphism of
X
over
B
is necessarily defined on
X_{0}
(since abelian varieties do not contain rational curves, e.g.,
[
Kim25
, Lemma 3.26]
). Hence
f_{b}:X_{b}\longrightarrow X_{b}
is indeed a regular automorphism for every
b\in B_{0}
and the definition makes sense.

5.7
.

Assume
\pi^{\prime}
in (
4.3
) is surjective. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

X^{\prime}
is Néron under
B_{0}\subset B
.

(2)

For every quasi-projective étale morphism
U\longrightarrow B
, a birational translation automorphism
f:X_{U}\dashrightarrow X_{U}
over
U
induces a regular translation automorphism
f:X^{\prime}_{U}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{U}
over
U
.

Proof.

(\Longrightarrow)
A birational automorphism
f
induces a regular automorphism
f:X_{U_{0}}\longrightarrow X_{U_{0}}
over
U_{0}
because it has abelian variety fibers. It subsequently extends to an automorphism
f:X^{\prime}_{U}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{U}
over
U
by the Néron mapping property of
X^{\prime}
.

(\Longleftarrow)
Since
\pi^{\prime}
is surjective and
P^{a}
is acting freely on
X^{\prime}
, it is enough to show that
X^{\prime}
is weakly Néron under
B_{0}\subset B
by
Theorem

3.27
. By
Section

3.1
and the surjectivity of
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
, up to taking a covering of
B
in étale topology we may assume
\pi^{\prime}
has a section
e:B\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
. Let
U\longrightarrow B
be a quasi-projective étale morphism (see
Remark

3.21
) and
s_{0}:U_{0}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{U_{0}}
a morphism. The difference
s_{0}-e_{0}
defines a translation automorphism of
X_{U_{0}}
, or a birational translation automorphism

f:X_{U}\dashrightarrow X_{U}.

By assumption,
f
defines an automorphism
f:X^{\prime}_{U}\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{U}
over
U
. In particular,
s_{0}
uniquely extends to a morphism
s=f\circ e:U\longrightarrow X^{\prime}_{U}
. This shows the weak Néron mapping property of
X^{\prime}
.
∎

This reduces the study of Néron mapping property of
X^{\prime}
to the study of all birational translation automorphisms locally on
B
. In the rest of this subsection, we focus on proving the following
section

5.1
, which will be a preparatory step to the proof of
Theorem

5.4
.

Let us explain the terminology beforehand. The smooth part of a Lagrangian fibration
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
is
surjective over codimension
1
points in
B
if the open subset
\pi(X^{\prime})\subset B
has codimension
\geq 2
complement. Equivalently, this means every codimension
1
(geometric) fiber
X_{\eta}=\pi^{-1}(\eta)
has at least one integral component. A birational map
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
over
B
is
isomorphic over codimension
1
points in
B
if there exists a dense open subscheme
U\subset B
of codimension
\geq 2
complement such that
f_{U}:X_{U}\dashrightarrow Y_{U}
is defined everywhere and is an isomorphism.

5.7
.

Let
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
and
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
be Lagrangian fibrations of smooth symplectic varieties. Assume
\pi^{\prime}
is surjective over codimension
1
points in
B
. Then every birational map
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
over
B
is isomorphic over codimension
1
points in
B
.

Observe the following before getting into its proof.

5.8
.

If
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
is surjective over codimension
1
points in
B
, then so is
\tau^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}=Y\setminus\Sing(\tau)\longrightarrow B
.

Proof.

Assume on the contrary that there exists a codimension
1
point
\eta\in B
with nowhere reduced fiber
Y_{\eta}
. Taking closure, we have a prime divisor
D=\overline{\{\eta\}}\subset B
such that
Y_{D}=\tau^{-1}(D)
is a nowhere reduced divisor in
Y
. But
X
and
Y
are both relative minimal models over
B
(i.e., they have terminal singularities, proper over
B
, and have relatively nef canonical divisors), so
f
is isomorphic in codimension
1
points by
[
KM98
, Corollary 3.54]
. This means
X_{D}=\pi^{-1}(D)
is a nowhere reduced divisor in
X
as well, contradicting the assumption that
\pi^{\prime}
is surjective over codimension
1
points in
B
.
∎

5.1.1.
Flops between Lagrangian fibrations

Let
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
and
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
be Lagarngian fibrations of smooth symplectic varieties, and let
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
an arbitrary birational map over
B
that is possibly non-symplectic. Since
X
(resp.
Y
) is a relative minimal model over
B
(i.e.,
X
has terminal singularities,
\pi
is proper, and
K_{X}
is
\pi
-nef),
[
Kaw08
, Theorem 1]
shows
f
is a composition of a finite sequence of
\varepsilon L
-flops where
L
is a Cartier divisor on
X
and
0<\varepsilon\ll 1
is a rational number (in particular,
(X,\varepsilon L)
is a terminal pair):

(5.9)

The diagram is defined over
B
. Specifying the last isomorphism
f^{(t+1)}
will be important in the case
Y=X
.

5.10
.

In (
5.9
), the following holds for all
i
.

(1)

X^{(i)}
is a smooth symplectic variety.

(2)

\bar{X}^{(i)}
is a (singular) symplectic variety.

(3)

X^{(i)}\longrightarrow B
(resp.
\bar{X}^{(i)}\longrightarrow B
) is a Lagrangian fibration.

(4)

Assume that
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
is surjective over codimension
1
points in
B
. Then so is
(\pi^{(i)})^{\prime}:(X^{(i)})^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
.

Proof.

Using induction on
i
, we may focus on the case
i=1
and (to ease notation) temporarily denote a single flop
f
by

Flops are isomorphic in codimension
1
, so the symplectic form
\sigma
on
X
defines a symplectic form on a codimension
2
complement of
Y
. Such a symplectic form extends to a regular
2
-form on the common resolution of
X
and
Y
, so this proves
Y
is a symplectic variety. Similarly,
\bar{X}
is a symplectic variety. Note that
Y
, as a flop of a smooth variety, is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial and terminal by
[
KM98
, Proposition 3.37, Corollary 3.42]
. Hence
[
Nam08
, Corollary 31]
applies and shows
Y
is smooth.

The varieties
X
,
\bar{X}
, and
Y
are isomorphic generically over
B
so the third item is clear. The last item is simply
Lemma

5.8
applied to
X\dashrightarrow Y
.
∎

This reduces the study of a birational map
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
to a single flop
f^{(i)}:X^{(i)}\dashrightarrow X^{(i+1)}
in (
5.9
). We may thus assume
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
is a single
\varepsilon L
-flop between
smooth
symplectic varieties. Denote the flopping diagram by

(5.11)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

where everything is Lagrangian fibered over
B
. Since
\pi^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
are surjective over codimension
1
points in
B
, we may use the results in §
4
up to taking a codimension
\geq 2
complement of
B
. Let us study this diagram step by step.

5.12
.

In (
5.11
), let
W\subset\Exc(g)
be an irreducible component of the exceptional locus of
g
and
\bar{W}=g(W)
its image in
\bar{X}
. Then
\dim W-\dim\bar{W}\geq 2
.

Proof.

Since
g:X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
is a symplectic resolution,
[
Wie03
, Theorem 1.2(i)]
applies and shows

\dim W-\dim\bar{W}\geq\codim W.

But
\codim W\geq 2
because
g
was a flopping contraction. This lemma will be generalized in
Section

5.2
.
∎

The following is a result in
[
HO09
, §2–3]
(especially Proposition 3.8). Let us recover their results to illustrate the use of
Theorem

4.2
and
4.7
. See
Remark

5.13
as well.

5.12
Hwang–Oguiso.

There exists a Zariski open subset
U\subset B
with codimension
\geq 2
complement with the following properties for every closed point
b\in U
:

(1)

The normalization
X_{b}^{\nu}
of the fiber
X_{b}
is a smooth projective variety, possibly disconnected.

(2)

Assume that a connected component
T
of
X_{b}^{\nu}
contains a rational curve
C
. Then
C
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and there exists a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle morphism

f:T\longrightarrow A

to an abelian variety
A
of dimension
n-1
, realizing
C
as one of its fibers.

Proof.

We prove
U=B_{1}
satisfies the desired property. The statements are trivial when
X_{b}
is an abelian variety, so let us assume
b\in B_{1}\setminus B_{0}
. The algebraic group
G=P^{\circ\circ}_{b}
acts on
X_{b}
by
Theorem

4.2
and hence its normalization
X_{b}^{\nu}
as well. The
\delta
-value of
G
is precisely
\delta(G)=1
by
Theorem

4.7
, so the Chevalley short exact sequence of
G
is

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
or
\mathbb{G}_{a}
is a
1
-dimensional connected and commutative linear algebraic group and
A
is an abelian variety of dimension
n-1
. Moreover, the stabilizer of each point
x\in X_{b}
is either trivial or
\mathbb{G}
again by the
\delta
-regularity in
Section

A.2.2
(it is trivial if and only if
x\in X_{b}^{\prime}
by
Section

4.3
). Consider the normalization morphism

\nu:X_{b}^{\nu}\longrightarrow X_{b},

which is automatically
G
-equivariant. The stabilizer dimension of each point in
X_{b}^{\nu}
is
\leq 1
. But the
G
-subvariety
\Sing(X_{b}^{\nu})
has dimension
\leq n-2
by normality, so this forces
\Sing(X_{b}^{\nu})=\varnothing
and proves the smoothness of
X_{b}^{\nu}
.

Take a connected component
T
of
X_{b}^{\nu}
, a smooth projective connected variety. If
\nu(T)\subset X_{b}
is a reduced component, then the
G
-action on
T
is generically free, so
T
can be considered as a compactification
j:G\hookrightarrow T
. Consider a rational map

Any rational map from a smooth variety to an abelian variety is everywhere defined, so
f
is a morphism. This morphism is
G
-equivariant, so every closed fiber of
f
is isomorphic to each other. In particular,
f
is smooth projective by generic smoothness. Since it is a compactification of a
\mathbb{G}
-torsor
G\longrightarrow A
, it must be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle. As a result,
C\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

If
\nu(T)\subset X_{b}
is a non-reduced component, then
\mathbb{G}\subset G
acts trivially on the entire
T
. Hence
T
has an induced
A
-action with finite stabilizers. Take a finite abelian subgroup
K\subset A
containing all stabilizer subgroups and consider the quotient
\bar{T}=T/K
with a free
\bar{A}=A/K
-action. Since
\bar{T}
is normal and
\bar{A}
acts on it freely, similar argument to above shows that
\bar{T}
is smooth. Take a free quotient

g:\bar{T}\longrightarrow C^{\prime}=\bar{T}/\bar{A},

where
C^{\prime}
is a
1
-dimensional smooth proper algebraic space, whence a smooth projective curve. Since the fibers of
g
are abelian varieties,
g
induces a surjective morphism
\bar{C}\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
from a rational projective curve
\bar{C}
in
\bar{T}
. This forces
C^{\prime}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
. But
H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(\mathbb{P}^{1},\bar{A})=0
, so
g
is necessarily a trivial
\bar{A}
-torsor and hence
\bar{T}\cong\bar{A}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

To conclude, compose
T\longrightarrow\bar{T}
with
\pr_{1}:\bar{T}\longrightarrow\bar{A}
and take its Stein factorization:

The
A
-action on
A^{\prime}
forces it to be smooth. Moreover, since
A^{\prime}\longrightarrow\bar{A}
is finite and
A
-equivariant, it is an étale covering and hence
A^{\prime}
is an abelian variety. Finally, since
f:T\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
is
A
-equivariant, it is a smooth projective morphism whose fibers are connected and containing a rational curve. The claim follows.
∎

5.13
.

In
Section

5.1.1
, the singular fiber
X_{b}
and hence its normalization
X_{b}^{\nu}
may not contain any rational curves
[
Mat01
, Proposition 6.1]

[
HO11
, Theorem 1.1]
. This arises when
X_{b}
is irreducible and non-reduced. In this case,
(X_{b})_{\red}\longrightarrow A
is an ellpitic curve fiber bundle over an abelian variety of dimension
n-1
(e.g., bielliptic surface). See
[
HO09
, Proposition 3.7–8]
and
[
Mat01
, Type
\mathrm{I}_{0}
in Table 4]
.

Proof of
Section

5.1
.

Using the decomposition (
5.9
), we may reduce to the case when
f
is a single
\varepsilon L
-flop of
X
as in (
5.11
). Let
W\subset\Exc(g)
be an irreducible component of the exceptional locus of
g
and
\bar{W}=g(W)
its image. Then
f
is isomorphic outside of
\pi(W)\subset B
. Hence, it is enough to show
\codim\pi(W)\geq 2
.

Let
\bar{x}\in\bar{W}
be a general point and set
b=\bar{\pi}(\bar{x})
. Since
\bar{X}
is symplectic and hence has canonical singularities,
[
HM07
, Corollary 1.5]
shows the fiber
W_{\bar{x}}
of
W\longrightarrow\bar{W}
is rationally chain connected. From
Lemma

5.12
, we have
\dim W_{\bar{x}}\geq 2
. But
W_{\bar{x}}
is contained in the fiber
X_{b}
, so this means
X_{b}
contains a
2
-dimensional rationally chain connected subvariety. This is impossible for
b\in U
where
U\subset B
is a Zariski open subset of codimension
\geq 2
complement defined in
Section

5.1.1
. Hence
b\notin U
, proving
\pi(W)\cap U=\varnothing
and in particular
\codim\pi(W)\geq 2
.
∎

5.2.
Codimension
1
behavior of birational maps

Section

5.1
, together with Raynaud’s extension
theorem

2.13
, implies the following proposition. This proposition in turn strengthens the discussions in the previous subsection.

5.13
.

Let
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
and
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
be Lagrangain fibrations of smooth symplectic varieties with surjective
\pi^{\prime}
and
\tau^{\prime}
. Let
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
be a birational map over
B
. Then

(1)

f
induces an isomorphism
P^{\circ\circ}_{X}\cong P^{\circ\circ}_{Y}
. Let us denote them by
P^{\circ\circ}
.

(2)

In (
5.9
),
X^{(i)}
and
\bar{X}^{(i)}
for all
i
admit
P^{\circ\circ}
-actions over
B
. As a consequence,
(X^{(i)},P^{\circ\circ})
and
(\bar{X}^{(i)},P^{\circ\circ})
are
\delta
-regular abelian fibrations.

(3)

g^{(i)}
and
h^{(i)}
are equivariant.

Proof.

(1) By
Section

5.1
, there exists a codimension
\geq 2
complement
U\subset B
such that
f_{U}:X_{U}\longrightarrow Y_{U}
over
U
is an isomorphism. This induces an isomorphism of smooth group spaces
P^{\circ\circ}_{X|U}\cong P^{\circ\circ}_{Y|U}
over
U
. The isomorphism uniquely extends to an isomorphism
P^{\circ\circ}_{X}\cong P^{\circ\circ}_{Y}
over
B
by
Theorem

2.13
.

(2) and (3) Again let us simply write
X=X^{(i)}
,
\bar{X}=\bar{X}^{(i)}
, and
Y=X^{(i+1)}
. Since
g:X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
is an isomorphism over
U
by
Section

5.1
, there exists a homomorphism of group spaces
P^{\circ\circ}_{X|U}\longrightarrow\Aut_{\bar{\pi}_{U}}
over
U
. This uniquely extends to a homomorphism
P^{\circ\circ}_{X}\longrightarrow\Aut_{\bar{\pi}}
over
B
again by
Theorem

2.13
, proving that
\bar{X}
admits a
P^{\circ\circ}
-action. To prove the morphism
g
is
P^{\circ\circ}
-equivariant, consider the diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The diagram commutes over
U
because
g
was an isomorphism over it. Since
\bar{X}\longrightarrow B
is separated and
(P^{\circ\circ}\times_{B}X)_{|U}\subset P^{\circ\circ}\times_{B}X
is dense by
Lemma

A.6
, the diagram commutes over the entire
B
by
Lemma

A.7
. Same discussions hold for
Y
and
h:Y\longrightarrow\bar{X}
.

Finally, recall that the
P^{\circ\circ}
-stabilizers of points in
X
are affine. Since
g:X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
was
P^{\circ\circ}
-equivariant, this shows that for each
b\in B
, there exists a point
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{b}\setminus\bar{W}_{b}=X_{b}\setminus W_{b}\neq\varnothing
with affine stabilizer. By
[
AF16
, Lemma 5.16]
(or the proof of
Lemma

A.29
), this shows that every point
x\in\bar{X}_{b}
has affine stabilizers and hence
(\bar{X},P^{\circ\circ})
is a weak abelian fibration.
∎

5.13
.

In (
5.11
), let
W\subset\Exc(g)
be an irreducible component of the exceptional locus of
g
and
\bar{W}=g(W)
its image in
\bar{X}
. Write
d=\codim_{X}W
and
e=\codim_{\bar{X}}\bar{W}
. Then

(1)

e=2d
.

(2)

W
is an irreducible component of
\pi^{-1}(D_{d})
and
\pi(W)
is an irreducible component of
D_{d}
with codimension
d
.

(3)

Every fiber of the morphism
W\longrightarrow\bar{W}
is rationally chain connected and has dimension
\geq d
.

(4)

The normalization
\bar{W}^{\nu}
of
\bar{W}
is a symplectic variety of dimension
2(n-d)
. The morphism
\pi
induces a Lagrangian fibration
\bar{W}^{\nu}\longrightarrow\pi(W)^{\nu}
.

Same results hold for
h
.

Proof.

(1) In
Lemma

5.12
, we already used the inequality
\dim W-\dim\bar{W}\geq\codim W
in
[
Wie03
, Theorem 1.2(i)]
, i.e., the inequality
e-d\geq d
. Let us prove the opposite inequality
e-d\leq d
.

The image
\pi(W)
is an irreducible subvariety of
B
. Since
\pi
is equidimensional, we have
\codim\pi(W)\leq\codim W=d
. Take the
largest
integer
k
with
\pi(W)\subset D_{k}
. We then have
k\leq\codim D_{k}\leq\codim\pi(W)
by the
\delta
-regularity of the Lagrangian fibration. Combining the inequalities, we get

(5.14)

k\leq\codim D_{k}\leq\codim\pi(W)\leq\codim W=d

A general fiber of the morphism
W\longrightarrow\bar{W}
has dimension
e-d
. Fixing a general closed point
b\in\pi(W)
, this shows
\dim W_{b}=\dim\bar{W}_{b}+(e-d)
. Note that
b\in D_{k}\setminus D_{k+1}
because
b\in\pi(W)
is chosen to be general and
k
is assumed to be maximal. Since
(\bar{X},P^{\circ\circ})
is a
\delta
-regular abelian fibration by
Section

5.2
,
\bar{W}_{b}\subset\bar{X}_{b}
has stabilizer dimension
\leq k
at every point. Hence
\dim\bar{W}_{b}\geq n-k
. We have a sequence of inequalities

(n-k)+(e-d)\leq\dim\bar{W}_{b}+(e-d)=\dim W_{b}\leq n.

This means
e-d\leq k
and hence
e-d\leq d
by (
5.14
). Therefore,
e=2d
.

(2) All the intermediate inequalities above are now equalities, so we have
k=d
,
\codim\pi(W)=d
, and that
\pi(W)
is an irreducible component of
D_{d}
. Since
\dim W=2n-d
,
\dim\pi(W)=n-d
, and
\pi
is equidimensional of relative dimension
n
, this forces
W
to be an irreducible component of
\pi^{-1}(\pi(W))
. (3) Apply
[
HM07
, Corollary 1.5]
to
g:X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
(note that
\bar{X}
is symplectic so it has canonical singularities). (4) This is simply a copy of
[
Mat15
, Theorem 3.1]
, based on the previous result of
[
Kal06
, Theorem 2.5]
.
∎

We can finally complete the proof of
Theorem

5.2
and
5.4
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.4
.

We have already proved in
Section

5.2
that
P^{\circ\circ}_{X}\cong P^{\circ\circ}_{Y}
and
f
is equivariant. Hence the
\delta
-loci of
\pi
and
\tau
coincide. It only remains to prove
f
is isomorphic over
B_{1}
. To do so, reduce it to the case when
f
is a single flop as in (
5.11
). Let
W\subset\Exc(g)
be an irreducible component of codimension
d\geq 2
. By
Section

5.2
, we have
\pi(W)\subset D_{d}
. In particular,
\pi(W)\cap B_{1}=\varnothing
and hence
g
is isomorphic over
B_{1}
. Similarly,
h
is isomorphic over
B_{1}
and hence the result follows.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

5.2
.

(1) To show
X_{1}^{\prime}
is Néron under
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
, thanks to
Lemma

5.7
, it is enough to prove that for every quasi-projective étale morphism
U\longrightarrow B_{1}
, a birational translation automorphism
f:X_{U}\dashrightarrow X_{U}
over
U
is a translation automorphism
f:X_{U}^{\prime}\longrightarrow X_{U}^{\prime}
over
U
. The
\delta
-regular loci are invariant under base change, so this means
U_{1}=U
. Hence, the birational automorphism
f
is in fact a regular automorphism on the entire domain
X_{U}
by
Theorem

5.4
.

(2) Thanks to
Theorem

3.22
, we only need to show
P^{a}_{1}
is weakly Néron under
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
. Let
U\longrightarrow B_{1}
be a quasi-projective étale morphism and
f:U_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}
a
U_{0}
-section. Since
P_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
represents the translation automorphisms of
X_{0}
by
Theorem

4.2
,
f
induces a birational translation automorphism
f:X_{U}\dashrightarrow X_{U}
over
U
. By
Theorem

5.4
and
U_{1}=U
, this uniquely extends to a translation automorphism of
X_{U}
, which in turn is equivalent to a section
U\longrightarrow P^{a}_{1}
. This proves the weak Néron mapping property of
P^{a}_{1}
. Finally, by
Section

3.4
,
X^{\prime}_{1}
is a
P^{a}_{1}
-torsor because
\pi^{\prime}
is surjective.
∎

We conclude this section with the following lemma, which will be used later on in the proof of
Section

6.2
and in §
6.3.3
. Recall from
Section

5.2
that every irreducible component
W\subset\Exc(g)
of the exceptional locus of a flop has codimension
\leq n
. If
\codim W=n
then
X\longrightarrow\bar{X}
contracts
W
to a point, so
[
WW03
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
AV24
, Theorem 1.2]
conclude that
W
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

5.15
.

Let
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
be a Lagrangian fibration of a smooth symplectic algebraic space
X
of dimension
2n
. Assume that a closed fiber
X_{b}
contains
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Let
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
be the Mukai flop of such
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and let
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
be the flopped locus. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

The scheme-theoretic component of
X_{b}
corresponding to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
is reduced.

(2)

The scheme-theoretic component of
Y_{b}
corresponding to
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is reduced.

Proof.

Before starting the proof, let us first notice that
Y
admits a Lagrangian fibration
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
with
\pi=\tau\circ f
. Moreover, since
X_{b}
and
Y_{b}
are lci schemes, they admit unique scheme-theoretic irreducible decompositions (the primary decomposition). Consider the Muaki flop diagram

}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.50029pt}{-17.13747pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 28.37 -23.71)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where
\epsilon
and
\delta
are blowups of
\mathbb{P}^{n}\subset X
and
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\subset Y
. They share the same exceptional divisor
E\subset\tilde{X}
so that both
\mathbb{P}^{n}\longleftarrow E\longrightarrow\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
are
\mathbb{P}^{n-1}
-bundles (
E\subset\mathbb{P}^{n}\times\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}
is the universal hyperplane). If
\mathbb{P}^{n}\subset X_{b}
is a reduced component, then
E=\epsilon^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{n})\subset\tilde{X}_{b}=\epsilon^{-1}(X_{b})
is a reduced component. By
E=\delta^{-1}(\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n})\subset\tilde{X}_{b}=\delta^{-1}(Y_{b})
, this implies that
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\subset Y_{b}
is a reduced component. The converse holds as well since
X
is the Mukai flop of
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{n}\subset Y
.
∎

6.
The Néron model of a Lagrangian fibration

This section extends the codimension
1
behavior of Lagrangian fibrations studied in §
5
to the deeper
\delta
-strata in
B
. Recall from
Theorem

5.2
that both the translation automorphism scheme
P^{a}
and the smooth locus
X^{\prime}
are Néron under
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
, and as result
X^{\prime}_{1}
is a
P_{1}
-torsor over
B_{1}
. When
n=1
, i.e., when
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
is a minimal elliptic fibration without multiple fibers, we have
B_{1}=B
and this recovers the original result of Néron–Raynaud in the introduction and
Example

3.8
. For higher dimensional Lagrangian fibrations, both
P^{a}
and
X^{\prime}
may fail to be Néron under the full extension of the base
B_{0}\subset B
. In fact, it is unclear from our previous discussions whether
P^{a}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
and
X^{\prime}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
may even admit a Néron model under the further extension
B_{1}\subset B
. We need a new input to tackle this problem: the new ingredient will be the
dual side
of the story.

Recall that the translation automorphism scheme
P^{a}
restricted over
B_{0}
was an abelian scheme
\nu_{0}:P_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
. Consider its dual abelian scheme
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
(e.g.,
[
FC90
, §I.1]
), which by definition is its neutral Picard scheme
\check{P}_{0}=\Pic^{\circ}_{\nu_{0}}\longrightarrow B_{0}
. Since
X_{0}
is a
P_{0}
-torsor, it has a same neutral Picard schemes to
P_{0}
. We may thus define the dual abelian scheme by

\check{P}_{0}=\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{0}}\longrightarrow B_{0},

which is a restriction of the full neutral Picard space
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}\longrightarrow B
of the Lagrangian fibration. A projective abelian scheme
P_{0}
and its dual
\check{P}_{0}
admit polarizations

\lambda_{0}:P_{0}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0},\qquad\check{\lambda}_{0}:\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}.

These finite étale homomorphisms connect many properties of
P_{0}
and
\check{P}_{0}
, including their Néron extendability. More specifically,
P_{0}
admits a Néron model
P^{a}_{1}
over
B_{1}
and
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is Néron under
B_{1}\subset B
(line bundles on a regular scheme are determined by their codimension
1
behavior), so the polarizations connect these complementary properties and yield the Néron models of both
P_{0}
and
\check{P}_{0}
.

6.1
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of
Theorem

4.2
and
5.2
. Then

(1)

There exists a Néron model
P\longrightarrow B
of the abelian scheme
P_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
.

(2)

There exists a Néron model
\check{P}\longrightarrow B
of the dual abelian scheme
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
.

(3)

Every finite étale homomorphism
\lambda_{0}:P_{0}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0}
(e.g., polarization) over
B_{0}
uniquely extends to a quasi-projective étale homomorphism

\lambda:P\longrightarrow\check{P}

over
B
. Same holds for finite étale homomorphisms
\check{\lambda}_{0}:\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}
.

(4)

P
and
\check{P}
are
\delta
-regular with the same
\delta
-loci.

Recall the notion of a birational translation automorphism in
Definition

5.6
. The Néron mapping property of
P
says that for every étale morphism
U\longrightarrow B
, we have

(6.2)

P(U)=\{f:X_{U}\dashrightarrow X_{U}:f\mbox{ is a birational translation automorphism over }U\}.

This means
P
can be considered as a moduli space of birational translation automorphisms of
\pi
; compare it with (
4.4
) and
Definition

4.6
.

6.3
.

We call the Néron model
P\longrightarrow B
in
Theorem

6.1
the
birational translation automorphism space
of
\pi
.

By construction, there is an open immersion
P^{a}\subset P
of group spaces over
B
. This immersion is an equality over
B_{1}
by
Theorem

5.2
but not over
B
in general. It is unclear from
Theorem

6.1
whether
P
is separated (or of finite type), but its numerically trivial open subgroup space
P^{\tau}\subset P
(§
2.4
) is always separated by
Section

3.6.1
. We obtain a sequence of open immersions of smooth commutative group spaces

(6.4)

P^{\circ\circ}\subset P^{a}\subset P^{\tau}\subset P.

For each inclusion, we present an example for which the inclusion is strict (§
6.3
). The three groups
P^{\circ\circ}
,
P^{\tau}
, and
P
are invariant under birational equivalences of
\pi
, but the group
P^{a}
is not.

Similar results hold for the Néron model of
\pi_{0}:X_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
under the extension
B_{0}\subset B
. The Néron model always exists, but checking whether it is of finite type or separated is a subtle question.

6.5
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of
Theorem

6.1
. Then

(1)

There exists a Néron model
X^{n}\longrightarrow B
of
\pi_{0}:X_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
. It contains
X^{\prime}
as a
P^{a}
-invariant open subscheme.

(2)

X^{n}=\bigcup_{Y_{U}}Y^{\prime}_{U}
where
U\longrightarrow B
runs through every quasi-projective étale morphism and
\tau:Y_{U}\longrightarrow U
a Lagrangian fibered smooth symplectic variety that is birational to
X_{U}
.

Consequently,
X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor over
B
.

To talk about the finite type and separatedness of the Néron models
P
and
X^{n}
, notice first that
P
is of finite type (resp. separated) if and only if
X^{n}
is. It is relatively easy to see the implications:
X^{\prime}=X^{n}

\Longrightarrow

X^{n}
is of finite type
\Longrightarrow

X^{n}
is separated. This motivates us to consider the following.

6.6
.

Define the following three subsets of
B
:

\displaystyle B_{\tor}

\displaystyle=\{b\in B:X^{\prime}_{b}=X^{n}_{b}\},

\displaystyle B_{\ft}

\displaystyle=\mbox{maximal Zariski open subset of }B\mbox{ over which }X^{n}\mbox{ is of finite type},

\displaystyle B_{\sep}

\displaystyle=\mbox{maximal Zariski open subset of }B\mbox{ over which }X^{n}\mbox{ is separated}.

Clearly,
B_{\tor}=B
(resp.
B_{\ft}=B
and
B_{\sep}=B
) if and only if
X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor (resp.
X^{n}
is of finite type and separated).

6.6
.

Keep the notations and assumptions of
Theorem

6.5
. Then

(1)

B_{\tor}
is the complement of a finite union of certain irreducible components of the
\delta
-loci
D_{i}
’s. In particular, it is Zariski open in
B
.

(2)

B_{1}\subset B_{\tor}\subset B_{\ft}\subset B_{\sep}\subset B
.

(3)

Assume
\dim X=2n=4
. Then the following are equivalent:

(a)

P^{\tau}=P
, i.e., every (local) birational translation automorphism is numerically trivial.

(b)

X^{\prime}=X^{n}
, i.e.,
X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor.

(c)

X^{n}\longrightarrow B
is of finite type.

(d)

X^{n}\longrightarrow B
is separated.

In particular,
B_{\tor}=B_{\ft}=B_{\sep}
.

(4)

B_{1}\subset B_{\tor}
and
B_{\sep}\subset B
may be strict.

We expect
Section

6
(3) to hold in arbitrary dimensions, and therefore the sequence (
6.4
) will be useful to understand the behavior of the Néron models
X^{n}
and
P
. We provide three examples in §
6.3
for which we can calculate all four groups in (
6.4
). In the first two examples, we have
P^{\tau}=P
and moreover
X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor. In the last example, we have
P^{\tau}\subsetneq P
and moreover
X^{\prime}
is not a
P
-torsor.

Though
X^{\prime}
fails to be a
P
-torsor in general, one may still hope whether there
exists
a smooth group space
G\longrightarrow B
making
X^{\prime}
a
G
-torsor. Unfortunately §
6.3.3
provides a counterexample for this statement, too:

6.6
.

There is an example of a Lagrangian fibration
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
with surjective
\pi^{\prime}
such that
X^{\prime}
is not a
G
-torsor for every smooth group space
G\longrightarrow B
.

The first two subsections will be devoted to the proof of the above results. Examples and applications will be presented in the next two sections §
6.3
–
6.4
. Again we will freely use the results in §
2
and §
3
. Notations and assumptions in
Theorem

4.2
and
5.2
will be used throughout.

6.1.
The Néron model of the automorphism and Picard space

A line bundle on a regular scheme is determined by its restriction to a codimension
\geq 2
complement. This can be interpreted as the Néron mapping property of the Picard space and is used in
[
BLR90
, Theorem 9.5.4]
and
[
Hol19
, Theorem 6.2]
in the context of constructing the Néron model of the relative Jacobian of a flat projective family of curves.

6.7
.

There exists a well-defined relative Picard space
\Pic_{\pi}\longrightarrow B
with
\Lie\Pic_{\pi}\cong\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}
. As a result, the neutral relative Picard space
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}\longrightarrow B
is a smooth group space.

Proof.

The existence of a group space
\Pic_{\pi}
(
Theorem

2.32
) is from the flat properness of
\pi
together with
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\fppf}=\mathcal{O}_{B}^{\fppf}
(
Lemma

4.12
). Its Lie algebra scheme can be computed from Matsushita’s result
R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\cong\Omega_{B}

[
Mat05
, Theorem 1.3]

[
Sch23
, §4]
(or
Theorem

4.19
). The smoothness of the Lie algebra scheme implies that its neutral component
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is a smooth group space by
Section

2.2.2
. We do not know whether
\Pic_{\pi}
is smooth.
∎

6.7
.

Assume
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
is surjective. Then the neutral Picard space
\Pic_{\pi}^{\circ}
is Néron under
B_{1}\subset B
.

Proof.

It is enough to check the Néron mapping property of
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
étale locally in
B
by
Section

3.1
. Since
\pi^{\prime}
is surjective, we may assume
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
has a section. Let
Z\longrightarrow B
be a smooth separated scheme of finite type (
Remark

3.2
). Both
Z
and
X_{Z}=X\times_{B}Z
are smooth varieties with dense open subsets
Z_{1}\subset Z
and
X_{Z_{1}}\subset X_{Z}
with codimension
\geq 2
complements. Hence the restriction homomorphisms

\Pic(X_{Z})\longrightarrow\Pic(X_{Z_{1}}),\qquad\Pic(Z)\longrightarrow\Pic(Z_{1})

are isomorphisms. Since
\pi
has a section, there exist isomorphisms
\Pic_{\pi}(Z)\cong\Pic(X_{Z})/\Pic Z
and
\Pic_{\pi}(Z_{1})\cong\Pic(X_{Z_{1}})/\Pic Z_{1}
by
[
Kle05
, Theorem 9.2.5]
(or Tag 0D28). This yields an isomorphism

\Pic_{\pi}(Z)\longrightarrow\Pic_{\pi}(Z_{1}),

which is the Néron mapping property of
\Pic_{\pi}
under
B_{1}\subset B
. Its neutral component
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is a connected component of
\Pic_{\pi}
so it satisfies the Néron mapping property, too.
∎

6.8
.

In fact, the above proof shows: if a connected component
\Pic^{\alpha}_{\pi}
of
\Pic_{\pi}
dominates
B
, then it is Néron under
B_{1}\subset B
.

The neutral Picard space
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is in general
not
Néron under the extension
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
. However, we can force it to be Néron by taking its separated quotient (this is the strategy in
[
BLR90
, Theorem 9.5.4]
and
[
Hol19
, Theorem 6.2]
). Recall from the introduction of this section that we have the equality
\check{P}_{0}=\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{0}}
.

6.9
.

The dual abelian scheme
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
admits a separated Néron model
\check{P}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
.

Proof.

We construct
\check{P}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
as a separated quotient of
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}\longrightarrow B_{1}
. Take an arbitrary polarization
\check{\lambda}_{0}:\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}
, a finite étale homomorphism of abelian schemes over
B_{0}
. By the Néron mapping property of
P^{a}
under
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
(
Theorem

5.2
), the polarization uniquely extends to a homomorphism

\check{\lambda}_{1}:\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}\longrightarrow P^{a}_{1}.

If
\check{\lambda}_{1}
is étale, then
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}\longrightarrow B_{1}
is aligned by
Section

2.3
.
4
4

4

We caution the reader that
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is not aligned over the entire base
B
in general.
We can therefore consider its separated quotient (
Section

2.3
)

\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{1}.

Recall from
Theorem

4.8
and
6.7
that we have
\Lie P^{a}\cong\Lie\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}\cong\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B}
. To show
\check{\lambda}_{1}
is étale, it is thus enough to show
\Lie\check{\lambda}_{1}:\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}^{*}_{B_{1}}
is an isomorphism. Equivalently, we may show the coherent sheaf homomorphism
\check{\lambda}_{1}^{*}:T_{B_{1}}\longrightarrow T_{B_{1}}
is an isomorphism. Choose a polarization
\lambda_{0}:P_{0}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0}
of
P_{0}
so that the compositions

(6.10)

\hbox to113.91pt{\vbox to19.95pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.9548pt\lower-7.82639pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

are both multiplication endomorphisms by
m
for a positive integer
m
(e.g., dual polarization in
[
BL04
, §14.4]
). Replacing
\lambda_{0}
by
2\lambda_{0}
if necessary, we may assume
\lambda_{0}
is a polarization obtained by a line bundle
L
on
X
(that is
\pi
-ample over
B_{0}
)
[
FC90
, §I.1.6]
. Let us explicitly define an extension of
\lambda_{0}
over
B
by

(6.11)

\lambda:P^{a}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi},\qquad f\longmapsto[f^{*}L\otimes L^{-1}].

Though
\lambda
may
not
be a group homomorphism (see
Remark

6.15
), it sends the identity section to the identity section. Hence we can consider the homomorphism between their conormal sheaves
\lambda^{*}:T_{B}\longrightarrow T_{B}
. Over
B_{0}
, (
6.10
) says the compositions
\lambda_{0}^{*}\circ\check{\lambda}_{0}^{*}
and
\check{\lambda}_{0}^{*}\circ\lambda_{0}^{*}
are both multiplication by
m
endomorphism of
T_{B_{0}}
. Hence both
\lambda_{1}^{*}\circ\check{\lambda}_{1}^{*}
and
\check{\lambda}_{1}^{*}\circ\lambda_{1}^{*}
are forced to be
m\cdot\id
on
T_{B_{1}}
because
T_{B_{1}}
is locally free, proving that
\check{\lambda}_{1}^{*}
is an isomorphism.

It remains to show the Néron mapping property of
\check{P}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
. The uniqueness part is clear from its separatedness, so let us prove the existence using the same strategy to the proof of
Section

6.1
. Assume without loss of generality that
\pi
has a section and let
Z\longrightarrow B_{1}
be a smooth separated scheme of finite type. The base change
X_{Z}
is a smooth variety so the localization exact sequence yields surjective homomorphisms
\Pic(X_{Z})\twoheadrightarrow\Pic(X_{Z_{0}})
and
\Pic Z\twoheadrightarrow\Pic Z_{0}
. This yields a surjective restriction map
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}(Z)\twoheadrightarrow\check{P}_{0}(Z_{0})
. But this map factors through

\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}(Z)\longrightarrow\check{P}_{1}(Z)\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0}(Z_{0}),

so the latter homomorphism
\check{P}_{1}(Z)\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0}(Z_{0})
is a surjection.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

6.1
.

(1) The translation automorphism scheme
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
is of finite type. Hence there exists a sufficiently divisible integer
m
such that the multiplication endomorphism
[m]
on
P^{a}
has the image in its strict neutral component:

(6.12)

[m]:P^{a}\longrightarrow P^{\circ\circ}.

Take an arbitrary polarization
\lambda_{0}:P_{0}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0}
and extend it to an étale homomorphism
\lambda_{1}:P^{\circ\circ}_{1}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{1}
using the Néron mapping property of
\check{P}_{1}
(
Lemma

6.9
). Since
P^{\circ\circ}
has connected fibers, the image of
\lambda_{1}
is contained in the strict neutral component
\check{P}^{\circ\circ}_{1}
. But recall that
\check{P}_{1}
was the separated quotient of
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}
by construction, so we have
\check{P}^{\circ\circ}_{1}=\Pic^{\circ\circ}_{\pi_{1}}\subset\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}
from
Section

2.3
. This means we have a homomorphism
\lambda_{1}:P^{\circ\circ}_{1}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}
over
B_{1}
. Now by the Néron mapping property of
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
under
B_{1}\subset B
(
Section

6.1
) or Raynaud’s extension
theorem

2.13
, this extends to a homomorphism

(6.13)

\lambda:P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}.

The composition of (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) is

(6.14)

f=\lambda\circ[m]:P^{a}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi},

which is a quasi-projective étale homomorphism of smooth commutative group spaces (
Remark

3.18
).
Corollary

3.17
shows
P^{a}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
has a Néron model
P\longrightarrow B
that contains
P^{a}
as an open subgroup scheme. Since
P^{a}
was already Néron under
B_{0}\subset B_{1}
, this shows
P
is Néron under
B_{0}\subset B
.

(2) Take a polarization
\check{\lambda}_{0}:\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow P_{0}
. By the Néron mapping property of
P
, it uniquely extends to an étale homomorphism
\lambda:\Pic^{\circ\circ}_{\pi}\longrightarrow P
. Since
\Pic^{\circ\circ}_{\pi}\longrightarrow B
is separated and of finite type by
Theorem

2.11
,
\lambda
is a quasi-projective étale homomorphism. Again applying
Corollary

3.17
,
\check{P}_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
has a Néron model
\check{P}\longrightarrow B
(whose strict neutral component is
\Pic^{\circ\circ}_{\pi}
). The restriction of
\check{P}
over
B_{1}
coincides with
\check{P}_{1}
in
Lemma

6.9
.

(3) Extension of
\lambda_{0}
to
\lambda:P\longrightarrow\check{P}
is done by the Néron mapping property of
\check{P}
. It is an étale homomorphism since its extension over
B_{1}
is étale as in the proof of
Lemma

6.9
(and then we can use
Section

3.4
). Its kernel
K=\ker\lambda
is Néron under
B_{0}\subset B
by
Section

3.5
. Since
K_{0}=\ker\lambda_{0}
is a finite étale group scheme over
B_{0}
, the quasi-projectiveness of
K
follows from
Theorem

3.11
.

(4)
P
is
\delta
-regular since it contains a
\delta
-regular group scheme
P^{a}
(
Theorem

4.7
) as an open subgroup scheme. To show the
\delta
-regularity of
\check{P}
, it is enough to show their
\delta
-functions agree. Take quasi-finite homomorphisms
\lambda:P\longrightarrow\check{P}
and
\check{\lambda}:\check{P}\longrightarrow P
such that
\lambda\circ\check{\lambda}
and
\check{\lambda}\circ\lambda
are multiplication by
m
endomorphisms. For each
b\in B
, the homomorphism
\lambda_{b}:P^{\circ\circ}_{b}\longrightarrow\check{P}^{\circ\circ}_{b}
is quasi-finite and hence finite by
[
BLR90
, Lemma 7.3.1]
. Therefore, it induces a finite homomorphism
\lambda_{b}:(P^{\circ\circ}_{b})_{\operatorname{aff}}\longrightarrow(\check{P}^{\circ\circ}_{b})_{\operatorname{aff}}
between their maximal affine subgroups. Same argument applies to
\check{\lambda}_{b}
and shows
(P^{\circ\circ}_{b})_{\operatorname{aff}}
and
(\check{P}^{\circ\circ}_{b})_{\operatorname{aff}}
are isogenous. In particular, their dimensions are the same.
∎

6.15
.

Fix a line bundle
L
on
X
and define a morphism

((
6.11
) restated)

\lambda:P^{a}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi},\qquad f\longmapsto\big[f^{*}L\otimes L^{-1}\big].

Over
B_{0}
, it is a homomorphism
\lambda_{0}:P_{0}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{0}
by the theory of abelian schemes. When
\lambda
is a homomorphism over the full base
B
, we say that
L

satisfies the theorem of the square

[
BLR90
, §6.3]
.
5
5

5

[
AF16
, Proposition 5.12]
calls such a line bundle
L
a
cubish line bundle
.
Since
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is Néron under
B_{1}\subset B
, the morphism
\lambda
is a homomorphism over
B
iff it is over
B_{1}
. We do not know how to check this even over
B_{1}
.

To overcome this issue, we introduced the multiplication map
[m]
in the proof of
Theorem

6.1
and considered instead

\lambda\circ[m]:P^{a}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi},\qquad f\longmapsto\big[(f^{*})^{\circ m}(L)\otimes L^{-1}\big]

as in (
6.14
). This is a homomorphism for every line bundle
L
.

We have constructed
\check{P}
as a Néron model of the separated quotient
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{1}
over
B_{1}
. Denote by
\check{E}_{1}
its kernel, or the closure of the identity section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}
, so that we have a short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow\check{E}_{1}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}\longrightarrow\check{P}_{1}\longrightarrow 0
over
B_{1}
. Extend it over
B
using the Néron mapping property to yield a left exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\check{E}\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}\longrightarrow\check{P},

where
\check{E}
is a Néron model of
\check{E}_{1}
by
Section

3.5
(Note:
\check{E}
is
not
the closure of the identity section of
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
). We do not know whether this sequence is right exact, but there is an easy situation when this is the case:

6.15
.

Assume that
\pi
has integral fibers over
B_{1}
. Then
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}=\check{P}
.

Proof.

The integral fiber assumption implies that
\Pic_{\pi_{1}}\longrightarrow B_{1}
is a separated group scheme, thanks to
[
Kle05
, Theorem 9.4.8]
or
[
BLR90
, Theorem 8.2.1]
. This means
\check{E}_{1}=0
and
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi_{1}}=\check{P}_{1}
. Since both
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
and
\check{P}
are Néron under
B_{1}\subset B
, the claim follows from the uniqueness of the Néron model.
∎

6.2.
Proof of the main result

With the Néron model
P
at hand, we can construct the Néron model
X^{n}
in two different ways: either a
P
-torsor or the union of smooth loci for all birational models of the Lagrangian fibration.

Proof of
Theorem

6.5
.

(1) Recall from
Theorem

5.2
that
X^{\prime}_{1}
is a
P_{1}
-torsor over
B_{1}
. This yields a cohomology class
[X_{1}^{\prime}]\in H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B_{1},P)
. The restriction map

H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B,P)\longrightarrow H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B_{1},P)

is injective and contains
X^{\prime}_{1}
in its image by
Section

3.4
. Hence
X^{\prime}_{1}\longrightarrow B_{1}
uniquely extends to a
P
-torsor
X^{n}\longrightarrow B
. It is a Néron model as desired by
Section

3.4
. The equality
X^{\prime}_{1}=X^{n}_{1}
over
B_{1}
uniquely extends to a moprhism
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow X^{n}
, which is an equivariant open immersion by
Lemma

3.28
.

(2) To ease notation, we write
U=B
and shrink
B
whenever needed. Say
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
is a Lagrangian fibered smooth symplectic variety and
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
is a birational map. By
Theorem

5.4
,
f_{1}:X_{1}\longrightarrow Y_{1}
is an isomorphism over
B_{1}
and hence induces an isomorphism between their Néron models
f:X^{n}\longrightarrow Y^{n}
over
B
. We thus have an open immersion
f^{-1}:Y^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y^{n}\cong X^{n}
and hence an open immersion

(6.16)

\bigcup_{f:X\dashrightarrow Y}f^{-1}(Y^{\prime})\subset X^{n}.

Fix a reference section
e:B\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
. Let
a:B\longrightarrow X^{n}
be an arbitrary (local) section. Considering
e
as a section of
X^{n}
, the difference between the two sections define a section
f=e-a:B\longrightarrow P
because
X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor. Recalling that
P
is the birational translation automorphism space, this yields a birational translation automorphism
f:X\dashrightarrow X
. We have constructed a commutative diagram

\hbox to108.87pt{\vbox to38.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 54.43219pt\lower-19.33725pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-54.43219pt}{-15.67752pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -75.32 -21.69)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

An alternative interpretation of this diagram is that the birational automorphism
f:X\dashrightarrow Y\coloneq X
to a Lagrangian fibered symplectic variety
\tau\coloneq\pi:Y\longrightarrow B
sends a rational section
a
of
X^{\prime}
to a regular section
e
of
Y^{\prime}
. In other words, we have a commutative diagram

This means the section
a:B\longrightarrow X^{n}
is contained in an open subscheme
f^{-1}(Y^{\prime})\subset X^{n}
. Varying the arbitrary section
a
, the open immersion (
6.16
) is an equality.
∎

The following is a more comprehensive version of
Section

6
(3). Compare it with
Lemma

5.7
.

6.16
.

Assume
\dim X=2n=4
. Then all the following are equivalent.

(1)

X^{\prime}=X^{n}
, or equivalently

(1n)

X^{\prime}
is Néron under
B_{0}\subset B
.

(1t)

X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor over
B
.

(1f)

X^{n}\longrightarrow B
is of finite type.

(1s)

X^{n}\longrightarrow B
is separated.

(2)

P^{\tau}=P
, or equivalently

(2f)

P\longrightarrow B
is of finite type.

(2s)

P\longrightarrow B
is separated.

(3)

\check{P}^{\tau}=\check{P}
, or equivalently

(3f)

\check{P}\longrightarrow B
is of finite type.

(3s)

\check{P}\longrightarrow B
is separated.

Proof.

(1)
\Longleftrightarrow
(1n)
\Longleftrightarrow
(1t) is clear from
Theorem

6.5
(1) that
X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor and
X^{\prime}\subset X^{n}
. (1f)
\Longleftrightarrow
(2f) and (1s)
\Longleftrightarrow
(2s) are again clear since
X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor. (2)
\Longleftrightarrow
(3) , (2f)
\Longleftrightarrow
(3f), and (2s)
\Longleftrightarrow
(3s) follow from the existence of quasi-projective étale homomorphisms
\lambda:P\longrightarrow\check{P}
and
\check{\lambda}:\check{P}\longrightarrow P
in
Theorem

6.1
. Therefore, we may show the equivalence between (1), (2), (2f), and (2s). (1)
\Longrightarrow
(2f) follows since
X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor. (2f)
\Longrightarrow
(2) is clear from definition of the numerically trivial subgroup
P^{\tau}
. (2)
\Longrightarrow
(2s) is a consequence of
Section

2.4
and
3.6.1
.

It remains to show (2s)
\Longrightarrow
(1), and this is the only part we use the assumption
\dim X=4
. Assume that
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
is not Néron under
B_{0}\subset B
.
Lemma

5.7
implies that (after shrinking
B
) there exists a birational translation automorphism
X\dashrightarrow X
whose restriction to
X^{\prime}\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
is not a regular automorphism. Such a birational map decomposes into a sequence of flops as in (
5.9
). When
\dim X=4
, it further decomposes into a sequence of
Mukai flops
by
[
WW03
, Theorem 1.2]
.

We may thus consider a single Mukai flop
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
arising in the sequence whose induced map
f:X^{\prime}\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
is not an isomorphism. The flopping
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and flopped
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
are contained in fibers
X_{b}
and
Y_{b}
for a closed point
b\in B
. Since
f:X^{\prime}\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
is not an isomorphism, both
\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset X_{b}
and
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\subset Y_{b}
are reduced components by
Lemma

5.15
. Consider a section
a:B\longrightarrow X^{\prime}
passing through the reduced component
\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset X_{b}
(after shrinking
B
). The closure of the rational section
B\longrightarrow X\dashrightarrow Y
has a fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
over
b
since
f
is a Mukai flop, and its intersection with
Y^{\prime}
is nonempty because
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\subset Y_{b}
is a reduced component. As a result, the closure of the rational section
B\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
has a
1
-dimensional fiber over
b
.

Finally, the varieties
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
admit open immersions to
P
by
Lemma

3.28
:
