If
P\longrightarrow B
is separated, then the section
B\longrightarrow P
is a closed immersion, violating the previous conclusion that the closure of the rational section
a:B\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
has a
1
-dimensional fiber over
b
. Hence
P\longrightarrow B
cannot be separated.
∎

6.17
.

The assumption
\dim X=4
in
Section

6.2
is used to produce a Mukai flop (so that we can use
Lemma

5.15
). We expect a variant of
[
CMSB02
, Theorem 9.1]
holds for Lagrangian fibrations, extending the result of
Section

5.2
, so that our strategy works for arbitrary dimensional symplectic varieties as well. We did not pursue this direction further.

Proof of
Section

6
.

(1) Let
b\in B\setminus B_{\tor}
be a point. That is, assume that
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
fails to be Néron at
b\in B
. By
Lemma

5.7
, up to shrinking
B
there exists a birational map
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
to
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
such that
f:X^{\prime}\dashrightarrow Y^{\prime}
has an undefined locus over
b
. By Kawamata’s decomposition theorem in (
5.9
) and
Section

5.2
, a flop arises over an irreducible component
T\subset D_{d}
of a
\delta
-locus containing
b
. This means
b\in T\subset B\setminus B_{\tor}
where
T\subset D_{d}
is an irreducible component. That is,
B\setminus B_{\tor}
is a finite union of irreducible components of the
\delta
-loci
D_{d}
’s.

(2)
X^{\prime}_{1}=X^{n}_{1}
implies
B_{1}\subset B_{\tor}
. Since
B_{\tor}
is an open subset of
B
, we can restrict to
B_{\tor}
and say
X^{\prime}_{\tor}=X^{n}_{\tor}
is separated over
B_{\tor}
. This means
B_{\tor}\subset B_{\sep}
. (3) was already proved in
Section

6.2
. (4) will be presented in §
6.3
.
∎

We conclude with the following weaker versions of
Section

6.2

6.17
.

If
P^{a}=P
then
X^{\prime}=X^{n}
.

Proof.

Immediate from
Lemma

5.7
.
∎

6.17
.

Assume that
\pi
has integral fibers over
B_{1}
. Then
P^{\tau}=P
if and only if
X^{\prime}=X^{n}
.

Proof.

The implication
(\Longleftarrow)
was proved in
Section

6.2
, so let us prove
(\Longrightarrow)
. Recall from
Section

6.1
that we have
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}=\check{P}
under such assumption. If we further assume that
P^{\tau}=P
, then
\check{P}^{\tau}=\check{P}
by the equivalence (2)
\Longleftrightarrow
(3) in
Section

6.2
and hence
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is numerically trivial.

Let
L
be a
\pi
-ample line bundle on
X
and consider the morphism

\lambda:P\longrightarrow\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi},\qquad f\longmapsto[f^{*}L\otimes L^{-1}],

similarly to (
6.11
) (first define it over
B_{1}
using
Theorem

5.4
and then extend it over
B
by the Néron mapping property of
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
). But
\Pic^{\circ}_{\pi}
is numerically trivial, so
f^{*}L\otimes L^{-1}
is a numerically trivial line bundle and hence
f^{*}L
is
\pi
-ample by the Nakai–Moishezon criterion. This shows that every
f\in P
is a regular translation automorphism. In other words,
P^{a}=P
and hence
X^{\prime}=X^{n}
by
Section

6.2
.
∎

6.3.
Examples and counterexamples

We present three examples that are illustrative to understand the behaviors of
P
and its subgroup spaces in (
6.4
). We will freely use our main results.

6.3.1.
First example: product of minimal elliptic surfaces

Let

\pi^{i}:S^{i}\longrightarrow\Delta\qquad\mbox{for}\quad i=1,\cdots,n

be proper minimal elliptic surfaces with no multiple fibers over a smooth quasi-projective curve
\Delta
. Consider a Lagrangian fibration of a smooth symplectic variety

\pi:X=S^{1}\times\cdots\times S^{n}\longrightarrow B=\Delta^{\times n}.

By Néron–Raynaud (or
Theorem

5.2
), each
S^{i}
has a translation automorphism scheme
(P^{i})^{a}=P^{i}\longrightarrow\Delta
making
(S^{i})^{\prime}
its torsor. By
Section

3.1
, both
P=P^{1}\times\cdots\times P^{n}\longrightarrow B
and
X^{\prime}=(S^{1})^{\prime}\times\cdots\times(S^{n})^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
are Néron under
B_{0}=\Delta_{0}^{\times n}\subset B
. Moreover,
P
regularly acts on the full symplectic variety
X
, so this shows

P^{\circ\circ}\subset P^{a}=P^{\tau}=P,

and that
X^{\prime}=X^{n}
from
Section

6.2
. The first inclusion is strict when
S^{i}
has a singular fiber with at least two integral components.

6.3.2.
Second example: Hilbert square of an elliptic surface

Let
S\longrightarrow\Delta
be a proper minimal elliptic surface over a smooth quasi-projective curve with a central singular fiber
S_{t_{0}}
of Kodaira type
\text{I}_{2}
over
t_{0}\in\Delta
. Consider a morphism

\pi:X=S^{[2]}\longrightarrow\bar{X}=S^{(2)}\longrightarrow B=\Delta^{(2)},

where
S^{[2]}
is the Hilbert scheme of length
2
subschemes on
S
, and
S^{(2)}=S^{\times 2}/\mathfrak{S}_{2}
and
\Delta^{(2)}=\Delta^{\times 2}/\mathfrak{S}_{2}
are symmetric squares. Note that
X
is a smooth symplectic fourfold,
B
is a smooth surface, and
\pi
is a Lagrangian fibration. The complement of the discriminant locus is

B_{0}=\{(t,s)\in\Delta_{0}^{(2)}:t\neq s\}\subset B.

We claim
P^{a}=P
, i.e., every (local) birational translation automorphism is regular. By the previous example in §
6.3.1
, we only need to consider the case around the diagonal in
B
. Shrinking
\Delta
, we may consider a birational translation
f:X\dashrightarrow X
over
B=\Delta^{(2)}
. It induces a birational translation
\bar{f}:\bar{X}\dashrightarrow\bar{X}
, which again induces an
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
-equivariant birational translation

g:S^{\times 2}\dashrightarrow S^{\times 2}\quad\mbox{over}\quad\Delta^{\times 2}.

Again by the previous example, every birational translation of
S^{\times 2}\longrightarrow\Delta^{\times 2}
is regular, so this shows
g
and hence
\bar{f}
is a regular automorphism. Therefore, the undefined locus of
f
is contained in the exceptional divisor
E
of
X=S^{[2]}\longrightarrow\bar{X}=S^{(2)}
. By
[
WW03
, Theorem 1.1]
, the undefined locus must be a disjoint union of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
’s. But the exceptional divisor
E
contains no
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in a single fiber because it is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundle over
S
. Hence
f
is defined everywhere. We conclude

P^{\circ\circ}\subsetneq P^{a}=P^{\tau}=P.

In particular,
X^{\prime}=X^{n}
by
Section

6
(or
Section

6.2
). To show the first inclusion is strict, notice that the fiber over
b=(t_{0},t_{0})\in B=\Delta^{(2)}
is

X_{b}\cong\big(\mathbb{P}^{1}\mbox{-bundle over }S_{t_{0}}\big)\cup 2\cdot\big(\mathbb{P}^{2}\cup(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{\times 2}\cup\mathbb{P}^{2}\big),

where the
2
indicates that the three irreducible components in
X_{b}
are non-reduced with multiplicity
2
(as a cycle). Hence
X^{\prime}_{b}
has two connected components and so does
P^{a}_{b}
.

Notice that the fiber
X_{b}
above contains two irreducible components isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Muaki flopping one
\mathbb{P}^{2}
yields a new smooth symplectic algebraic space
X\dashrightarrow Y
with a Lagrangian fibration
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
. Recall that
P^{\circ\circ}
,
P^{\tau}
, and
P
are birational invariants. However, we have lost the
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
-symmetry on the two irreducible components isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
’s in the fiber
Y_{b}
, so this means for the new Lagrangian fibration
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
, we have

P^{\circ\circ}=P^{a}\subsetneq P^{\tau}=P.

This modified example still satisfies
P^{\tau}=P
, so
Y^{\prime}=Y^{n}
is a
P
-torsor by
Section

6
. The reason for this phenomenon is because the Mukai flopped
\mathbb{P}^{2}
has multiplicity
2
, so the birational map
X\dashrightarrow Y
still induces an isomorphism
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow Y^{\prime}
and the Néron mapping property is preserved by
Lemma

5.7
. If we can flop a
reduced
irreducible component
\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
X_{b}
, then this will no longer be the case and the torsor property will fail. This leads us to the third example.

6.3.3.
Third example: Beauville–Mukai system of a degree
2
K3 surface

We thank Antonio Rapagnetta for pointing out an example in
[
MRV17
, Example 3.11]
,
[
OS79
, §13 Example (3)]
, and
[
Mum72
, p.271]
. Let us first present two preparatory lemmas.

6.18
.

Assume
X^{\prime}
is a
G
-torsor for a smooth group space
G\longrightarrow B
. Then
G
is an open subgroup space of
P^{\tau}
.

Proof.

Since
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
is finite type and separated, so is
G\longrightarrow B
by descent. Note that
X^{\prime}_{1}
is both a
G_{1}
-torsor and
P_{1}
-torsor by
Theorem

5.2
. This forces
G
and
P
to be isomorphic over
B_{1}
(see
[
MB18
]
). By the Néron mapping property of
P
under
B_{1}\subset B
, this isomorphism extends to an open immersion
G\hookrightarrow P
(
Section

3.4
). Since
P^{\tau}
is a maximal numerically trivial subgroup space of
P
, the immersion factors though
P^{\tau}
.
∎

6.19
.

Assume
\dim X=2n=4
. Say a closed fiber
X_{b}
contains a reduced component isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and another (not necessarily reduced) irreducible component
S
with
S\cap\mathbb{P}^{2}\neq\varnothing
. Let
f:X\dashrightarrow X^{+}
be the Mukai flop of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\subset X^{+}_{b}
the flopped locus, and
S^{+}\subset X^{+}_{b}
the strict transform of
S
. Then

(1)

S\cap\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
S^{+}\cap\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}\subset\check{\mathbb{P}}^{2}
are projective dual to each other.

(2)

Every irreducible component of
S\cap\mathbb{P}^{2}
is either a point or a line in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Moreover, it can contain at most three points and three lines.

Proof.

(1) By
[
Mat00
, Theorem 1]
, both
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
S_{\red}
are Lagrangian subvarieties of
X
. Therefore, this is a consequence of
[
Leu02
, §4.2]
, where the projective dual is called the Legendre transformation.

(2) Since
\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset X_{b}
is an integral component,
\mathbb{P}^{2}\cap X^{\prime}_{b}
is isomorphic to
P^{\circ\circ}_{b}
, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\times 2}
,
\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{G}_{a}
, or
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\times 2}
. This means every
1
-dimensional irreducible component of
S\cap\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a line, and there are at most three of them. If there is a
0
-dimensional irreducible component, applying the same argument to the Mukai flop says there are at most three of them.
∎

Consider a degree
2
K3 surface
\varphi:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{2}
branched along a smooth sextic curve in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Choose the sextic sufficiently so that it meets with a line
\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
along precisely three points with multiplicity
2
. The preimage

\varphi^{-1}(\mathbb{P}^{1})=C_{1}\cup C_{2}

is a union of two smooth rational curves with three transversal intersections.

Let
L=\varphi^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(1)
be an ample line bundle of degree
2
and set
v=(0,L,1)\in H^{*}(S,\mathbb{Z})
, a primitive Mukai vector. The Beauville–Mukai system

\pi:X=M_{v}(S)\longrightarrow B=|L|\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}

is a Lagrangian fibration of a projective hyper-Kähler fourfold of
\text{K3}^{[2]}
-type. Over the closed point
b=[C_{1}\cup C_{2}]\in B
,
\pi
has a
reduced
fiber with three irreducible components

X_{b}=S_{1}\cup S_{2}\cup S_{3}.

The two surfaces
S_{1}
and
S_{3}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
S_{2}
is a projective surface that normalizes to a degree
6
del Pezzo surface
\tilde{S}_{2}\longrightarrow S_{2}
(that is,
\tilde{S}_{2}
is isomorphic to a blowup of three non-collinear points in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
). More precisely, there are six distinct points
p_{i},q_{i}\in\tilde{S}_{2}
for
i=1,2,3
so that
S_{2}
is precisely
\tilde{S}_{2}
with
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
identified. The interesting configuration of
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
, and
S_{3}
is well-described in
[
Mum72
, p.271]
and
[
OS79
, Figure 35 in p.84]
. We simply recall that
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
consists of three lines in
S_{1}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and similarly
S_{2}\cap S_{3}
consists of three lines in
S_{3}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
. The intersection
S_{1}\cap S_{3}
consists of three points (compare with
Lemma

6.19
).

We imitate the method in
[
HT10
, Theorem 6.2]
to construct an infinite sequence of Mukai flops. Perform a Mukai flop of
S_{1}\cong\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset X
and yield a smooth symplectic algebraic space
X\dashrightarrow X^{+}
. It has a fiber

X_{b}^{+}=S_{1}^{+}\cup S_{2}^{+}\cup S_{3}^{+},

where
S_{1}^{+}
is the flopped locus, and
S_{2}^{+}
and
S_{3}^{+}
are strict transforms of
S_{2}
and
S_{3}
. Here are the more precise descriptions of
S_{i}^{+}
:

•

S_{1}^{+}
is a flopped locus, so it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and is a reduced component of
X_{b}^{+}
(
Lemma

5.15
).

•

Consider the Mukai flop diagram
X\longrightarrow\bar{X}\longleftarrow X^{+}
. The image
\bar{S}_{2}\subset\bar{X}
of
S_{2}
is a contraction of three lines in
S_{2}
and hence isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with three points
p,q,r\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
identified. By
Lemma

6.19
, the strict transform
S_{2}^{+}
separates this point to three different points, the projective dual of three lines in
S_{2}
, so it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

•

This time, consider the roof diagram
X\longleftarrow\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X^{+}
. The strict transform
\tilde{S}_{3}\subset\tilde{X}
is the blowup of
S_{3}
along three points
S_{1}\cap S_{3}\subset S_{3}
, so it is isomorphic to a degree
6
del Pezzo surface. Its image
S_{3}^{+}
under the contraction
\tilde{X}\longrightarrow X^{+}
identifies three pairs of points.

Therefore, the configuration of
X_{b}^{+}
looks similar to
X_{b}
but the roles of each irreducible components are shuffled. Now
S_{1}^{+}
and
S_{2}^{+}
are the two components of
X_{b}^{+}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Flopping
S_{1}^{+}
will yield back the original
X
, so the only nontrivial Mukai flop is the flop of
S_{2}^{+}
. This gives another smooth symplectic algebraic space
X^{+}\dashrightarrow X^{++}
whose fiber is

X_{b}^{++}=S_{1}^{++}\cup S_{2}^{++}\cup S_{3}^{++}.

Similar argument shows that
S_{1}^{++}
is now a degree
6
del Pezzo surface (with three pairs of points identified), and
S_{2}^{++}
and
S_{3}^{++}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Flop
S_{3}^{++}
again and yield
X^{+++}
, and keep going. We have constructed an infinite sequence of Mukai flops.

L
on each component

Table 2.
Infinite sequence of Mukai flops and the degree of
L
on the three irreducible components of their fibers over
b
.

6.20
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, the composition of
k
Mukai flops

X\dashrightarrow X^{+}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow X^{+k}

as above is not an isomorphism.

Proof.

Take an arbitrary
\pi
-ample line bundle
L
on
X
. Since
S_{1}
and
S_{3}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, their Picard groups are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
. Let
a>0
and
b>0
be the degree of
L_{|S_{1}}
and
L_{|S_{3}}
, respectively. That is, if
l
is a line in
S_{1}=\mathbb{P}^{2}
then
a
is the intersection number of
L
and
l
(similarly for
b
). Since
\tilde{S}_{2}
is a degree
6
del Pezzo surface, its Picard group is
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
and we can compute the intersection numbers of
L
with the rational curve generators of
\Pic S_{2}
.

Let us now consider the strict transform of
L
to
X^{+}
and denote it by the same letter
L
for simplicity. Since
X^{+}
was the Mukai flop of
S_{1}\subset X
, the degree of
L_{|S_{1}^{+}}
becomes
-a
. The strict transform
S_{2}^{+}
is now isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, so we can consider the degree of
L_{|S_{2}^{+}}
. It is computed by the configuration of six lines on the degree
6
del Pezzo surface
S_{2}
and their contraction; one can check the degree is precisely
2a+b
. After another flop, we can play the same game and compute the degree of
L
restricted to each irreducible component of the fiber
X^{++}_{b}
. This calculation shows that on the
k
-th step
X^{+k}
, the degree of
L
on two
\mathbb{P}^{2}
components of the fiber
X^{+k}_{b}
are (see
Table

2
)

(k+1)a+kb>0\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad-ka-(k-1)b<0.

Since there is a negative degree component,
L
cannot be
\pi
-ample on
X^{+k}
. Therefore, the composition of a series of Mukai flops
X\dashrightarrow X^{+k}
will never be an isomorphism for every
k
.
∎

We claim that the sequence of inclusions (
6.4
) for this example is

(6.21)

P^{\circ\circ}=P^{a}=P^{\tau}\subsetneq P.

The last strict inclusion can be shown as in the proof of
Section

6.2
. Namely, take two birational models
X\dashrightarrow X^{+}
that are connected by a single Mukai flop. By
Theorem

6.5
, their smooth parts are open subschemes of
X^{n}
and we have a commutative diagram

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.39998pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 29.07 2.45 L 10.39 -8.19}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.86888}{0.49503}{-0.49503}{0.86888}{21.00786pt}{1.7727pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(0.86888 0.49503 -0.49503 0.86888 29.07 2.45)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=miter} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 2.71 C 0.95 2.71 1.72 2.1 1.72 1.36 C 1.72 0.61 0.95 0 0 0}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{-0.86888}{-0.49503}{0.49503}{-0.86888}{7.3377pt}{-6.01544pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(-0.86888 -0.49503 0.49503 -0.86888 10.15 -8.32)} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray=none,stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap=round} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin=round} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

This forces
X^{n}\longrightarrow B
to be non-separated by
Lemma

5.15
and hence
P^{\tau}\subsetneq P
by
Section

6
. Equivalently,
X^{\prime}\subsetneq X^{n}
or
X^{\prime}
is not a
P
-torsor.

To show the equality
P^{a}=P^{\tau}
, let
f
be a local section of
P^{\tau}
around
b
. That is, shrinking
B
,
f:X\dashrightarrow X
is a birational translation automorphism whose self-composition
f^{\circ m}:X\dashrightarrow X
is a regular automorphism for some
m\geq 1
. By
[
WW03
, Theorem 1.2]
,
f
decomposes into a sequence of Mukai flops

f:X\dashrightarrow X^{(1)}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow X^{(t)}\xlongrightarrow{\cong}X.

Since
f
is an isomorphism over
B_{1}\subset B
by
Theorem

5.4
, shrinking
B
, we may assume that the Mukai flops are over
b\in B
. There is only one possible sequence of Mukai flops we can take, which is
X\dashrightarrow X^{+}\dashrightarrow X^{++}\dashrightarrow\cdots
as above. By
Lemma

6.20
,
f^{\circ m}
will never be an isomorphism unless
f
was an isomorphism at the beginning. This proves
P^{a}=P^{\tau}
. Finally,
P^{a}_{b}
cannot have multiple connected components because it acts freely on
X^{\prime}_{b}
and not all three components of
X_{b}
are isomorphic. This proves
P^{\circ\circ}=P^{a}
.

We have shown that
X^{\prime}
is not a
P
-torsor. Assume that there
exists
a smooth group space
G\longrightarrow B
making
X^{\prime}
a
G
-torsor.
Lemma

6.18
shows
G
must be an open subgroup space of
P^{\tau}
. Because
P^{\tau}=P^{\circ\circ}
has connected fibers by (
6.21
), this forces
G=P^{\circ\circ}
. Now
X^{\prime}_{b}
had three connected components whereas
G_{b}=P^{\circ\circ}_{b}
has one, which is a contradiction. This justifies
Section

6
.

6.4.
Applications

Let us finally present applications of our main results and their relations to several known results in literature.

6.4.1.
Arinkin–Fedorov’s result: the elliptic locus

The following is slightly stronger than
[
AF16
, Theorem 2]
.

6.22
Arinkin–Fedorov.

If every fiber of
\pi
is integral, then the inclusions in (
6.4
) are equalities:

P^{\circ\circ}=P^{a}=P^{\tau}=P.

As a result,
X^{\prime}=X^{n}
is a
P
-torsor.

Proof.

Say
f:X\dashrightarrow X
is a birational translation automorphism over
B
(after shrinking
B
locally). By Kawamata,
f
decomposes to finite sequence of flops as in (
5.9
). For a single flop (
5.11
), every irreducible component of the flopping locus has to be an irreducible component of
\pi^{-1}(T)
where
T\subset D_{d}
is an irreducible component (
Section

5.2
). But if the fibers of
\pi
are (geometrically) integral, then
\pi^{-1}(T)
is integral and hence this cannot be contracted to
\bar{X}
in (
5.11
) (because the fiber dimension function of
\bar{X}\longrightarrow B
should be upper-semicontinuous). This means
f
is an automorphism, showing
P^{a}=P
. Now
X^{\prime}
admits a free
P^{a}
-action but
X^{\prime}
has connected fibers by assumption, so
P^{a}
also has to have connected fibers. This shows
P^{\circ\circ}=P^{a}
.
∎

An alternative interpretation of this result is
B_{\elliptic}\subset B_{\tor}
, where the former is the open locus over which the fiber of
\pi
is geometrically integral (see
[
EGA $IV˙3$
, Theorem 12.2.4]
) and the latter is as in
Definition

6.6
.

6.4.2.
Domain of definition of local rational sections

Let
U\longrightarrow B
be an étale or smooth morphism and
a:U\dashrightarrow X
be an
(étale or smooth) local rational section
of the Lagrangian fibration. The Néron mapping property of
X^{\prime}\longrightarrow B
under
B_{0}\subset B_{\tor}
shows
a
is necessarily defined on
U_{\tor}=U\times_{B}B_{\tor}
.

6.22
.

Let
U\longrightarrow B
be a smooth morphism. Then every rational map
U\dashrightarrow X
over
B
is necessarily defined over
B_{\tor}
. ∎

In particular, since
B_{\tor}
contains
B_{1}
and
B_{\elliptic}
, every local rational section is necessarily defined over both
B_{1}
and
B_{\elliptic}
. Here is an alternative version: for every local rational section
U\dashrightarrow X
, there exists a birational model
f:X\dashrightarrow Y
such that the composition
f\circ a:U\dashrightarrow Y
is regular.

6.4.3.
Hwang–Oguiso’s result: codimension
1
fibers

We have already discussed this in
Section

5.1.1
and
5.13
, so let us be short. In
[
HO09
,
HO11
]
, Hwang–Oguiso studied the structure of general singular fibers of
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
. Let
b
be a general closed point in the discriminant locus and
X_{b}=\pi^{-1}(b)
. In loc. cit., two sets of vector fields in
X_{b}
are defined, a twisted vector field
\lambda
and Hamiltonian vector fields
\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{n-1}
. The integration of
\lambda
and
\xi_{i}
’s yields the notion of a characteristic cycle and a
\mathbb{C}^{n-1}
-action on
X_{b}
, which are crucial to understand the structure of
X_{b}
in the paper. Let us now consider the Chevalley short exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}\longrightarrow P^{\circ\circ}_{b}\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mathbb{G}
is a connected algebraic group of dimension
1
and
A
is an abelian variety of dimension
n-1
. The characteristic cycle is a connected sequence of closures of
\mathbb{G}
-orbits, and the
\mathbb{C}^{n-1}
-action is the
\Lie A\cong\mathbb{C}^{n-1}
-action on
X_{b}
. This explains why their method is related to the group
P^{\circ\circ}
.

6.4.4.
Abasheva–Rogov’s result: the Tate–Shafarevich group

In
[
AR21
]
, Abasheva–Rogov studied the
Tate–Shafarevich group
of a higher-dimensional Lagrangian fibration (based on work of
[
Mar14
, §7]
) and its relations with the degenerate twistor deformation in
[
Ver15
,
BDV22
]
. Abasheva–Rogov’s definition of the Tate–Shafarevich group is (in our terminology)

\Sha^{\circ\circ}_{an}(B)=H^{1}_{an}(B,P^{\circ\circ}).

The definition of Tate–Shafarevich group is not uniform in literature (e.g., Markman’s paper or
[
HM23
]
). We propose to study several groups together as follows.

6.23
.

The
strict neutral, regular, numerically trivial, and birational Tate–Shafarevich groups
of
\pi
are the first étale cohomology of the groups in (
6.4
):

\Sha^{*}(B)=\Sha^{*}(B,\pi)=H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B,P^{*})\qquad\mbox{for}\quad*=\circ\circ,\ a,\ \tau,\mbox{ or }\varnothing.

Their analytic versions are similarly defined:

\Sha^{*}_{an}(B)=\Sha^{*}_{an}(B,\pi)=H^{1}_{an}(B,P^{*})\qquad\mbox{for}\quad*=\circ\circ,\ a,\ \tau,\mbox{ or }\varnothing.

Note that
Theorem

6.5
gives a natural element

[X^{n}]\ \ \in\ \Sha(B)=H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B,P).

A study of these groups and their relations should be done more comprehensively in separate work. Let us present some basic observations to illustrate the interactions of these groups.

6.23
Torsion.

(1)

\Sha(B)
is torsion.

(2)

If
P^{\tau}=P
then
\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)
and
\Sha^{a}(B)
are both torsion.

Proof.

(1) We thank Olivier Benoist and James Hotchkiss for helping the following argument. Notice the inclusion

\Sha(B)=H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B,P)\ \subset\ \Sha(B_{0})=H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B_{0},P_{0})

from
Section

3.4
. Let us further claim
H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B_{0},P_{0})\subset H^{1}(K,P_{K})
where
K
is the function field of
B
. Indeed, if a
P_{0}
-torsor
N_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
admits a
K
-rational point then its Zariski closure
T\subset N_{0}
defines a proper birational morphism
T\longrightarrow B_{0}
. Its positive-dimensional fiber is rationally chain connected by
[
HM07
, Corollary 1.5]
, violating the fact that fibers of
N_{0}\longrightarrow B_{0}
are abelian varieties. Hence
T\longrightarrow B_{0}
is an isomorphism by Zariski’s main theorem, proving the desired inclusion. Now
H^{1}(K,P_{K})
is torsion since every
P_{K}
-torsor is projective by
[
BLR90
, Theorem 6.4.1]
and projective torsors correspond to torsion elements by
[
Ray70
, Proposition XIII.2.3]
.

(2) It is enough to show the following two separate statements:

•

\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)\longrightarrow\Sha^{a}(B)
has a finite kernel and cokernel.

•

\Sha^{a}(B)\longrightarrow\Sha^{\tau}(B)
has a torsion kernel and cokernel.

For the first item, let
\epsilon=\pi_{0}(P^{a})
be the
\pi_{0}
-group (§
2.4
) and take the long exact sequence of
0\longrightarrow P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow P^{a}\longrightarrow\epsilon\longrightarrow 0
. Since
P^{a}
is quasi-projective,
\epsilon
is an étale constructible sheaf (of finite abelian groups) and hence
H^{i}(B,\epsilon)
is finite for all
i\geq 0
. For the second one, take the long exact sequence of
0\longrightarrow P^{a}\longrightarrow P^{\tau}\longrightarrow\epsilon\longrightarrow 0
and observe that
\epsilon
is an étale sheaf of torsion abelian groups.
∎

6.23
Tate–Shafarevich twist; Markman
[
Mar14
]
, Abasheva–Rogov
[
AR21
]
.

For each
\alpha\in\Sha^{a}(B)
, there exists a proper Lagrangian fibration
\pi^{(\alpha)}:X^{(\alpha)}\longrightarrow B
from a smooth algebraic space
X^{(\alpha)}
, called a
Tate–Shafarevich twist
of
\pi
.

Proof.

This is the construction in the paragraph after
[
Mar14
, Observation 1.4]
or
[
AR21
, §2.2]
. We copy their construction for classes in
\Sha^{a}(B)
(same works for
\Sha^{a}_{an}(B)
). A class
\alpha\in\Sha^{a}(B)=H^{1}_{\acute{e}t}(B,P^{a})
is representable by a Čech cocycle
\{f_{ij}\in P^{a}(U_{ij})\}
where
B^{*}=\bigsqcup_{i}U_{i}\longrightarrow B
is a covering in étale topology,
B^{**}=\bigsqcup_{ij}U_{ij}\longrightarrow B
for
U_{ij}=U_{i}\times_{B}U_{j}
, and
B^{***}=B^{*}\times_{B}B^{*}\times_{B}B^{*}
. We may consider
X\longrightarrow B
as a descent of
X^{*}\longrightarrow B^{*}
along
B^{*}\longrightarrow B
with a descent datum
\varphi:p_{1}^{*}X^{*}\longrightarrow p_{2}^{*}X^{*}
satisfying a cocycle condition.

Since
P^{a}
is a translation automorphism scheme,
f_{ij}:X_{ij}\longrightarrow X_{ij}
is a translation automorphism over
U_{ij}
and hence induces an isomorphism
f:(X^{(\alpha)})^{*}\coloneq X^{*}\longrightarrow X^{*}
over
B^{*}
. Define a twisted isomorphism
\varphi^{(\alpha)}
as follows:

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.62987pt}{-10.10602pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.64 -13.98)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The cocycle condition of
f_{ij}
implies the cocyle condition of the descent datum
\varphi^{(\alpha)}
, proving the existence of a descent
\pi^{(\alpha)}:X^{(\alpha)}\longrightarrow B
. We add three remarks:

•

The Tate–Shafarevich twist corresponding to
0\in\Sha^{a}(B)
is
\pi^{(0)}=\pi
.

•

If
\alpha,\beta\in\Sha^{a}(B)
or
\Sha^{a}_{an}(B)
, then
\pi^{(\alpha)}\cong\pi^{(\beta)}
if and only if
\alpha=\beta
. This is because a translation isomorphism
h:X^{(\alpha)}\longrightarrow X^{(\beta)}
corresponds to a coboundary condition of
\alpha=(f_{ij})
and
\beta=(g_{ij})
.

•

The homomorphism
\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)\longrightarrow\Sha^{a}(B)
may not be injective, so it is better to think about the Tate–Shafarevich twist for elements in
\Sha^{a}(B)
instead of
\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)
.∎

6.23
Analytification.

Assume further that
B
is proper. Then the analytification homomorphisms have the following properties.

(1)

\Sha^{a}(B)\longrightarrow\Sha^{a}_{an}(B)
is injective.

(2)

\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)\longrightarrow\Sha^{\circ\circ}_{an}(B)
is injective and induces
\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)_{\tor}=\Sha^{\circ\circ}_{an}(B)_{\tor}
.

Proof.

(1) An element
\alpha\in\Sha^{a}(B)
defines a Tate–Shafarevich twist
\pi^{(\alpha)}:X^{(\alpha)}\longrightarrow B
by
Section

6.4.4
, which is a flat proper algebraic space. If
X
and
X^{(\alpha)}
are proper varieties that are analytically isomorphic, they are algebraically isomorphic by GAGA.

(2) Consider the
\pi_{0}
-group short exact sequence
0\longrightarrow P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow P^{a}\longrightarrow\epsilon\longrightarrow 0
and compare its étale and analytic long exact sequences

The second vertical arrow is bijective because
\epsilon
is an étale constructible sheaf. Since
P^{a}\longrightarrow B
is quasi-projective, we may consider an open immersion into a projective morphism
P^{a}\hookrightarrow\overline{P^{a}}\longrightarrow B
. Now every analytic section
B\longrightarrow P^{a}
induces an analytic section
B\longrightarrow\overline{P^{a}}
, which is algebraic by GAGA. This means the first vertical arrow is bijective. The injectivity of the third arrow now follows from the four lemma (diagram chasing).

For the second statement, we prove the
m
-torsion parts of
\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)
and
\Sha^{\circ\circ}_{an}(B)
agree. Consider the multiplication by
m
endomorphism on
P^{\circ\circ}
and its kernel
K
. Since
P^{\circ\circ}
is quasi-projective,
K
is an étale constructible sheaf of finite abelian groups. Compare the étale and analytic long exact sequences of
0\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow 0
:

The left vertical arrow is an isomorphism by the Artin comparison theorem, showing the isomorphism of
m
-torsion parts
\Sha^{\circ\circ}(B)[m]=\Sha^{\circ\circ}_{an}(B)[m]
. Same argument yields
\Sha^{a}(B)[m]=\Sha^{a}_{an}(B)[m]
for every
m
coprime to the orders of
\pi_{0}(P^{a})
.
∎

6.4.5.
de Cataldo–Rapagnetta–Saccà’s result

In
[
CRS21
]
, de Cataldo–Rapagnetta–Saccà analyzed the singular fibers of two different but related Lagrangian fibrations of projective hyper-Kähler manifolds. Let
S
be a projective K3 surface with
\Pic(S)=\mathbb{Z}H
for an ample line bundle
H
and consider two Mukai vectors
v=(0,2H,2)
and
v^{\prime}=(0,2H,1)
in
H^{*}(S,\mathbb{Z})
. Consider the Beauville–Mukai systems over
B=|2H|=\mathbb{P}^{5}
:

\pi:X\longrightarrow M_{v}(B)\longrightarrow B,\qquad\tau:Y=M_{v^{\prime}}(S)\longrightarrow B,

where
X
is a OG10-type and
Y
is a
\text{K3}^{[5]}
-type hyper-Kähler manifold. The relative Jacobian of the universal family of curves
\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow B=|2H|
defines a smooth commutative group scheme
J=P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow B
with connected fibers acting on both
X
and
Y
over
B
.

The paper shows that both
\pi:X\longrightarrow B
and
\tau:Y\longrightarrow B
are
\delta
-regular abelian fibrations with respect to the
P^{\circ\circ}
-action. Moreover, it calculates all non-integral singular fibers of
\pi
and
\tau
, showing the structure of the
\delta
-loci

D_{1}=|H|^{(2)}\cup D_{1,\elliptic},\quad D_{2}=|H|\cup D_{2,\elliptic},\quad D_{3}=|H|\cup D_{3,\elliptic},\quad D_{4}=D_{4,\elliptic},\quad\cdots

where the closed immersions
|H|^{(2)}=|H|^{\times 2}/\mathfrak{S}_{2}\subset|2H|
and
|H|\subset|2H|
are defined naturally and the subscript
-_{\elliptic}
indicates the locus over which the fiber is geometrically integral.

In their discussions,
X_{0}
and
Y_{0}
are both
P^{\circ\circ}_{0}
-torsors over
B_{0}
. Therefore, from our
Theorem

6.1
, the birational translation automorphism spaces of
X
and
Y
are isomorphic:
P=P_{X}=P_{Y}
. By
Theorem

5.2
,
X_{1}^{\prime}
and
Y_{1}^{\prime}
are both
P_{1}
-torsors and indeed this agrees with their calculations. The inclusion
P^{\circ\circ}\subset P
is not an equality over
B_{1}
, because
P_{b}(=X^{\prime}_{b}=Y^{\prime}_{b})
has two connected components when
b\in|H|^{(2)}\cap B_{1}
. Hence
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
cannot be
P^{\circ\circ}
-torsors over
B_{1}
. Further analyzing their results together with our previous discussions will yield the following:

6.23
.

Notation as in the discussion above about
[
CRS21
]
. Restricting everything over
B\setminus|H|
, we have the following.

(1)

X^{\prime}
is a
P
-torsor and the group spaces in (
6.4
) for
\pi
are:

P^{\circ\circ}\subsetneq P^{a}=P^{\tau}=P.

(2)

Same statements hold for
Y^{\prime}
and
\tau
.

In particular, both
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
are Néron under
B_{0}\subset B\setminus|H|
, and
Y
is a Tate–Shafarevich twist of
X
by an element in
\Sha^{a}(B\setminus|H|)
(see
Section

6.4.4
). ∎

6.24
.

(1)

Over
|H|
, the fibers of
\tau
are nowhere reduced by computations in
[
CRS21
]
and
[
Hel21
, Theorem 1.1]
, so our results do not apply.

(2)

X
is of OG10-type and
Y
is of
\text{K3}^{[5]}
-type. Hence they are not deformation equivalent but yet
X
and
Y
are Tate–Shafarevich twist of each other in a precise sense (over
B\setminus|H|
).

Similar descriptions are given for a generalized Kummer
6
-fold and OG6-type hyper-Kähler manifold in
[
Wu24
]
. Same type of analyzation should work. Note also from
[
Kim25
, Theorem 5.1]
that the Néron model
P\longrightarrow B
contains global sections
K=(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\oplus 4}
and
\check{P}=P/K
, explaining the coefficients
16
in their result.

Appendix A
Backgrounds

The use of algebraic spaces in this article will be necessary due to the following reasons:

•

The theory of Néron models is well-behaved (e.g., smooth descent) when they are assumed to be algebraic spaces.

•

The torsor representing an element in
H^{1}
may fail to be a scheme.

•

The relative Picard space of a flat projective family may fail to be a scheme.

•

A Mukai flop of a symplectic variety may fail to be a scheme.

•

Some results in this article generalize to smooth, separated, and symplectic algebraic spaces
X
of finite type.

It is often immediate to upgrade a statement about schemes to that about algebraic spaces (e.g., when the statement is étale local on the source). In such cases, we will use the statement for algebraic spaces without explaining it explicitly.

In this article, every scheme is locally Noetherian and defined over a field of characteristic
0
. Every algebraic space is locally Noetherian and
locally separated
over a field of characteristic
0
, so that it is a
decent space
the sense of
[
Stacks
, Tag 06NK, 088J]
as well as quasi-separated
[
Stacks
, Tag 0BB6]
. Since
[
Stacks
]
will be cited quite often, we will specify only its Tag number when we cite their results, e.g., (Tag ABCD) means
[
Stacks
, Tag ABCD]
.

A.1.
Scheme theory facts

We collect various scheme theory facts in this section.

A.1.1.
Sections

The following well-known results about local sections are extensively used throughout the article. Recall that the notion of a reside field is well-defined for points on a (decent) algebraic space (Tag 0EMV).

A.0
[
BLR90
, Proposition 2.2.14]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a smooth morphism between algebraic spaces. Let
s\in S
be a point and
x\in X_{s}
a closed point on the fiber. Then there exists an étale morphism
\phi:U\longrightarrow S
and a point
s^{\prime}\in U
lying above
s
with
k(s^{\prime})=k(x)
, such that
f
has a
U
-section
h:U\longrightarrow X
with
h(s^{\prime})=x
:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

A.0
[
SGA $3˙I$
,
\mathrm{VI_{B}}
Lemma 5.6.1]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a locally finite type and universally open morphism between algebraic spaces. Let
s\in S
be a point and
x\in X_{s}
a closed point. Then there exists an étale morphism
U\longrightarrow S
and finite surjective morphism
S^{\prime}\longrightarrow U
, such that the composition

\phi:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow U\longrightarrow S

satisfies
\phi^{-1}(s)=\{s^{\prime}\}
,
k(s^{\prime})=k(x)
, and that
f
has an
S^{\prime}
-section
h:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow X
with
h(s^{\prime})=x
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

A.1
.

We may choose the étale morphism
\phi:U\longrightarrow S
in
Section

A.1.1
to be quasi-finite. We may similarly do so in
Section

A.1.1
, so that the morphism
\phi:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is quasi-finite. We may furthermore assume
S^{\prime}
is integral. In this case,
\phi:S^{\prime}\longrightarrow S
is equidimensional in the sense of §
A.1.5
.

A.1
[
BLR90
, Proposition 3.1.2]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a locally finite type morphism between regular algebraic spaces. Then a section
S\longrightarrow X
of
f
passes through a regular point of
X_{s}
for all
s\in S
.

A.2
.

Section

A.1.1
fails once
X
is singular. Consider a minimal proper elliptic surface
\pi:S\longrightarrow\Delta
over a Dedekind scheme with central fiber
S_{t_{0}}=C_{1}\cup C_{2}
of Kodaira type
\text{I}_{2}
. Assume there exists a section
s:\Delta\longrightarrow S
passing through
C_{1}
. Contracting a
(-2)
-curve
C_{1}
yields a proper flat morphism
\bar{\pi}:\bar{S}\longrightarrow\Delta
from a surface
\bar{S}
with an ordinary double point singularity. Then
s
induces a section of
\bar{\pi}
passing through a singular point of
\bar{S}_{t_{0}}
.

A.1.2.
Fitting subschemes

This subsection generally follows
[
Eis95
, §20.2]
.

A.3
Fitting subscheme.

Let
F
be a coherent sheaf on a locally Noetherian scheme
X
. Consider its finite rank locally free presentation

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qquad\mbox{with}\quad r=\rk E_{0}.

For each
i\geq 0
, the wedge power
\wedge^{r-i}f:\wedge^{r-i}E_{1}\longrightarrow\wedge^{r-i}E_{0}
induces a homomorphism

f^{r-i}:\wedge^{r-i}E_{1}\otimes\wedge^{r-i}E_{0}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}.

The
i
-th Fitting subscheme of
F
is a closed subscheme
\Fitt_{i}F\subset X
defined by the ideal sheaf
\im f^{r-i}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

We have a descending sequence of closed subschemes

X\supset\Fitt_{0}F\supset\Fitt_{1}F\supset\cdots,

which becomes an empty set in finite steps. Here are the properties of Fitting subschemes we use.

A.3
.

Let
F
be a coherent sheaf on
X
.

(1)

The scheme-theoretic support
\Supp F
and the
0
-th Fitting subscheme
\Fitt_{0}F
are set-theoretically identical and scheme-theoretically related by

\Fitt_{0}F\supset\Supp F.

(2)

F
is locally free of rank
i
on the locally closed subscheme
\Fitt_{i-1}F\setminus\Fitt_{i}F
.

(3)

If
f:Y\longrightarrow X
is a morphism between locally Noetherian schemes, then there exists a canonical isomorphism
\Fitt_{i}(f^{*}F)=f^{-1}(\Fitt_{i}(F))
.

A.3
[
K“”13
, Lemma 1.4]
.

Let
E_{1}\xlongrightarrow{f}E_{0}\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow 0
be a locally free presentation of a coherent sheaf
F
with
\rk E_{i}=r_{i}
. Consider the dual of
f
and its cokernel
E_{0}^{\vee}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{\vee}\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
. Then we have an equality

\Fitt_{i}F=\Fitt_{i+r_{1}-r_{0}}C.

A.4
[
Har77
, Proposition III.10.6]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a proper flat morphism of relative dimension
d
between smooth schemes over an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
. Then

\codim f(\Fitt_{d+i-1}\Omega_{f})\geq i\qquad\mbox{for all}\quad i\geq 0.

Proof.

To see our statement is a special case of Hartshorne’s, consider a locally free presentation
f^{*}\Omega_{S}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X}\longrightarrow\Omega_{f}\longrightarrow 0
. By definition,
\Fitt_{d+i-1}\Omega_{f}
consists of points
x\in X
over which the rank of
f^{*}\Omega_{S}\longrightarrow\Omega_{X}
is
\leq\dim S-i
. This reduces our statement to Hartshorne’s.
∎

A.1.3.
Scheme-theoretically dense open subspaces

This subsection summarizes (Tag 0831). Because we are assuming algebraic spaces are locally Noetherian, we can adopt the simpler definition in (Tag 0835).

A.5
.

Let
S
be a locally Noetherian algebraic space. Its open subspace
S_{0}\subset S
is called
(scheme-theoretically) dense
if the scheme-theoretic image of the open immersion
S_{0}\longrightarrow S
is
S
.

If
S
is
\mathrm{(S_{1})}
, i.e., it has no embedded points, then an open subspace
S_{0}\subset S
is scheme-theoretically dense if and only if it is topologically dense in Zariski topology (Tag 083P).

A.6
.

Let
S_{0}\subset S
be a dense open subspace. Then for a flat morphism
X\longrightarrow S
form a locally Noetherian algebraic space
X
, the open subspace
X_{0}=X\times_{S}S_{0}\subset X
is dense.

Proof.

The scheme-theoretic image of a qcqs morphism is compatible with arbitrary flat base change
[
BLR90
, Proposition 2.5.2]
. The open immersion
S_{0}\subset S
is qcqs because
S
is locally Noetherian.
∎

A.7
Tag 084N.

Let
f,g:X\longrightarrow Y
be morphisms of algebraic spaces over
S
and
U\subset X
a dense open subspace. Assume
Y
is separated over
S
. Then
f_{|U}=g_{|U}
implies
f=g
.

A.1.4.
Traits

This subsection follows
[
Hol19
, §5.1]
. A
Dedekind scheme
is a regular (connected) Noetherian scheme of dimension
1
. A local Dedekind scheme is the Spec of a discrete valuation ring (dvr).

A.8
.

Let
S
be an algebraic space.

(1)

A
trait
of
S
is a nonconstant morphism
\Delta\longrightarrow S
from a local Dedekind scheme
\Delta
. Given a point
s\in S
, a
trait passing through
s
is a trait sending the closed point
0\in\Delta
to
s
.

(2)

A trait is called
flat
if it is a flat morphism. A trait is called
unramified
(resp.
quasi-finite
) if the induced morphism
\Delta\longrightarrow\Spec\mathcal{O}^{h}_{S,s}
is
unramified
(resp.
quasi-finite
).

(3)

Say we have a preferred choice of a dense open subspace
S_{0}\subset S
. A trait is called
non-degenerate
if it sends the generic point
\xi\in\Delta
to a point in
S_{0}
.

A trait can be an arbitrary nonconstant morphism
\Delta\longrightarrow S
; for example, it can be ramified or the field extension
k(0)/k(s)
may even be transcendental. An unramified trait is quasi-finite (Tag 02V5).

A.8
[
BLR90
, Proposition 2.4.1]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be an algebraic space locally of finite type over a reduced scheme
S
. Let
x\in X
and
s=f(x)\in S
be points. Then
f
is flat at
x
if and only if for every trait
\Delta\longrightarrow S
passing through
s
, the base change
X_{\Delta}\longrightarrow\Delta
is flat at every point
x^{\prime}\in X_{\Delta}
lying over
x
.

A.8
[
Har77
, Proposition III.9.7]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow\Delta
be an algebraic space locally of finite type over a Dedekind scheme
\Delta
. Then
f
is flat if and only if
f
sends every associated point of
X
to a generic point of
\Delta
.

A.1.5.
Universally equidimensional morphisms

This section summarizes
[
SV00
, §2.1]
and some parts in
[
EGA $IV˙3$
, §13–15]
.

A.9
.

Let
S
be a locally Noetherian scheme. A locally finite type algebraic space
f:X\longrightarrow S
is called
(locally) equidimensional
if:

(1)

The fiber dimension function

(A.10)

X\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z},\qquad x\longmapsto\dim_{x}X_{f(x)}

is (locally) constant.

(2)

Every generic point of
X
is sent to a generic point of
S
.

The morphism
f
is called
universally (locally) equidimensional
if
X_{T}\longrightarrow T
is (locally) equidimensional for arbitrary base change
T\longrightarrow S
of schemes.

A.11
.

The original reference
[
SV00
, §2.1]
assumes everything is separated. However, (local) equidimensionality is an étale local property on both the source and target, so we can reduce to the case
\Spec A\longrightarrow\Spec B
.

The fiber dimension function (
A.10
) is upper-semicontinuous by Chevalley’s theorem
[
EGA $IV˙3$
, Theorem 13.1.3]
, so it is locally constant if and only if it is locally constant on closed points of
X
. The following two results show the relation between universally open and universally equidimensional morphisms.

A.11
[
SV00
, Proposition 2.1.7]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a locally finite type algebraic space.

(1)

If (
A.10
) is (locally) constant and
f
is open, then
f
is (locally) equidimensional.

(2)

f
is universally (locally) equidimensional if and only if (
A.10
) is (locally) constant and
f
is universally open.

A.11
[
SV00
, Proposition 2.1.7]
.

If
S
is geometrically unibranch, then
f:X\longrightarrow S
is equidimensional if and only if it is universally equidimensional.

A.12
.

•

The morphism
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}\sqcup\Spec k\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
is not equidimensional.

•

Define
S=(xy=0)\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{(x,y)}
and
X=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{x}\sqcup(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{y}\setminus\{y=0\})
. Then
X\longrightarrow S
is equidimensional but not universally equidimensional, either because its restriction to
(x=0)\subset S
is not equidimensional or it is not open.

The following relates universal equidimensionality to flatness.

A.13
[
EGA $IV˙3$
, Corollary 15.2.3]
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow S
be a locally finite type algebraic space. Let
x\in X
and
s=f(x)\in S
. Assume

(1)

f
is universally open at generic points of the irreducible components of
X_{s}
containing
x
.

(2)

X_{s}
is (geometrically) reduced at
x
.

(3)

S
is reduced at
s
.

Then
f
is flat at
x
.

A.14
Flatness and universal equidimensionality.

•

A flat morphism is universally locally equidimensional.

•

If
X
is non-reduced, then
X_{\red}\hookrightarrow X
is equidimensional but not flat.

•

Let
G
be a finite group acting on
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k}
. The quotient
\mathbb{A}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}/G
is universally equidimensional, but it is flat if and only if
\mathbb{A}^{n}/G
is regular.

•

Let
f:X\longrightarrow\Delta
be an algebraic space locally of finite type over a Dedekind scheme. Then
f
is flat if and only if every associated scheme-theoretic component of
X
dominates
\Delta
. It is universally equidimensional if and only if every set-theoretic irreducible component of
X
dominates
\Delta
.

A.1.6.
Linear schemes

This section roughly follows
[
Nit05
, §5.3.3]
.

A.15
.

Let
S
be a (locally Noetherian) algebraic space. A
linear space
is an affine morphism of finite type

\mathbb{V}_{F}=\Spec_{S}(\Sym^{*}F)\longrightarrow S

for a coherent sheaf
F
on
S
. When
S
is a scheme, we call it a
linear scheme
.

An fppf sheaf on an algebraic space
S
is a functor
F:(\mathsf{Sp}/S)^{\opp}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Set}
satisfying the sheaf axioms for the big fppf topology on
\mathsf{Sp}/S
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{S}^{\fppf}
be the fppf structure sheaf on
S
(represented by
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
). An
fppf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module
is an fppf abelian sheaf
F
on
S
equipped with a bilinear map
\mathcal{O}_{S}^{\fppf}\times F\longrightarrow F
satisfying the axioms of modules.

A coherent sheaf
F
on an algebraic space can define an fppf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-module in (at least) two different ways. The first way is to consider its
associated fppf sheaf
defined by

(A.16)

F^{\fppf}:(\mathsf{Sp}/S)^{\opp}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Ab},\qquad\big[f:T\longrightarrow S\big]\longmapsto H^{0}(T,f^{*}F).

The second way is to consider its
fppf dual
defined by

(A.17)

\Hom^{\fppf}(F,\mathcal{O}_{S}):(\mathsf{Sp}/S)^{\opp}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Ab},\qquad\big[f:T\longrightarrow S\big]\longmapsto\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}(f^{*}F,\mathcal{O}_{T}).

Both are fppf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules.

A.18
.

Let
F
be a coherent sheaf on an algebraic space
S
. Then the linear space
\mathbb{V}_{F}\longrightarrow S
represents the fppf dual (
A.17
) of
F
.

Proof.

For every affine morphism
f:T=\Spec_{S}\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow S
, we have
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{T}=\mathcal{A}
and thus

\Hom_{S}(T,\mathbb{V}_{F})=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{S}}(F,\mathcal{A})=\Hom_{\mathcal{O}_{T}}(f^{*}F,\mathcal{O}_{T}).\qed

Therefore, a linear space
V\longrightarrow S
has a zero section
S\longrightarrow V
, addition
V\times_{S}V\longrightarrow V
, and scalar multiplication
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times_{S}V\longrightarrow V
satisfying the usual axioms of modules. In particular, it is a separated and commutative group algebraic space. Given a linear space
f:V\longrightarrow S
, its associated coherent sheaf
F
can be obtained in two different ways:

•

F
is the conormal sheaf of the zero section
S\longrightarrow V
.

•

Consider the
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-grading on
f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{V}
from the
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
-scalar multiplication structure. Then
F=(f_{*}\mathcal{O}_{V})_{1}
is its degree
1
part.

A.18
.

Let
V
and
W
be fppf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules and
f:V\longrightarrow W
a homomorphism of fppf
\mathcal{O}_{S}
-modules. Assume
V
and
W
are representable by linear spaces associated to coherent sheaves
F
and
G
. Then
\ker f
is representable by a linear space associated to the coherent sheaf
\coker(G\longrightarrow F)
.

Proof.

By Yoneda,
f
is a morphism of linear spaces
f:\mathbb{V}_{F}\longrightarrow\mathbb{V}_{G}
that induces a homomorphism of coherent sheaves
G\longrightarrow F
. Take its cokernel
G\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow C\longrightarrow 0
and apply the fppf dual functor to it.
∎

An interesting example of a linear space is a
tangent space
. A morphism between algebraic spaces
f:X\longrightarrow S
induces a cotangent sheaf
\Omega_{f}
on
X
. Its linear space

(A.19)

\mathbb{T}_{f}=\mathbb{V}_{\Omega_{f}}\longrightarrow X

is called a
tangent space
of
f
; for every geometric point
\bar{x}
of
X
over
\bar{s}
, the fiber of the tangent space is
\mathbb{T}_{f,\bar{x}}=(\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{x}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{x}}^{2})^{\vee}
, the Zariski tangent space of the geometric fiber
X_{\bar{s}}
at
\bar{x}

[
Har77
, Proposition II.8.7]
. It represents the fppf
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module
T_{f}^{\fppf}=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}^{\fppf}(\Omega_{f},\mathcal{O}_{X})
.

A.1.7.
Others

The following is a nice application of Zariski’s main theorem.

A.19
.

Let
f:X\longrightarrow Y
be a dominant morphism between integral and separated algebraic spaces of finite type over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
0
. Assume
Y
is normal and
f:X(k)\longrightarrow Y(k)
is injective. Then
f
is an open immersion.

Proof.

We follow Jason Starr’s answer in
[
Sta17
]
. The morphism
f
is quasi-finite because
f:X(k)\longrightarrow Y(k)
is quasi-finite
[
GW20
, Remark 12.16]
. By generic smoothness,
X
contains an open dense subscheme
U
where
f_{|U}
is smooth, whence étale by its quasi-finiteness. Now using the injectivity of
f
on closed points,
f_{|U}
is an open immersion so
f
is birational.

Since
f
is quasi-finite separated and
Y
is qcqs, Zariski’s main theorem (Tag 082K) says that there exists a factorization
X\xrightarrow{j}Z\xrightarrow{g}Y
where
j
is an open immersion and
g
is finite. The morphism
g
is birational as well. Now a finite extension
\mathcal{O}_{Y}\hookrightarrow g_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
has to be an equality, as
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
is integrally closed and
g_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Z}
is contained in the function field
K(Y)
. This proves
g
is an isomorphism.
∎

A.2.
Group scheme actions and
\delta
-regularity

Let us recall the definition of
\delta
-regular group schemes and their actions on weak abelian fibrations.

A.2.1.
Group scheme actions

We quickly clarify our terminology for a group scheme action on an algebraic space. Our reference includes
[
MFK94
, §0]
and
[
Ray70
, §VI]
. Throughout, we assume
G\longrightarrow S
is a group scheme (or a group algebraic space as in §
2
) and
f:X\longrightarrow S
is an algebraic space. A
G
-action
on
X
is a morphism of algebraic spaces
G\times_{S}X\longrightarrow X
satisfying the axiom of group actions. Given a
G
-action on
X
, its
universal translation
morphism is

(A.20)

\phi:G\times_{S}X\longrightarrow X\times_{S}X,\qquad(g,x)\longmapsto(g.x,x).

A.21
.

Consider a
G
-action on an algebraic space
f:X\longrightarrow S
.

(1)

(
[
MFK94
, Definition 0.8]
) The action is
free
if the universal translation morphism
\phi
in (
A.20
) is an immersion.

(2)

(
[
Ray70
, Definition VI.1.1]
) The action is
transitive
(or
X
is a
G
-homogeneous space
) if
\phi
is faithfully flat.

(3)

X
is a
G
-torsor
if the action is free and transitive, i.e.,
\phi
is an isomorphism.

(4)

The action is
faithful
if for every point
s\in S
, the
G_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
is faithful.

A.22
.

Let
T\longrightarrow S
be a morphism. Given a
T
-section
x:T\longrightarrow X
, we define its
orbit map

\phi_{x}:G_{T}\longrightarrow X_{T}
by the pullback

It is a locally closed subgroup scheme of
G\times_{S}X=f^{*}G\longrightarrow X
.

A.23
.

The map
d\phi
is described as follows: take the fppf sheaf homomorphism
G\longrightarrow\underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{f}
, its Lie algebra
\Lie G\longrightarrow\Lie\underline{\mathrm{Aut}}_{f}=f_{*}\mathbb{T}_{f}
, and its adjoint
f^{*}(\Lie G)\longrightarrow\mathbb{T}_{f}
.

Proof.

C_{e}
be the conormal sheaf of the identity section of
G
. Denote by
j:X\longrightarrow\St
the identity section, and
p:G\times_{S}X\longrightarrow X
and
q:X\times_{S}X\longrightarrow X
the second projections. Consider the commutative diagram

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Take the cotangent sequence
\phi^{*}\Omega_{q}\longrightarrow\Omega_{p}\longrightarrow\Omega_{\phi}\longrightarrow 0
and pull it back by
i^{*}
to yield:

\Omega_{f}\longrightarrow f^{*}C_{e}\longrightarrow C_{j}\longrightarrow 0.

Here the last term is from isomorphisms
i^{*}\Omega_{\phi}=j^{*}\Omega_{\phi^{\prime}}=C_{j}
. Its fppf dual is the desired left exact sequence.

Let us now check the map defined in the first paragraph and the map
d\phi
in the statement coincide. Set
\Spec k[\epsilon]=\Spec k[t]/t^{2}
and realize the tangent schemes as Hom-schemes

\mathbb{T}_{p}=\Hom_{X}(X\times_{k}k[\epsilon],G\times_{S}X),\qquad\mathbb{T}_{q}=\Hom_{X}(X\times_{k}k[\epsilon],X\times_{S}X).

For each test scheme
x:T\longrightarrow X
, the map
i^{*}\mathbb{T}_{p}\longrightarrow i^{*}\phi^{*}\mathbb{T}_{q}
in the first paragraph over
T
is

\Hom_{T}(T\times_{k}k[\epsilon],G_{T})\longrightarrow\Hom_{T}(T\times_{k}k[\epsilon],X_{T})

given by the composition with the orbit map
\phi_{x}:G_{T}\longrightarrow X_{T}
. Same description holds for the map
d\phi
given in the statement.
∎

A.23
.

Let
G\longrightarrow S
(resp.
f:X\longrightarrow S
) be a smooth, separated, and finite type group space (resp. algebraic space with a
G
-action) over a normal scheme
S
. Assume that
X
is a
G
-torsor over a dense open subscheme
S_{0}\subset S
and
\Lie\St\longrightarrow X
is trivial. Then the
G
-action on
X
is free over
S
.

Proof.

Let us first show that the universal translation morphism
\phi:G\times_{S}X\longrightarrow X\times_{S}X
in (
A.20
) is quasi-finite. The morphism is defined over
X
by second projections, so we may show the orbit map
\phi_{x}:G_{x}\longrightarrow X_{x}
is quasi-finite for every
x\in X
. Note that the stabilizer scheme
\St\longrightarrow X
is quasi-finite because its relative dimension is
0
(
\Lie\St
is trivial) and it is of finite type. Hence for every
x\in X
, the stabilizer
\St_{x}
at
x
is a finite algebraic group. For each point
y\in X_{x}
, its preimage under
\phi_{x}:G_{x}\longrightarrow X_{x}
is either empty or isomorphic to
\St_{x}\times_{k(x)}k(y)
. Hence it is a finite set so
\phi_{x}
is quasi-finite.

We now claim
\phi
is an open immersion. Note that
\phi
is quasi-finite, separated, and birational (isomorphic over
S_{0}
), and that
X\times_{S}X
is normal. By Zariski’s main theorem as in
Section

A.1.7
,
\phi
is an open immersion.
∎

A.2.2.
Ngô’s
\delta
-regularity and weak abelian fibration

Our reference for this section is Ngô’s original article
[
Ng“ˆo10
, §7.1]

[
Ng“ˆo11
, §2]
together with
[
AF16
, §5.4]
,
[
CRS21
, §2.2]
, and
[
Anc+23
, §6]
. In this subsection, we assume
S
is a Noetherian scheme of characteristic
0
.

Given a connected algebraic group
G
over a field
k
of characteristic
0
, Chevalley’s structure theorem (e.g.,
[
BLR90
, Theorem 9.2.1]
) claims there is a canonical short exact sequence

1\longrightarrow G_{\operatorname{aff}}\longrightarrow G\longrightarrow G_{\operatorname{ab}}\longrightarrow 1,

where
G_{\operatorname{aff}}
is a connected linear algebraic group and
G_{\operatorname{ab}}
is an abelian variety, both defined over
k
. Now let
P\longrightarrow S
be a smooth commutative group scheme. For each point
s\in S
, the fiber
P_{s}
is a locally algebraic group over
k(s)
, so its (strict) neutral component
P^{\circ\circ}_{s}
admits a Chevalley short exact sequence as above. We call

(A.24)

\delta:S\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z},\qquad s\longmapsto\dim(P^{\circ\circ}_{s})_{\operatorname{aff}}

the
\delta
-function
. Notice that the
\delta
-function of
P
is identical to that of its strict neutral component
P^{\circ\circ}
(§
2.2.1
). Since
P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow S
is of finite type (
Theorem

2.11
), the
\delta
-function is an upper-semicontinuous function by
[
Ng“ˆo10
, Lemma 5.6.3]
.

A.25
\delta
-regular group scheme.

Let
S
be a Noetherian scheme. A smooth commutative group scheme
P\longrightarrow S
is
\delta
-regular
if for each
i\geq 0
, the closed subset

(A.26)

D_{i}=\{s\in S:\delta(s)\geq i\}\ \subset\ S

has codimension
\geq i
(i.e., every irreducible component of it has codimension
\geq i
).

Note that if
P\longrightarrow S
is
\delta
-regular then
D_{1}\subset S
is a strict inclusion, so its neutral component
P^{\circ}\longrightarrow S
(§
2.2.2
) is an abelian scheme over
S_{0}=S\setminus D_{1}
.

A.27
Weak abelian fibration.

Let
S
be a Noetherian scheme. A
weak abelian fibration
over
S
is a pair
(X,P)
such that

(i)

f:X\longrightarrow S
is a proper equidimensional morphism of relative dimension
d
.

(ii)

P\longrightarrow S
is a smooth, commutative, and quasi-projective group scheme acting on
X
over
S
. It has the same relative dimension
d
.

(iii)

For every point
s\in S
, the kernel of the
P_{s}
-action on
X_{s}
is affine.

We simply call
X\longrightarrow S
a weak abelian fibration if the
P
-action on
X
is clear in context.

A.28
.

Definition

A.27
is more restrictive than Ngô’s original definition
[
Ng“ˆo10
, §7.1.1–4]
in two respects. (i) We required
f
to be equidimensional. (ii) We assumed
P\longrightarrow S
is quasi-projective instead of assuming its Tate module is
polarizable
as in
[
Ng“ˆo10
, §7.1.4]
.

A.29
.

Let
(X,P)
be a weak abelian fibration over a Noetherian scheme
S
as in
Definition

A.27
. Then

(1)

The Tate module of
P
is polarizable.

(2)

Every point
x\in X
has an affine stabilizer
\St_{x}
.

In particular,
(X,P)
is a weak abelian fibration in the sense of
[
Ng“ˆo10
, §7.1.1]
.

Proof.

(1) This is Theorem 1.2 and 6.2 in
[
AF24
]
: every relative ample line bundle on
\nu:P^{\circ\circ}\longrightarrow S
induces a polarization of the Tate module
R^{2d-1}\nu_{!}\mathbb{Q}_{l}
. (2) This is
[
AF16
, Lemma 5.16]
: if
s\in S
is a point, then a single point
x\in X_{s}
has an affine stabilizer
\Longleftrightarrow
every point
x\in X_{s}
has an affine stabilizer
\Longleftrightarrow

\ker(P_{s}\longrightarrow\Aut_{X_{s}/k(s)})
is affine.
∎

The following is the original idea of Ngô that the
\delta
-regularity of a group scheme can be checked from its action on a weak abelian fibration
X\longrightarrow S
.

A.29
.

Let
(X,P)
be a weak abelian fibration over a Noetherian scheme
S
and
\St\longrightarrow X
the stabilizer scheme of the action. Then

(1)

The
\delta
-function in (
A.24
) can be computed by

\delta(s)=\max\{\dim\St_{x}:x\in X_{s}\}\qquad\mbox{for all}\quad s\in S.

(2)

The
\delta
-loci in (
A.26
) can be computed by

D_{i}=\pi\big(\Fitt_{i-1}C_{e}\big),

where
C_{e}
is the conormal sheaf of the identity section
e:X\longrightarrow\St
.

Proof.

(1) This is tacitly mentioned in the discussion of algebraic integrable systems in
[
Ng“ˆo11
, §2]
and proved in
[
AF16
, Lemma 5.17]
: it is a consequence of Borel’s fixed point theorem. (2) From the previous item,
D_{i}
is the image of the closed set

\{x\in X:\dim\St_{x}\geq i\}\ \subset\ X.

The fiber dimension of
\St
is the fiber dimension of the coherent sheaf
C_{e}
. Hence this closed subset is the
(i-1)
-th Fitting subscheme of
C_{e}
(see §
A.1.2
).
∎

A.30
\delta
-regular abelian fibration.

A
\delta
-regular abelian fibration
is a weak abelian fibration
(X,P)
such that
P
is
\delta
-regular.
